A comparison of categories of Nori motivic sheaves
Emil Jacobsen, Luca Terenzi

Abstract
We show that two distinct candidate theories of Nori motivic sheaves, introduced by Ivorra–Morel and by Ayoub, respectively, are canonically equivalent. The proof, which exploits the six functor formalism systematically, is based on the Tannakian theory of motivic local systems. As a consequence, we obtain a system of realization functors of Voevodsky motivic sheaves into Nori motivic sheaves compatible with the six operations, previously constructed by Tubach using different methods.



Introduction

Motivation and goal of the paper

Let
k
be a field of characteristic
0
endowed with a complex embedding
\sigma\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
In the 1990s, Nori constructed an unconditional candidate
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
for the conjectural
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category of mixed motives over
k
:
by design, it is the finest possible abelian category of coefficients computing singular cohomology groups of
k
-varieties.
As shown by Nori, the abelian category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
carries a canonical tensor product making it neutral Tannakian over
\mathbb{Q}
;
its Tannaka dual is Nori’s motivic Galois group.

The conjectural theory of mixed motives over
k
should fit into a theory of mixed motivic sheaves over
k
-varieties giving rise to a universal six functor formalism.
Recently, two distinct approaches to promote Nori’s theory of motives to a theory of mixed motivic sheaves have been developed.
The first construction, initiated by Ivorra–Morel in
[
IM24
]
and completed by the second-named author in
[
Ter24b
]
, generalizes the universal property of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
as an abelian category.
The second construction, described by Ayoub in
[
Ayo21
]
, generalizes the presentation of Nori motives as representations of the motivic Galois group.
Both constructions build on the pre-existing theory of Voevodsky motivic sheaves over
k
-varieties:
in fact, they could be both regarded as a way to force the conjectural motivic perverse
t
-structure on Voevodsky’s categories to exist.

Since the two approaches differ both at a conceptual and at a technical level, at first sight it is not clear at all whether they should define equivalent theories of Nori motivic sheaves.
The main goal of the present paper is to prove that this is indeed the case.
The proof, which sheds new light on both constructions, is based on the Tannakian study of motivic local systems due to the first-named author in
[
Jac25
]
.

This comparison result allows us to combine the advantages of both approaches to Nori motivic sheaves.
An important application is the construction of realization functors of Voevodsky motivic sheaves into Ivorra–Morel’s categories:
this result was originally obtained by Tubach in
[
Tub25
]
;
using the features of Ayoub’s construction, we offer an alternative proof which highlights the role of the motivic Galois group.

History and previous work

Before stating our results in a more precise form, we need to review the two proposed constructions of Nori motivic sheaves.
As mentioned above, both of them build on the theory of Voevodsky motivic sheaves and are motivated by conjectural properties of the latter.
In order to clarify this point, it is convenient to start by recalling Voevodsky’s and Nori’s approaches to mixed motives in parallel, stressing their interrelation.

For the purposes of the present paper, we are only interested in
\mathbb{Q}
-linear categories of motives.
The conjectural theory of
Mixed Motives
over
k
is expected to be characterized by two distinctive properties of different nature:

(i)

It should arise from the geometry of algebraic cycles on
k
-varieties.

(ii)

It should take the shape of an abelian category of coefficients defining the universal cohomology theory on
k
-varieties.

More precisely, categorical invariants such as morphisms and extensions between mixed motives should be computable in terms of
K
-theory groups.
The abelian category of mixed motives is expected to carry a monoidal structure making it neutral Tannakian over
\mathbb{Q}
, and also a theory of weights refining the classical Hodge-theoretic notion.
From a conceptual viewpoint, it is reasonable to require the two properties (i) and (ii) together:
the only known procedure to construct universal cohomology classes uniformly within different cohomology theories is by taking the image of algebraic cycles under the various cycle class maps.
From a practical viewpoint, however, it is not clear how to impose (i) and (ii) simultaneously.
In fact, the expectation that algebraic cycles should give rise to an abelian category of coefficients led Grothendieck to formulate the so-called Standard Conjectures, which remain widely open to date.

Anyway, there exist two unconditional approaches to construct categories of mixed motives over
k
.
Roughly speaking, each of them is only known to satisfy one of the two key properties stated above:

(i)

Voevodsky’s approach (pursued independently also by Hanamura and by Levine) yields a triangulated category
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)
having the correct relation to algebraic cycles, but not known to arise from an abelian category.

(ii)

Nori’s approach yields an abelian category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
enjoying a suitable universal property, but not known to be computable in terms of algebraic cycles.

In lack of a complete theory of mixed motives, the two candidate theories represent its closest approximations from the geometric side of algebraic cycles and from the linear-algebraic side of cohomology, respectively.
It is worth stressing that Voevodsky’s and Nori’s constructions look quite different:
while the first is purely cycle-theoretic and does not depend on the existence of any classical cohomology theory, the second requires the choice of one classical cohomology theory as a model for the category of motives.
However, they are believed to be essentially equivalent:
Nori’s category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
should be the correct abelian category of mixed motives, while Voevodsky’s category
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)
should be its bounded derived category.
In particular, the triangulated category
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)
should carry a
motivic
t
-structure
with heart equivalent to
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
.

The existence of the motivic
t
-structure is an extremely deep conjecture:
it would imply a large part of the Standard Conjectures, as well as the Beilinson–Soulé Vanishing Conjecture in Algebraic
K
-theory.
In any case, by work of Nori, there exists a canonical realization functor

\Nri_{k}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k))

(1)

compatible with the monoidal structures.
This leads to a strong comparison result on the Tannakian level:
as proved by Choudhury–Gallauer in
[
CG17
]
, Nori’s motivic Galois group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
is canonically isomorphic to Ayoub’s motivic Galois group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
, constructed directly out of Voevodsky motives.
So far, this result is the stronger piece of evidence towards the conjectural equivalence.

Classical cohomology theories arise from suitable theories of sheaves over
k
-varieties.
Typically, these give rise to a system of triangulated categories of coefficients related by Grothendieck’s six functor formalism.
By analogy, the conjectural theory of Mixed Motives should extend to a theory of
Mixed Motivic Sheaves
defining the universal six functor formalism on
k
-varieties.
The precise formulation of this expectation can be found in Beilinson’s article
[
Bei87
]
, where the abelian categories of mixed motivic sheaves are modelled on perverse sheaves rather than on ordinary sheaves.
The insight that perverse sheaves should provide the natural model for refined abelian categories of coefficients, justified by the formalism of cohomological weights, lies at the heart of Saito’s theory of mixed Hodge modules.
A crucial foundational result is Beilinson’s equivalence
[
Bei87a
, Thm. 1.3]
, which allows one to regard the usual constructible derived category as the derived category of perverse sheaves.

From a technical viewpoint, the construction of the six operations in the motivic world turns out to be easiest in the setting of Voevodsky motives.
Based on seminal ideas of Voevodsky, this subject has been studied thoroughly by Ayoub in
[
Ayo07
]
and by Cisinski–Déglise in
[
CD19
]
:
the outcome is a system of triangulated categories
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
related by the six operations.
As shown by Drew–Gallauer in
[
DG22
]
, this really defines the universal six functor formalism on
k
-varieties, provided such a property is stated in the language of
\infty
-categories.
By analogy with the case of mixed motives, it is natural to conjecture the existence of a
motivic perverse
t
-structure
on
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
.
In order to construct a candidate for the conjectural perverse heart unconditionally, one can try to extend Nori’s approach to mixed motives over general bases.
In fact, this can be done in two different ways, according to the feature of Nori’s category that one wants to generalize:
either its universal property as an abelian category or its presentation as representations of the motivic Galois group.
In both cases, the construction requires the choice of a complex embedding
\sigma\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, as for Nori’s original theory.
Using the associated analytification of
k
-varieties, one defines a system of Betti realization functors

\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\to\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)

into the usual constructible derived categories.
By stabilizing the image of
\Bti_{X}^{*}
inside
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
appropriately, one obtains a well-behaved triangulated category
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
of
constructible complexes of geometric origin
.
As in
[
BBD+18
]
, one can define a perverse
t
-structure on the latter:
its heart
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
is the category of
perverse sheaves of geometric origin
, and the abelian category of motivic perverse sheaves should be a motivic enhancement of the latter.

Ivorra–Morel’s approach to Nori motivic sheaves puts the accent on the universal property:
their category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
is defined as the universal abelian category factoring the homological functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\Bti_{X}^{*}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)\xrightarrow{{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X).

The existence of Nori’s realization functor (
1
) implies that this definition recovers
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
when
X=\Spec(k)
.
By
[
IM24
]
and
[
Ter24b
]
, as
X
varies among quasi-projective
k
-varieties, the derived categories
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
are endowed with the six operations, compatibly with those on the underlying constructible derived categories.
The construction of this six functor formalism is long and technical, since one has to reconstruct the six operations starting from
t
-exact functors and natural transformations thereof.
Ayoub’s approach to Nori motivic sheaves puts the accent on the motivic Galois group:
the group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
acts naturally on the triangulated categories
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
, and one is led to consider the associated categories of homotopy-fixed points

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}.

Choudhury–Gallauer’s isomorphism between
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
implies that this definition recovers
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k))
when
X=\Spec(k)
.
Over a general
k
-variety
X
, one can interpret
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
informally as a category of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
-valued constructible complexes.
Since the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
-action is compatible with the six operations, these pass automatically to the homotopy-fixed points, and they even exist
\infty
-categorically.
In conclusion, there are two natural ways to extend Nori’s theory of motives to a six functor formalism, and they enjoy distinct advantages:
the universal property is only visible in Ivorra–Morel’s setting, whereas the functoriality is much cleaner in Ayoub’s setting.

Main results

Our main result is the following comparison theorem:

Theorem

(Thm.
5.4
, Prop.
5.5
)
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, there exists a canonical equivalence of triangulated categories

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}.

As
X
varies, these are compatible with the six operations.

Let us give an overview of the proof strategy.
This is an intentionally simplified account:
it does not touch certain technical questions about perverse sheaves of geometric origin that we need to solve along the way.

If the above comparison result is to hold, there should be a way to carve out the abelian category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
inside the triangulated category
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
as the heart of a suitable perverse
t
-structure, and the corresponding realization functor should identify
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
with the derived category of its perverse heart.
We check that this is indeed the case:

Proposition

(Prop.
4.11
, Thm.
4.14
)
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, the stable
\infty
-category
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
carries a
t
-structure compatible with the perverse
t
-structure on
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
.
Its heart is equivalent to
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
, and the associated realization functor

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)})\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}

is an equivalence.

The existence of the perverse
t
-structure on
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
essentially follows from the
t
-exactness of the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
-action on
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
.
The final equivalence is a
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
-equivariant version of Beilinson’s classical result, and the proof is based on Beilinson’s argument
[
Bei87a
, § 2]
.

Once this preliminary task is completed, we construct a canonical system of exact functors

o_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}

by exploiting the universal property of Ivorra–Morel’s abelian categories.
Passing to the derived categories, we obtain the triangulated comparison functors

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\xrightarrow{o_{X}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}.

Their compatibility with the six operations is a formal consequence of the way these operations are constructed in Ivorra–Morel’s setting.
In particular, one does not need to know in advance that the comparison functors are equivalences in order to know their compatibility with the six operations;
in fact, it is the latter result which plays a key role in the proof of the former.

Arguing by Noetherian induction via localization triangles, the comparison statement for Nori motivic sheaves reduces its generic variant:

Proposition

(Prop.
5.10
)
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, the functor

\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(U))\to\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(U)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}

induced in the colimit over the dense open subsets
U\subset X
is an equivalence.

The main advantage of this generic statement is that it is amenable to Tannakian methods.
Indeed, if
X
is a smooth
k
-variety, ordinary local systems over
X
embed into perverse sheaves after shifting appropriately inside
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
;
we have a similar inclusion
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
inside
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
.
We are led to consider the full abelian subcategory
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
spanned by the object whose underlying perverse sheaf belongs to
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
.
Our comparison functor restricts to an exact monoidal functor

o_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}.

Since every perverse sheaf over
X
restricts to a shifted local system over some dense open subset, it suffices to show that these functors induce an equivalence in the colimit.
If
X
is geometrically connected over
k
, the two categories involved are neutral Tannakian over
\mathbb{Q}
, and the question can be translated in terms of their Tannaka dual groups.
In the case when
X=\Spec(k)
, everything comes down to Choudhury–Gallauer’s isomorphism between
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
.
In general, one needs to measure the difference between the motivic Galois group of
X
and that of
k
.
We do this as follows:

Theorem

(Thm.
2.24
, Thm.
3.18
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth, geometrically connected
k
-variety.
Then, for every closed point
x\in X(\bar{k})
, the following statements hold:

1.

The Tannaka dual
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)
of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
fits into the fundamental exact sequence

1\to\pi_{1}^{\textup{geo}}(X,x)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)\to 1.

2.

The Tannaka dual
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(X,x)
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
fits into the fundamental exact sequence

1\to\pi_{1}^{\textup{geo}}(X,x)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(X,x)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)\to 1.

Here, the group
\pi_{1}^{\textup{geo}}(X,x)
is Tannaka dual to
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
:
it should be regarded as the closest motivic approximation to the usual topological fundamental group.
The proof of exactness of the fundamental sequences consists of two steps:
first we treat the case when
x
is a
k
-rational point (in which case the two sequences are even split), then we deduce the general case by a Galois-descent argument.
In the
k
-rational case, the two exact sequences are established in different ways:
the first one was obtained by the first-named author in
[
Jac25
]
using Hodge-theoretic inputs, while the second one is obtained in the present paper using abstract properties of equivariant Tannakian categories.

At this point, the reader might observe that the exactness of the two fundamental sequences would already imply that the comparison functor
o_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
is an equivalence, at least when
X
is geometrically connected over
k
.
The issue is that, in the main body of the paper, we have to consider two distinct notions of local system of geometric origin:
while Ayoub’s category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
is tailored to the second fundamental sequence, the natural category of local systems to use for the first one is a priori smaller.
In any case, we can show that the possible difference between these two categories of local systems disappears in the colimit, which allows us to prove the generic equivalence.
Once the full comparison theorem is known, we deduce that the two notions of local system of geometric origin must in fact coincide.
We obtain a similar result for perverse sheaves of geometric origin.

Part of the technical difficulty of the paper comes from the necessity to understand how the various categories involved behave under finite extensions of the base field
k
:
this is crucial, for example, to apply Galois-descent ideas in the study of the fundamental sequences.
In particular, we have to prove some invariance properties of Ayoub’s categories of motivic local systems with respect to the chosen base field
k
, which are already known to hold for Ivorra–Morel’s categories.
To this end, we establish some auxiliary short exact sequences of Tannaka groups involving Artin motivic sheaves, generalizing results by Nori and Ayoub.
The reader may safely skip these additional passages in a first reading, by assuming
k
algebraically closed throughout the entire paper.

As an application of our comparison theorem, we recover one of the main results of Tubach’s paper
[
Tub25
]
, in which Nori’s realization functor (
1
) is extended to motivic sheaves:

Theorem

(Thm.
6.1
, Thm.
6.3
, Prop.
6.6
)
.

The six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
admits a canonical
\infty
-categorical enhancement.
Consequently, for every
k
-variety
X
there exists a canonical realization functor

\Nri_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))

(2)

such that the Betti realization over
X
factors as

\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\Nri_{X}^{*}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\xrightarrow{\iota_{X}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X).

As
X
varies, the functors (
2
) are compatible with the six operations and respect weights.

The existence and essential uniqueness of the realization functors (
2
), as well as their compatibility with the six operations, follow at once from the
\infty
-categorical universal property of the six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
.
The same universal property implies that all self-equivalences of Voevodsky motivic sheaves as a six functor formalism are trivial.
Exploiting the realization functors (
2
), we obtain a partial analogue of this property for Nori motivic sheaves:

Theorem

(Thm.
6.13
)
.

All self-equivalences of the six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
respecting the perverse
t
-structures are trivial.

It is natural to expect that every self-equivalence of Nori motivic sheaves be
t
-exact.
Unfortunately, this looks like a deeper question, related to the existence of the motivic perverse
t
-structure on
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
.
In fact, as proved by Tubach in
[
Tub25
, Thm. 4.11]
, the existence of the motivic perverse
t
-structures over all
k
-varieties would imply that all realization functors (
2
) are equivalences.
This would allow one to rephrase our comparison theorem in terms of Voevodsky motivic sheaves.
It would be interesting to have some unconditional analogue of our result purely in the setting of Voevodsky motives.

One last by-product of our comparison theorem is the following independence property of Ayoub’s construction of Nori motivic sheaves:

Theorem

(Thm.
6.16
)
.

Fix two distinct complex embeddings
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
Then, for every
k
-variety
X
, the
\infty
-categories of homotopy-fixed points
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
constructed via
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are canonically equivalent, compatibly with the six operations.

This result is non-trivial because the six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
does depend on the chosen complex embedding.
The key point is that Ivorra–Morel’s categories are already known to be independent of all auxiliary choices, as it should be the case for categories of motivic sheaves.

Related and future work

The
\infty
-categorical enhancement of Ivorra–Morel’s theory of Nori motivic sheaves, and the consequent realization from Voevodsky motivic sheaves, have been obtained previously by Tubach in
[
Tub25
]
using different methods.
Tubach’s methods also apply to other six functor formalisms endowed with ordinary
t
-structures, such as Saito’s mixed Hodge modules.
In fact, the Hodge-theoretic version of Tubach’s result plays a role in the construction of the fundamental short exact sequence by the first-named author.

Compared to Tubach’s approach to Nori motivic sheaves, ours has two main advantages:
it does not resort to the ordinary
t
-structure, and it yields a more explicit description of the realization functors from Voevodsky motivic sheaves.
In a forthcoming project, we plan to study the Hodge-theoretic variant of Ayoub’s construction of Nori motivic sheaves as homotopy-fixed points:
this will yield a clean Tannakian description of the six functor formalism on mixed Hodge modules of geometric origin.
We will then recover the Hodge realization functor of Nori motivic sheaves constructed in
[
Tub25
]
as a natural restriction functor on homotopy-fixed points.

Structure of the paper

In Section
1
, after reviewing the formalism of Voevodsky motivic sheaves, we explain how they relate to sheaves of geometric origin through the Betti realization.
Along the way, we study the compatibility of the theory with finite extensions of the base field;
since these technical results play a purely auxiliary role in the paper, the proofs can be safely skipped in a first reading.

In Section
2
, we review Ivorra–Morel’s theory of Nori motivic sheaves in some detail.
After explaining the definition of these categories in terms of Voevodsky motivic sheaves, we summarize the construction of the six operations.
In the final part, we focus on motivic local systems, and we discuss their relation to local systems of geometric origin through the fundamental exact sequence.

The goal of Section
3
is to derive the same fundamental sequence in the setting of Voevodsky motivic sheaves.
The main constructions rely on the formalism of equivariant objects under pro-algebraic groups, discussed in Appendix
A
.

In Section
4
, following Ayoub, we study categories of equivariant complexes of geometric origin under the motivic Galois group.
The main result is the construction of a perverse
t
-structure satisfying the equivariant version of Beilinson’s equivalence.

Section
5
is devoted to our main result, in which we compare Ivorra–Morel’s categories of Nori motives with Ayoub’s equivariant categories.
The proof combines the main results of the previous three sections.

In the final Section
6
, we apply our comparison theorem to the construction of realization functors from Voevodsky motivic sheaves to Nori motivic sheaves.
Other interesting consequences of our main result are discussed as well.

Notation and conventions

Algebraic Geometry

•

We work over a base field
k
of characteristic
0
endowed with a complex embedding
\sigma\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

•

By a
k
-variety
we mean a reduced, separated
k
-scheme of finite type.

•

By a
morphism of
k
-varieties
we mean a morphism of
k
-schemes.

•

Given a
k
-variety
X
and an open subvariety
U\subset X
, by the
closed complement
of
U
in
X
we mean the closed subspace
X\setminus U
endowed with the reduced scheme structure.

•

Given a
k
-variety
X
, we let
\mathsf{Op}_{X}
denote the poset of Zariski dense open subsets of
X
, ordered by inclusion.

Analytic sheaves

Let
X
be a
k
-variety.

•

We let
X^{\sigma}
denote the complex-analytic space underlying the complex variety
X\times_{k,\sigma}\mathbb{C}
, and we call it the
\sigma
-analytification
of
X
.

•

We let
D(X)
denote the derived category of sheaves of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces for the complex-analytic topology on
X^{\sigma}
.

•

We let
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\subset D(X)
denote the
k
-algebraic constructible derived category (see Definition
1.14
).

•

We let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)\subset\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
denote the heart of the perverse
t
-structure.

•

If
X
is smooth over
k
, we let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
denote the abelian subcategory of shifted local systems (see Notation
1.28
).

Motivic sheaves and motivic Galois groups

•

We let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
denote Nori’s motivic Galois group;
we write it as
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k,\sigma)
if we want to stress its dependence on
\sigma
.

•

We let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
denote Ayoub’s spectral motivic Galois group;
we write it as
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k,\sigma)
if we want to stress its dependence on
\sigma
.

•

We let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
denote the classical pro-algebraic group underlying
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
;
we write it as
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma)
if we want to stress its dependence on
\sigma
.

•

We let
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
denote the stable
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear étale Voevodsky motivic sheaves on
X
.

•

We let
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
denote the sub-
\infty
-category of constructible motivic sheaves.

•

We let
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
denote the abelian category of perverse Nori motives over
X
;
we write it as
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X)
if we want to stress its dependence on
\sigma
(and, implicitly, on the base field
k
).

1
Preliminaries on Voevodsky motives and sheaves of geometric origin

This first section focuses on the theory of Voevodsky motivic sheaves, which lies at the background of the recent approaches to Nori motivic sheaves.
After recalling well-known facts about Voevodsky motives and the Betti realization, we introduce the associated Betti algebras and summarize their properties.
Then we review the theory of constructible sheaves of geometric origin, following Ayoub’s treatment in
[
Ayo21
, § 1.6]
.
We are particularly interested in the abelian categories of perverse sheaves of geometric origin and in their subcategories of shifted local systems.

Throughout, we work over a base field
k
of characteristic
0
.
For future reference, in the course of the section we study the behaviour of the theory with respect to finite extensions of
k
;
the details of this technical discussion can be safely skipped in a first reading.

1.1
Voevodsky motives and the Betti realization

For every
k
-variety
X

there exists a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear triangulated category of

Voevodsky motivic sheaves
on
X
,
which underlies a symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category.
Several constructions of these motivic categories are available in the literature, notably:
Voevodsky’s original construction
of Nisnevich motives with transfers,
étale motives without transfers by Ayoub (see
[
Ayo07
]
),
and Beilinson motives by Cisinski–Déglise (see
[
CD19
]
).
All these approaches are known to yield equivalent categories (see
[
Ayo14b
, Thm. B.1]
and
[
CD19
, Thm. 16.2.18]
).
Throughout this paper, we work with Ayoub’s version of the theory.

We let
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
denote the category of

\mathbb{Q}
-linear étale Voevodsky motivic sheaves over
X
:
it is constructed in
[
Ayo14b
, § 3]

starting from the category
\mathsf{Sm}/X
of smooth
X
-schemes,
by taking the
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear presheaves on it
and imposing
étale descent,

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance,
and
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-stability.
By construction, there is a canonical functor

M_{X}\colon\mathsf{Sm}/X\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)

associating to every smooth
X
-scheme
W
its relative motive.
The object
\mathbb{Q}_{X}:=M_{X}(X)\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
,
called the
unit motive
over
X
,
is the unit for the monoidal structure on
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
.
By
[
Ayo07
, Thm. 4.5.67]
, the triangulated category
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
is compactly generated by the Tate twists of the motives
M_{X}(W)
with
W\in\mathsf{Sm}/X
.

As
X
varies among all
k
-varieties, the categories
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
are endowed with a canonical six functor formalism:
Ayoub’s work
[
Ayo07
]
describes the construction of the six operations over quasi-projective bases, and Cisinski–Déglise’s work
[
CD19
]
extends it beyond the quasi-projective setting.
The inverse image functor

f^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(Y)\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)

associated to a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
is defined by extension of the base-change functor

\mathsf{Sm}/Y\to\mathsf{Sm}/X,\quad W\mapsto W\times_{Y}X.

Its right adjoint is the direct image functor

f_{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(Y).

The tensor product functor

-\otimes-\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\times\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)

is defined by extension of the fibre product

\mathsf{Sm}/X\times\mathsf{Sm}/X\to\mathsf{Sm}/X,\quad(W_{1},W_{2})\mapsto W_{1}\times_{X}W_{2}.

These rules allow one to compare certain combinations of the six operations by looking at their effect on the generating sites.

Remark 1.1
.

In general, the direct image functor
f_{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(Y)
does not extend a functor
\mathsf{Sm}/X\to\mathsf{Sm}/Y
.
There is, however, one important exception:
given a finite étale morphism
q\colon X^{\prime}\to X
, the functor
q_{*}
coincides with the left adjoint to
q^{*}
, which extends the forgetful functor
\mathsf{Sm}/{X^{\prime}}\to\mathsf{Sm}/X
.
Moreover, the natural transformation between functors
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)

\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)}\xrightarrow{\eta}q_{*}q^{*}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)}

and the natural transformation between functors
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})

\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})}\xrightarrow{\eta}q^{*}q_{*}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})}

are both multiplication by the degree of
q
(see
[
CD19
, Prop. 13.7.6]
).
It follows that every object
M\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
is a direct summand of
q_{*}q^{*}M
, while every object
M^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})
is a direct summand of
q^{*}q_{*}M^{\prime}
.

An object of
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
is called
constructible
if it belongs to the thick triangulated subcategory generated by the motives
M_{X}(W)
with
W\in\mathsf{Sm}/X
.
Since
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
is compactly generated by such motives, it follows that an object is constructible if and only if it is compact (see
[
CD19
, Prop. 15.1.4]
).
On the other hand, by
[
CD16
, Thm. 6.3.26]
, this notion of constructibility matches with the topological intuition, namely:
an object
A\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
is constructible if and only if there is a stratification of
X
into locally closed subvarieties
S
such that the inverse image of
A
on each stratum
S

is dualizable in the symmetric monoidal category
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(S)
.
We let
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
denote the full subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
spanned by the constructible objects.
By
[
Ayo14b
, Thm. 8.10, Thm. 8.12]
,
the subcategories
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
are stable under the six operations.
In conclusion,

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
is the smallest thick triangulated subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
containing the unit motive
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
and stable under any functor
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
obtained out of the six operations.

Let us now assume that we are given a complex embedding

\sigma\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
This allows us to compare the theory of Voevodsky motives with the classical theory of sheaves for the analytic topology.

Notation 1.2
.

Let
X
be a
k
-variety.

•

We let
X^{\sigma}
denote the complex-analytic space attached to
X
via
\sigma
:
it is the complex-analytic space underlying the
\mathbb{C}
-variety
X\times_{k,\sigma}\mathbb{C}
.

•

We let
D(X)
denote the derived category of sheaves of
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces over the complex-analytic space
X^{\sigma}
;
we write it as
D_{\sigma}(X)
if we want to stress its dependence on the chosen embedding
\sigma
(and, implicitly, on the base field
k
).

In
[
Ayo10
]
,
Ayoub constructs a system of triangulated adjunctions

\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\leftrightarrows D(X)\colon\Bti_{X,*}.

The functor
\Bti_{X}^{*}
,
called the
Betti realization functor
,
extends the change-of-site functor

\mathsf{Sm}/X\to\mathsf{Sm}/{X^{\sigma}},\quad W\mapsto W^{\sigma}.

The functors
\Bti_{X}^{*}
are compatible with inverse images and with the tensor product.
Consequently, the right adjoint functors
\Bti_{X,*}
are compatible with direct images.
The combination of
[
Ayo10
, Thm. 3.4, Thm. 3.7, Thm. 3.8]
shows that the functors

\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\to D(X)

obtained by restriction to constructible objects
commute with all the six operations.

The Betti realization is controlled by the Betti algebra:

Lemma 1.3

(
[
Ayo21
, Defn. 1.4.1]
)
.

The following hold:

1.

For every
k
-variety
X
, the object

\mathcal{B}_{X}:=\Bti_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)

is naturally a commutative algebra object, called the Betti algebra of
X
.

2.

For every morphism of
k
-varieties
f\colon X\to Y
, there is a canonical commutative algebra isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)

f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{Y}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{B}_{X}.

In particular, for every
k
-variety
X
, there is a canonical isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)

a_{X}^{*}\mathcal{B}_{k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{B}_{X},

where
a_{X}\colon X\to\Spec(k)
denotes the structural morphism.

Proof.

The first statement is a formal consequence of
the fact that
\Bti_{X}^{*}
is symmetric monoidal.
Let us just mention that the unit of
\mathcal{B}_{X}
is the unit morphism

\mathbb{Q}_{X}\xrightarrow{\eta}\Bti_{X,*}\Bti_{X}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}=\mathcal{B}_{X},

while the multiplication is defined as the composite

\displaystyle\Bti_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}\otimes\Bti_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}\xrightarrow{\eta}

\displaystyle\Bti_{X,*}\Bti_{X}^{*}(\Bti_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}\otimes\Bti_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bti_{X,*}(\Bti_{X}^{*}\Bti_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}\otimes\Bti_{X}^{*}\Bti_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\Bti_{X,*}(\mathbb{Q}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{X})=\Bti_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}.

See
[
Ayo14
, Cor. 1.15]
for more details.

The morphism in the second statement is defined as the composite

f^{*}\Bti_{Y,*}\mathbb{Q}_{Y}\xrightarrow{\eta}f^{*}\Bti_{Y,*}f_{*}f^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Y}=f^{*}f_{*}\Bti_{X,*}f^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Y}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\Bti_{X,*}f^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Y}=\Bti_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}.

Compatibility with multiplication and units follows formally from
the lax monoidality of the direct image functors
f_{*}
and of the functors
\Bti_{X,*}
and
\Bti_{Y,*}
.
In order to show that it is an isomorphism,
it suffices to check that the induced map

\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)}(A,f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{Y})\to\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)}(A,\mathcal{B}_{X})

is bijective when
A
runs over a well-chosen family
of compact generators of
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
.
This can be done as in the proof of
[
Ayo21
, Prop. 1.6.6]

(see also
[
Ayo21
, Rem. 1.6.7]
).
∎

Let us insist that
\mathcal{B}_{X}
is a commutative algebra object in
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
in the
\infty
-categorical sense.
This gives access to a well-behaved theory of modules over the Betti algebra, discussed in Subsection
1.2
below.

In the final part of the present subsection, we spell out the behaviour of Betti algebras under finite extensions of the base field
k
;
this is the prototype for several auxiliary results collected in the present section.
To avoid possible confusion, we need to use more explicit notation:

Notation 1.4
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, we write

\Bti_{\sigma,X}^{*}
,

\Bti_{\sigma,X,*}
,
and
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma,X}
in place of

\Bti_{X}^{*}
,

\Bti_{X,*}
,
and
\mathcal{B}_{X}
,
respectively,
if we want to stress their dependence on
\sigma

(and, implicitly, on the base field
k
).

Let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite extension.
Given a
k
-variety
X
, set
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
, and let
e_{X}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
denote the corresponding finite étale morphism.
Choose a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma
.

Proposition 1.5
.

The object
e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
is naturally a commutative algebra object, and there is a canonical commutative algebra isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)

\mathcal{B}_{\sigma,X}=e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}.

The starting point is the identification of complex-analytic spaces

X^{\sigma}=(X^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime}},

(3)

induced by the identification of
\mathbb{C}
-varieties

X\times_{k,\sigma}\mathbb{C}=(X\times_{k}k^{\prime})\times_{k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\mathbb{C}.

(4)

This determines a canonical equivalence of monoidal stable
\infty
-categories

D_{\sigma}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}),

which is compatible with the Betti realization functors in the expected way:

Lemma 1.6
.

The diagram of monoidal functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\sigma}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{X}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{\sigma,X}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes up to monoidal natural isomorphism.

Proof.

Indeed, the identification of complex-analytic spaces (
3
) extends to a natural isomorphism between functors on
\mathsf{Sm}/X

W^{\sigma}=(W\times_{X}X^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime}},

compatibly with the Cartesian monoidal structure.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.5
.

The commutative algebra structure of
e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}
is induced by that of
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}
:
the unit is defined as

\mathbb{Q}_{X}\xrightarrow{\eta}e_{X,*}e_{X}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}=e_{X,*}\mathbb{Q}_{X^{\prime}}\rightarrow e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}},

while the multiplication is defined as

\displaystyle e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}\otimes e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\eta}e_{X,*}e_{X}^{*}(e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}\otimes e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=e_{X,*}(e_{X}^{*}e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}\otimes e_{X}^{*}e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\epsilon}e_{X,*}(\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}.

The isomorphism in the statement
comes from the commutative diagram of
Lemma
1.6
.
Its compatibility with the units and with the multiplication maps comes down to the compatibility of units and co-units of adjunctions with respect to composition of functors;
we leave the details to the interested reader.
∎

Since the scheme
X^{\prime}
can be regarded either as a
k^{\prime}
-variety or as a
k
-variety, one can attach to it two distinct Betti algebras, namely
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma,X^{\prime}}
.
In order to understand their relation, it is convenient to consider all possible choices of
\sigma^{\prime}
at once.
So let
\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
denote the set of complex embeddings of
k^{\prime}
extending
\sigma
, and let us temporarily forget about the previous choice of an element
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
.

Proposition 1.7
.

There is a canonical commutative algebra isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})

\mathcal{B}_{\sigma,X^{\prime}}=\bigoplus_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}.

To begin with, note that we have a decomposition of complex-analytic spaces

(X^{\prime})^{\sigma}=\coprod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}(X^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime}}.

(5)

It is induced by the decomposition of
\mathbb{C}
-varieties

X^{\prime}\times_{k,\sigma}\mathbb{C}=(X\times_{k}k^{\prime})\times_{k,\sigma}\mathbb{C}=X\times_{k}(k^{\prime}\times_{k,\sigma}\mathbb{C})=\coprod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}X\times_{k,\sigma}\mathbb{C}=\coprod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}X^{\prime}\times_{k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\mathbb{C},

where the last passage witnesses the identifications of the form (
4
) as
\sigma^{\prime}
varies in
\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
.
We deduce a canonical equivalence of monoidal stable
\infty
-categories

D_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})=\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}).

(6)

This allows us to identify each factor
D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})
with the full sub-
\infty
-category of
D_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
spanned by the objects with support on
(X^{\prime})^{\sigma^{\prime}}
.

Remark 1.8
.

Under the equivalence (
6
), the unit object
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma,X^{\prime}}\in D_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
corresponds to the unit tuple
(\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}})_{\sigma^{\prime}}
.
The sheaves
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}
, extended by zero to
(X^{\prime})^{\sigma}
, form a family of pairwise orthogonal idempotents with respect to the monoidal structure of
D_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
, and the subcategory
D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})\subset D_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
can be identified with the essential image of the endofunctor
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}\otimes-
.

The decomposition (
6
) is compatible with the Betti realization functors in the expected way:

Lemma 1.9
.

The diagram of monoidal functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{\sigma,X^{\prime}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*})_{\sigma^{\prime}}}

commutes up to monoidal natural isomorphism.

Proof.

Indeed, the decomposition (
5
) extends to a functorial decomposition on
\mathsf{Sm}/{X^{\prime}}

W^{\sigma}=\coprod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}W^{\sigma^{\prime}}

compatible with the Cartesian monoidal structure.
This is induced by the functorial decomposition of
\mathbb{C}
-varieties

W\times_{k}\mathbb{C}=W\times_{X^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}\times_{k}\mathbb{C})=\coprod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}W\times_{X^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}\times_{k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\mathbb{C})=\coprod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}W\times_{k^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\mathbb{C},

where the second passage witnesses the base decomposition (
5
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.7
.

Note that the right adjoint to the functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})\to\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}),\quad A\mapsto(\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}A)_{\sigma^{\prime}}

is given by

\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime}),\quad(K_{\sigma^{\prime}})_{\sigma^{\prime}}\mapsto\bigoplus_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime},*}K_{\sigma^{\prime}}.

We deduce the commutative algebra isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\sigma,X^{\prime}}:=\Bti_{\sigma,X^{\prime},*}\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma,X^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Bti_{\sigma,X^{\prime},*}\Bti_{\sigma,X^{\prime}}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{X^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime},*}\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{X^{\prime}}

(by Lemma
1.9
)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime},*}\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=:\bigoplus_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}},

as wanted.
∎

1.2
Constructible complexes of geometric origin

We need to study
the Betti realization
\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\to D(X)

more closely.
This leads us to review the theory of constructible sheaves.
Since the complex-analytic spaces considered here arise from
k
-varieties,
the correct notion of constructibility is
k
-algebraic.

Throughout, we fix a complex embedding
\sigma\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

Notation 1.10
.

Let
X
be a
k
-variety.

•

By a
k
-stratification

\Sigma
of
X
we mean a collection of locally-closed
k
-subvarieties
S
of
X
, called the
strata
of
\Sigma
, with the following properties:

(i)

Each stratum
S\in\Sigma
is smooth over
k
and connected.

(ii)

The strata of
\Sigma
define a partition of the underlying topological space of
X
.

(iii)

For each stratum
S\in\Sigma
, the Zariski closure
\bar{S}
of
S
inside
X
is a union of strata.

•

Given two
k
-stratifications
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
of
X
, we say that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a
refinement
of
\Sigma
if each stratum of
\Sigma
is a union of strata of
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

•

We let
\mathsf{Strat}_{X/k}
denote the poset of
k
-stratifications of
X
, with the order defined by saying that
\Sigma\leq\Sigma^{\prime}
if and only if
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a refinement of
\Sigma
.

Given two
k
-stratifications
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\mathsf{Strat}_{X/k}
, the collection of all intersections
S_{1}\cap S_{2}
with
S_{1}\in\Sigma_{1}
and
S_{2}\in\Sigma_{2}
satisfies conditions (ii) and (iii) of a
k
-stratification, but not necessarily condition (i).
Nevertheless, any collection of locally closed
k
-subvarieties of
X
satisfying (ii) and (iii) admits a refinement which also satisfies (i).
This implies that the poset
\mathsf{Strat}_{X/k}
is filtered.

Definition 1.11
.

Let
X
be a
k
-variety, and let
\Sigma\in\mathsf{Strat}_{X/k}
.

•

An object
K\in D(X)
is
\Sigma
-constructible
if, for every stratum
S\in\Sigma
, with inclusion
s\colon S\hookrightarrow X
, the object
s^{*}K\in D(S)
is dualizable.

•

We let
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X,\Sigma)
denote the full sub-
\infty
-category of
D(X)
spanned by the
\Sigma
-constructible objects.

•

We define the stable
\infty
-category of
constructible complexes
as the filtered union

\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X):=\bigcup_{\Sigma\in\mathsf{Strat}_{X/k}}\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X,\Sigma)\subset D(X).

Let us note the following result:

Proposition 1.12

(
[
Ayo21
, Prop. 1.2.13]
)
.

As
X
varies among all
k
-varieties, the subcategories
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\subset D(X)
are stable under the six operations.

Corollary 1.13
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, the Betti realization
\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\to D(X)
restricts to

\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\to\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X).

Proof.

By
[
CD16
, Thm. 6.3.26]
, for every object
A\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
there is a
k
-algebraic stratification of
X
such that the restriction of
A
to each stratum is dualizable;
using localization triangles, one can reconstruct
A
from such restrictions in a finite number of steps.
Hence, in view of Proposition
1.12
, it suffices to observe that the Betti realization functors take dualizable constructible motives to dualizable sheaves (being symmetric monoidal).
∎

Note that, however, the right adjoint
\Bti_{X,*}\colon D(X)\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
does not take
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
to
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
:
for instance, the Betti algebra
\mathcal{B}_{X}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
is not constructible.

The most interesting part of
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
consists of the complexes obtained out of relative cohomology of
k
-varieties:

Definition 1.14

(
[
Ayo21
, Def. 1.6.1, Rmk. 1.6.2]
)
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, we define the stable
\infty
-category
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
of
constructible complexes of geometric origin
as the smallest sub-
\infty
-category of
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
containing all objects of the form
p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W^{\sigma}}=\Bti_{X}^{*}(p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W})
, with
p\colon W\to X
a proper morphism of
k
-varieties, and stable under finite limits, finite colimits, and retracts:
in other words, the full sub-
\infty
-category spanned by the thick triangulated subcategory generated by the objects
p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W^{\sigma}}
above;
we write it as
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)
if we want to stress its dependence on
\sigma
.

Setting
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X,\Sigma):=\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)\cap\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X,\Sigma)
for every
k
-stratification
\Sigma
of
X
, we get the filtered union

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)=\bigcup_{\Sigma\in\mathsf{Strat}_{X/k}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X,\Sigma).

In the rest of the present subsection, we discuss the close relation between sheaves of geometric origin and Voevodsky motives.
A first remarkable result is the following:

Proposition 1.15

(
[
Ayo21
, Rem. 1.6.4]
)
.

As
X
varies among all
k
-varietes, the subcategories
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)\subset\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
are stable under the six operations, as well as under Beilinson’s gluing functors.

Proof.

Stability under inverse images follows from proper base-change, and stability under the tensor product follows from the projection formula.
The method of
[
Ayo07
, §§ 2.2.2, 2.3.10]
allows one to deduce stability under the six operations from this.
Stability under the gluing functors follows from their description in terms of the six operations (see
[
Mor25
, §§ 3-7]
or
[
IM24
, § 3]
).
∎

Corollary 1.16

(
[
Ayo21
, Rem. 1.6.4]
)
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, the Betti realization
\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\to\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
refines to

\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X).

Proof.

By
[
Ayo07
, Lem. 2.2.23]
, the stable
\infty
-category
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
is generated under colimits, negative Tate twists, and negative shifts by the objects of the form
p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W}
with
p\colon W\to X
a proper morphism.
Hence, the result follows from Proposition
1.15
.
∎

This leads to a more conceptual description of sheaves of geometric origin as a six functor formalism.

Notation 1.17

(
[
Ayo21
, Rmk. 1.1.21]
)
.

Let
X
be a
k
-variety.

•

We let
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})
denote the stable
\infty
-category of
\mathcal{B}_{X}
-modules in
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
, and we write

\mathcal{B}_{X}\otimes-\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})

for the canonical functor.

•

We let
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})
denote the smallest sub-
\infty
-category of
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})
containing the image of
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
and stable under finite limits, finite colimits, and retracts.

Proposition 1.18

(
[
Ayo21
, Prop. 1.6.6]
,
[
CD19
, Ex. 17.1.7]
)
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, the Betti realization
\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\to D(X)
admits a canonical factorization of the form

\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{B}_{X}\otimes-}\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})\to D(X),

compatibly with the six operations.
Moreover, the functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})\to D(X)

obtained by restriction is fully faithful.

Corollary 1.19
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, there is a canonical equivalence of stable
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X),

compatibly with the six operations.

Proof.

Indeed, compatibility of the factorization in Proposition
1.18
with the six operations implies that the generators of
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
lie in the essential image of
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})
.
∎

Remark 1.20
.

Although the functors
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})\to D(X)
are not fully faithful, it is still possible to construct by hand a six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
of unbounded complexes of geometric origin such that the previous functors factor through equivalences
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
(see
[
Ayo21
, Defn. 1.6.1]
).
Despite the suggestive notation,
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
is not defined as a full subcategory of
D(X)
.

Later in the paper, we need to know how complexes of geometric origin change under finite extensions of the base field
k
.
For this reason, we now come back to the situation considered at the end of Subsection
1.1
, and we relate the results obtained there to sheaves of geometric origin.
So let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite extension.
Given a
k
-variety
X
, set
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
, and write
e_{X}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
for the corresponding finite étale morphism.
Choose a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}

extending
\sigma
.
We have a symmetric monoidal functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{\sigma,X})=\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X;e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}})\xrightarrow{e_{X}^{*}}\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime};e_{X}^{*}e_{X,*}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}})\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime};\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}),

where the first equivalence is induced by Proposition
1.5
.
By restriction to constructible objects,
this induces a symmetric monoidal functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{\sigma,X})\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime};\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}).

(7)

In view of Corollary
1.19
,
we look at the corresponding functor on constructible complexes.

Lemma 1.21
.

The functor

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})

corresponding to (
7
) under the identifications of Corollary
1.19
is an equivalence.

Proof.

The canonical equivalence
D_{\sigma}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})
allows us to view both categories in the statement as full subcategories of
D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})
, and we claim that their essential images coincide.
To this end, it suffices to check that each category contains the generators of the other one.

In one direction, given a proper morphism of
k
-varieties
p\colon Y\rightarrow X
, let
p^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
denote the proper morphism of
k^{\prime}
-varieties obtained by base-change to
k^{\prime}
:
it fits into the Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{X}}

We have a chain of natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\Bti_{\sigma,X}^{*}(p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Y})

\displaystyle=\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}(e_{X}^{*}p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Y})

(by Lemma
1.6
)

\displaystyle=\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}(p^{\prime}_{*}e_{Y}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Y})

(by proper base-change)

\displaystyle=\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}(p^{\prime}_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Y^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\textup{(as $e_{Y}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Y}=\mathbb{Q}_{Y^{\prime}}$)}.

Thus, each generator of
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)
belongs to
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})
.
Note that the inclusion
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})
just obtained is compatible with the functor (
7
):
essentially, this comes down to Lemma
1.6
.

In the other direction, given a proper morphism of
k^{\prime}
-varieties
q\colon Z\rightarrow X^{\prime}
, consider the composite morphism of
k
-varieties
r:=e_{X}\circ q\colon Z\rightarrow X
.
Since
e_{X}
is finite étale, the object
q_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Z}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime})
is a direct summand of
e_{X}^{*}e_{X,*}q_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Z}
(by Remark
1.1
).
Since
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)
is closed under retracts inside
D_{\sigma}(X)=D_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})
(by definition), it suffices to check that
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)
contains the complex
\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}(e_{X}^{*}e_{X,*}q_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Z})
in order to conclude that it contains
\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}(q_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Z})
.
But we have

\displaystyle\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}(e_{X}^{*}e_{X,*}q_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Z})=

\displaystyle\Bti_{\sigma,X}^{*}(e_{X,*}q_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Z})

(by Lemma
1.6
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bti_{\sigma,X}(r_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{Z})

\displaystyle\textup{(as $e_{X}\circ q=r$)}.

Thus, each generator of
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})
belongs to
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)
.
This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Recall that the categories of analytic sheaves on
X^{\prime}
, regarded either as a
k^{\prime}
-variety or as a
k
-variety, are related by the decomposition (
6
), indexed by the set
\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
of complex embeddings of
k^{\prime}
extending
\sigma
.
In the following, we forget again about the previous choice of an element
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
and rather consider all such extensions at once.
We have a canonical equivalence

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime};\Btialg_{\sigma,X^{\prime}})=\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime};\oplus_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\Btialg_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}})=\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X^{\prime};\Btialg_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}),

where the first equivalence is induced by Proposition
1.7
.
By restriction to constructible objects, it induces an equivalence

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime};\Btialg_{\sigma,X^{\prime}})=\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime};\Btialg_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}).

In view of Corollary
1.19
, we look for the same relation on constructible complexes.

Lemma 1.22
.

The equivalence (
6
) restrict to a monoidal equivalence

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})=\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})

making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime};\Btialg_{\sigma,X^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime};\Btialg_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commute up to monoidal natural isomorphism.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime};\Btialg_{\sigma,X^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime};\Btialg_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime})}

commutes up to monoidal natural isomorphism.
Since the algebra structure of Betti algebras comes from the Betti realization, this follows formally from Lemma
1.9
.
∎

1.3
Perverse sheaves and local systems of geometric origin

Let
X
be a
k
-variety.
The theory of
[
BBD+18
]
defines the perverse
t
-structure on
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
;
its heart
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
is the abelian category of
k
-algebraically constructible perverse sheaves on
X^{\sigma}
, which we simply call
perverse sheaves
on
X
here.

We want to specialize the theory of perverse sheaf to the setting of sheaves of geometric origin.

Notation 1.23
.

We let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
denote the intersection
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
;
we write it as
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
if we want to stress its dependence on the embedding
\sigma
.

This is one possible definition of perverse sheaves of geometric origin:
the superscript is meant to distinguish it from another possible definition, introduced in Subsection
2.1
.

In order to make sure that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
is a reasonable category of perverse sheaves, we need the following result:

Proposition 1.24

(
[
Ayo21
, Rmk. 1.6.23]
)
.

The perverse
t
-structure on
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
restricts to a
t
-structure on
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
, and its heart
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
is stable under subquotients and extensions inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
.

Proof.

We want to apply the criterion of
[
Ayo21
, Lem. 1.6.22]
.
To this end, let
\mathcal{S}
denote the collection of all subquotients in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
of the perverse sheaves
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{n}(p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W^{\sigma}})
with
p\colon W\to X
proper and
n\geq 0
.
Define
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
as the smallest sub-
\infty
-category
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
containing
\mathcal{S}
and stable under finite colimits and negative shifts:
in other words, the full sub-
\infty
-category spanned by the triangulated subcategory generated by
\mathcal{S}
.
By
[
Ayo21
, Lem. 1.6.22]
, the perverse
t
-structure on
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
restricts to a
t
-structure on
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
, and the heart
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
of the latter coincides with the smallest abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
containing
\mathcal{S}
and stable under subquotients and extensions.
In order to conclude, we claim that
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
coincides in fact with
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
.

To this end, we start by checking that all objects of
\mathcal{S}
belong to
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
.
This is a consequence of the Decomposition Theorem
[
BBD+18
, Thm. 6.2.5]
:
for every proper morphism
p\colon W\to X
, the complex
p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W^{\sigma}}\in\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
decomposes as

p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W^{\sigma}}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{n}(p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W^{\sigma}})[-n],

and moreover each direct summand
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{n}(p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W^{\sigma}})
is semisimple in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
.
Since
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
is stable under retracts and negative shifts (by definition), it contains indeed the whole of
\mathcal{S}
, and therefore it contains
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
.

It remains to show that
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
is stable under retracts inside
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
, which follows formally from the boundedness of the perverse
t
-structure.
In detail, fix a non-zero object
K\in\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
which is isomorphic in
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
to a direct sum
K^{\prime}\oplus K^{\prime\prime}
, and let us show that
K^{\prime}
lies in
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
as well.
To this end, let
a(K)
(resp.
b(K)
) denotes the minimal (resp. maximal) index
n
with
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{n}(K)\neq 0
, and let
l(K):=b(K)-a(K)
denote the perverse cohomological length of
K
.
We prove the claim by induction on
l(K)\geq 0
.
The base step is when
l(K)=0
, in which case
K\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}[a(K)]
and the conclusion is already known (as
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
is stable under retracts inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
).
For the inductive step, assume that
l(K)\geq 1
and that the conclusion is known to hold for all complexes with perverse cohomological length smaller than
l(K)
.
Form the truncation triangle in
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)

\tau_{<b}K\to K\to\tau_{\geq b}K\to(\tau_{<b}K)[1],

and observe that the direct sum decomposition of
K
induces a decomposition of the whole triangle.
Since
l(\tau_{<b}K)<l(K)
and
l(\tau_{\geq b}K)=0
(by construction), the direct summands
\tau_{<b}K^{\prime}
and
\tau_{\geq b}K^{\prime}
lie in
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
by inductive hypothesis.
Since
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
is a triangulated subcategory of
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
, this implies that
K^{\prime}
lies in
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)_{\mathcal{S}}
as well.
This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Remark 1.25
.

It follows that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
coincides with the classical abelian category of perverse sheaves of geometric origin, as defined in
[
BBD+18
, § 6.2.4]
.

Perverse sheaves of geometric origin have a useful characterization in terms of Voevodsky motives:

Corollary 1.26
.

The category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
coincides with the smallest abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
containing the image of the homological functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\Bti_{X}^{*}}\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)

and stable under subquotients and extensions.

Proof.

As shown in the proof of Proposition
1.24
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
coincides with the smallest abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
containing the perverse sheaves
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{n}(p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W^{\sigma}})
with
p:W\to X
proper and
n\geq 0
and stable under subquotients and extensions.
Therefore, in order to conclude that the abelian category in the statement equals
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
, it suffices to show that each of them contains the generators of the other one.

In one direction, it suffices to observe that the above perverse sheaves
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}(p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W^{\sigma}})={}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\Bti_{X}^{*}(p_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{W})
obviously lie in the essential image of
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ\Bti_{X}^{*}
.

In the other direction, it suffices to check that the composite functor
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ\Bti_{X}^{*}
factors through
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
.
This follows from the fact that
\Bti_{X}^{*}
factors thorough
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
(by Corollary
1.16
).
∎

For future reference, we spell out the behaviour of perverse sheaves of geometric origin with respect to finite extensions of
k
.
As usual, let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite extension, set
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
, and write
e_{X}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
for the corresponding finite étale morphism.
Choose a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma
.

Lemma 1.27
.

The following hold:

1.

There is a canonical equivalence

\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime}).

(8)

2.

If
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois, the inverse image functor
e_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime})
induces a canonical equivalence

\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime})^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)},

where the action of
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
on
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime})
is induced by its action on
X^{\prime}
via
k
-automorphisms.

Proof.

The first statement follows from the fact that the equivalence of Lemma
1.21
clearly respects the perverse
t
-structures.

The second statement follows from the fact that the assignment
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
, regarded as a fibered category over étale morphisms, is a stack.

In detail, since
e_{X}
is finite étale, the functor
e_{X}^{*}
identifies objects of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
with objects of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime})
endowed with an isomorphism between their inverse images under the two projections
X^{\prime}\times_{X}X^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
, subject to the usual cocycle condition over the triple fibered product
X^{\prime}\times_{X}X^{\prime}\times_{X}X^{\prime}
;
similarly, it identifies morphisms in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
with those morphisms in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime})
which are compatible with the additional isomorphisms.
This holds regardless of whether
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois or not.
The hypothesis that
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois implies that the scheme
X^{\prime}\times_{X}X^{\prime}=X^{\prime}\times_{k^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}\times_{k}k^{\prime})
is a disjoint union of
[k^{\prime}\colon k]
-many copies of
X^{\prime}
permuted transitively by
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
.
Under this identification, equipping an object of
