\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime})
with an isomorphism between its two inverse images, subject to the cocycle condition, amounts precisely to turning it into an equivariant object under the
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
-action.
∎

From now until the end of the present section, let
X
be a smooth
k
-variety.
Recall from
[
BBD+18
, § 4]
that, if
X
is connected of dimension
d\geq 0
, the shift functor
[d]\colon\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\to\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
identifies the abelian category of local systems on
X
with a full abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
;
this naturally generalizes to arbitrary smooth
k
-varieties, by considering each connected component separately.
Throughout this paper, we always view local systems as perverse sheaves:

Notation 1.28
.

We let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
denote the full abelian subcategory of shifted local systems inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
.

Let us record the following important property:

Lemma 1.29
.

The abelian subcategory
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
is stable under subquotients and extensions.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
X
is connected, say of dimension
d
.

In this case, stability under extensions follows from the facts that extensions in the abelian category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
coincide with extensions in the triangulated category
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
(see
[
BBD+18
, Thm. 1.3.6]
), that shifting by
-d
induces a bijection on extensions in
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
(being an equivalence), and that ordinary local systems are stable under extensions inside ordinary constructible sheaves.

Stability under subquotients follows from the fact that every object of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
has finite length (see
[
BBD+18
, Thm. 4.3.1(i)]
) and that an object
L\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
is irreducible in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
if and only if it is irreducible in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
(see
[
BBD+18
, Lem. 4.3.3]
).
∎

Note that, with our conventions, the abelian category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
carries a shifted tensor product:
it is defined by the formula

L_{1}\otimes^{\dagger}L_{2}:=(L_{1}[-d]\otimes L_{2}[-d])[d]

when
X
is connected of dimension
d
, and using the same formula separately on each connected component of
X
in the general case.

We are mostly interested in the case when
X
is geometrically connected over
k
, so that the complex-analytic space
X^{\sigma}
is connected.
In this case, the abelian category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
, equipped with the shifted tensor product, is a neutral Tannakian category over
\mathbb{Q}
:
the fibre functor at a closed point
x\in X^{\sigma}
is the shifted inverse image functor

x^{*}[-d]\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}},

where
d
denotes again the dimension of
X
.

Remark 1.30
.

Assume
X
connected of dimension
d
, and let
a_{X}\colon X\to\Spec(k)
denote the structural morphism.
The shifted inverse image functor

a_{X}^{*}[d]\colon\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(\Spec(k))\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)

takes values in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
and admits a right adjoint, given by the composite

{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ a_{X,*}[-d]\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(\Spec(k))=\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}.

It is a well-known fact from ordinary sheaf theory that
a_{X}^{*}[d]
is fully faithful, with essential image closed under subquotients.
This can be expressed purely in terms of the six operations:
for every
V\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(\Spec(k))
, the unit morphism

\eta\colon V\to{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}a_{X,*}[-d](a_{X}^{*}[d]V)

is invertible and, for every subobject
L\subset a_{X}^{*}[d]V
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
, the co-unit morphism

\epsilon\colon a_{X}^{*}[d]({}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}a_{X,*}[-d]L)\to L

is invertible.
The result generalizes naturally to arbitrary smooth
k
-varieties.

The theory of perverse sheaves of geometric origin gives access to a good notion of local system of geometric origin:

Notation 1.31
.

We let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
denote the intersection
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
;
we write it as
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
if we want to stress its dependence on the embedding
\sigma
.

Note that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}
is an abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
, stable under subquotients and extensions (by Lemma
1.29
).
Moreover, since
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
is stable under subquotients and extensions inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
(by Proposition
1.24
),
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
enjoys the same stability properties inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
.
In fact, since constructible complexes of geometric origin are stable under the tensor product (by Proposition
1.15
), in the geometrically connected case
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
is even a Tannakian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
.
We defer the study of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
from a Tannakian viewpoint to Section
3
.

Example 1.32
.

When
X=\Spec(k)
, there is no difference between perverse sheaves, ordinary constructible sheaves, or local systems.
In fact, we have canonical monoidal equivalences

\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(\Spec(k))=\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(\Spec(k))=\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}},

induced by the global sections functor.
∎

The behaviour of local systems of geometric origin with respect to finite extensions of the base field
k
is analogous to that of perverse sheaves:

Lemma 1.33
.

With the same notation as in Lemma
1.27
, the following statements hold:

1.

There is a canonical equivalence

\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X).

2.

If
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois, the inverse image functor
e_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime})
induces a canonical equivalence

\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime})^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)},

where the action of
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
on
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime})
is induced by its action on
X^{\prime}
via
k
-automorphisms.

Proof.

The first statement follows from the fact that the equivalence of Lemma
1.21
respects shifted local systems:
indeed, it respects dualizable complexes (being symmetric monoidal) as well as the ordinary
t
-structures.

The second statement is a particular case of Lemma
1.27
(2), because a perverse sheaf on
X
is a shifted local system if and only if this holds for its restriction to any given étale cover of
X
.
∎

2
Recollections on perverse Nori motives

In this section we summarize some highlights of the theory of perverse Nori motives, developed in
[
IM24
]
and
[
Ter24b
]
building on Nori’s theory of mixed motives (see
[
HM17
]
):
it is a theory of mixed motivic sheaves satisfying the formal properties listed by Beilinson in
[
Bei87
, § 5.10]
.

Throughout, we work over a field
k
of characteristic
0

endowed with a complex embedding

\sigma\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
The category of perverse Nori motives over a
k
-variety
X

is modelled on the abelian category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
.
We start by reviewing the definition of perverse Nori motives
and their general functoriality.
We then focus on the Tannakian aspects of the theory:
we discuss Nori’s motivic Galois group over a field,
and finally, following
[
Jac25
]
, we study the motivic Galois group of a general
k
-variety.

2.1
Universal property and functoriality

For every
k
-variety
X
, the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
of
perverse Nori motives
over
X
is defined in
[
IM24
, § 2.1]
as the universal abelian factorization of the homological functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\Bti_{X}^{*}}\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X).

By construction, it comes equipped with a homological functor

h_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)

and with a faithful exact functor

\iota_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)

such that the homological functor
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ\Bti_{X}^{*}
factors as the composite

{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{h_{X}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\xrightarrow{\iota_{X}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X).

By design,
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
is initial among all abelian categories
through which such a factorization is possible:
given another abelian category
\mathcal{A}(X)
endowed with a homological functor
h^{\prime}_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}(X)
,
a faithful exact functor

\iota^{\prime}_{X}\colon\mathcal{A}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
,
and a natural isomorphism between functors
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)

\kappa_{X}\colon{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ\Bti_{X}^{*}=\iota_{X}\circ h_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}\iota^{\prime}_{X}\circ h^{\prime}_{X},

there exist a unique faithful exact functor

o_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}(X)

providing a factorization of
h^{\prime}_{X}
of the form

h^{\prime}_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{h_{X}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\xrightarrow{o_{X}}\mathcal{A}(X),

and a unique natural isomorphism between functors
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)

\tilde{\kappa}_{X}\colon\iota_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}\iota^{\prime}_{X}\circ o_{X}

making the diagram of functors
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}\circ h_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime}_{X}\circ o_{X}\circ h_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime}_{X}\circ h^{\prime}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\kappa}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{X}}

commute.
In fact, in this situation,
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
can be identified with
the universal abelian factorization of the homological functor

h^{\prime}_{X}

(see
[
Ter24a
, Prop. 1.10, Cor. 2.9]
).

For instance, since the Betti realization takes values in complexes of geometric origin (by Corollary
1.16
), one could take
\mathcal{A}(X):=\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
- that is, one could replace
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
by
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
in the definition of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
.
In fact, this observation suggests considering a possible alternative notion of perverse sheaf of geometric origin:

Notation 2.1
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, we let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
denote the smallest abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
containing the essential image of
\iota_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
and stable under subquotients;
we write it as
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
if we want to stress its dependence on the embedding
\sigma
.

Since
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
is stable under subquotients inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
(by Proposition
1.24
), we have the inclusion
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
.
At this stage, it is not clear how far this inclusion is from an equivalence:
the point is that, while we already know that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
is stable under extensions inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
(again by Proposition
1.24
), no such stability is required in the definition of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
.

Remark 2.2
.

The triangulated category
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
is expected to carry a
t
-structure compatible with the perverse
t
-structure on
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
via the Betti realization.
If this
motivic perverse
t
-structure
on
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
exists, then its heart
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)^{\heartsuit}
enjoys the same universal property as
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
, and so the exact functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)^{\heartsuit}\subset\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{h_{X}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)

is necessarily an equivalence.
In the lack of a motivic perverse
t
-structure on
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
,
the homological functor
h_{X}
should be regarded as a formal way to define the degree
0
perverse cohomology object of a motivic complex.
By the very construction of universal abelian factorizations,
the abelian category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)

is generated by the image of
h_{X}
under subquotients
(see
[
IM24
, § 1]
).

The faithful exact functor
\iota_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)

extends to a conservative triangulated functor

\iota_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X))=\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X),

where the last passage witnesses Beilinson’s equivalence
[
Bei87a
, Thm. 1.3]
.
These functors can be used to lift the rich functoriality of the constructible derived categories to the setting of Nori motives.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
IM24
, Thm. 5.1]
,
[
Ter24b
, Thm. 2.1, Thm. 5.1]
)
.

As
X
varies among quasi-projective
k
-varieties,
the triangulated categories
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))

are endowed with a canonical six functor formalism,
and the triangulated functors
\iota_{X}

commute with the six operations.

The proof of this result is based on the method of
[
Ayo07
]
and
[
Ayo10
]
, which is tailored to the quasi-projective setting.
The four operations of type
f^{*}
,
f_{*}
,
f_{!}
and
f^{!}
are constructed in
[
IM24
]
, while the closed monoidal structure is constructed in
[
Ter24b
]
.
In both works, the basic principle is to exploit the universal property of perverse Nori motives in order to lift
t
-exact functors on the constructible derived categories and natural transformations thereof to the motivic level.
For future reference, let us illustrate this principle in a simple case:

Example 2.4

(
[
IM24
, § 2.5]
)
.

Let
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
be an open immersion of (quasi-projective)
k
-varieties.
By
[
BBD+18
, Prop. 4.2.5]
, the inverse image functor

j^{*}\colon\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(U)

(9)

is
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures, and so induces an exact functor

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U).

In fact, as a consequence of
[
Vol10
, Thm. 1]
, the trivial derived functor

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U))

recovers the original triangulated functor (
9
) modulo Beilinson’s equivalences.
Now consider the analogous inverse image functor of Voevodsky motives

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(U).

(10)

By
[
Ayo10
, Lem. 4.3]
, the upper half of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{X}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{U}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}

(11)

commutes up to canonical natural isomorphism, and thus the same holds for the outer rectangle.
Applying
[
Ter24a
, Prop. 2.5]
, we get a canonical exact functor

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(U)

(12)

making both halves of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(U,\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}

(13)

commute up to natural isomorphism.
Hence, the induced triangulated functor

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(U))

makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(U))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}

(14)

commute up to the induced natural isomorphism.
There is a similar lifting principle for natural transformations, stated in
[
Ter24a
, Prop. 3.4]
.
In particular, the usual connection isomorphisms on the constructible derived categories with respect to composition of open immersions lift to analogous natural isomorphisms on perverse Nori motives.
In this way, the assignment
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
becomes a triangulated fibered category over the category of open immersions between
k
-varieties.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Let us stress that (
12
) is the unique exact functor making the upper half of (
13
) commute on the nose;
the natural isomorphism filling the lower half of (
13
) is then completely determined by its compatibility with the natural isomorphism filling (
11
).
In other words, while the existence of the exact functor (
12
) depends on the existence of (
9
) (and on the commutativity of (
11
)), its actual expression only depends on the functor (
10
).

The same discussion of Example
2.4
applies to any class of
t
-exact functors on the constructible derived categories which is part of the six functor formalism and is stable under composition:
in particular, it applies to shifted inverse images under smooth morphisms and to direct images under closed immersions.
There is a similar lifting result for multilinear functors which are
t
-exact in each variable, such as the external tensor product (see
[
Ter24a
, § 4]
).
However, lifting the external tensor product to the motivic level
requires working with an alternative presentation of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
(see Subsection
2.2
below for the case
X=\Spec(k)
, and
[
Ter24b
, Thm. 1.12]
for the general statement).

The key tool to extend the functors obtained out of the universal property to a complete six functor formalism is provided by Beilinson’s gluing functors, which include unipotent nearby and vanishing cycles (see
[
IM24
, § 3]
).
Although these functors are typically not regarded as building blocks of the six functor formalism but rather as an outcome of the latter, they can be lifted to perverse Nori motives by direct application of the universal property:

Proposition 2.6

(
[
IM24
, § 3.5]
,
[
Ter24b
, § 1]
)
.

The abelian categories
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
are endowed with Beilinson’s gluing functors, and they satisfy Beilinson’s perverse gluing formalism.

The gluing functors play a crucial role in at least three steps of
[
IM24
]
:
showing that inverse images under closed immersions exist,
constructing the connection isomorphisms for composition of inverse image functors, and showing that direct images under general morphisms exist.
The perverse gluing formalism for Nori motives, as stated in
[
Ter24b
, Prop. 1.7]
, is used to show that the aforementioned alternative presentation of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
really defines the same category.

2.2
Nori’s motivic Galois group

By
[
IM24
, Prop. 2.11]
, when
X=\Spec(k)
Ivorra–Morel’s definition of perverse Nori motives recovers the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
of Nori motives.
This category was originally introduced by Nori in
[
Fak00
]
:
by design, it is the finest possible
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category computing the singular cohomology groups of all pairs of
k
-varieties.
Adopting Ivorra–Morel’s definition, we obtain a factorization of the homological functor
H^{0}\circ\Bti_{k}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
as

H^{0}\circ\Bti_{k}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)\xrightarrow{h_{k}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)\xrightarrow{\iota_{k}}\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Here, we identify
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(\Spec(k))
with
\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
as in Example
1.32
.
Note that the forgetful functor

\iota_{k}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}

sends non-zero objects to non-zero objects, being faithful and exact.

Example 2.7
.

The simplest non-zero Nori motive is the cohomology of the point

\mathbb{Q}_{k}:=h_{k}^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{k})\in\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k),

called the
unit motive
.
Note that the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
\End_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}_{k})
has dimension
1
:
it cannot be
0
(since
\mathbb{Q}_{k}
is non-zero) nor bigger than
1
(since the underlying vector space
\iota_{k}(\mathbb{Q}_{k})=\mathbb{Q}
is
1
-dimensional).
∎

Example 2.8
.

More interesting Nori motives arise from the cohomology of zero-dimensional
k
-varieties.
For instance, let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite field extension, and write
e\colon\Spec(k^{\prime})\to\Spec(k)
for the corresponding finite étale morphism.
Consider the object

\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}:=e_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}=e_{*}e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k},

usually called an
Artin motive
.
The underlying
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
\iota_{k}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})=\Bti_{k}^{*}(e_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}})
is the
0
-th cohomology group
H^{0}(\Spec(k^{\prime})^{\sigma};\mathbb{Q})
.
The complex-analytic space
\Spec(k^{\prime})^{\sigma}
consists of
[k^{\prime}\colon k]
points, indexed by the set
\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
of complex embeddings of
k^{\prime}
extending
\sigma
.
Hence, its cohomology can be naturally identified with
\mathbb{Q}^{\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}
.

We claim that, if
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois, the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\End_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})
is canonically isomorphic to the group algebra
\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}]
.
To see this, consider the group homomorphism

\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}\to\Aut_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})

sending an element
t\in\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
to the composite isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)

\alpha_{\phi}\colon e_{*}e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k}\xrightarrow{\eta}e_{*}f_{*}f^{*}e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k}=(ef)_{*}(ef)^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k}=e_{*}e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k},

where
f
denotes the automorphism of
\Spec(k^{\prime})
induced by
t^{-1}
.
Under the faithful exact functor
\iota_{k}
, this matches with the right
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
-action on
H^{0}(\Spec(k^{\prime})^{\sigma};\mathbb{Q})
induced by the left action

\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)\times\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})\to\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C}),\quad(r,\sigma^{\prime})\mapsto\sigma^{\prime}\circ r^{-1}.

In order to check that the induced
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra homomorphism

\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}]\to\End_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})

is an isomorphism, the trick is to extend scalars to
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
and use the factorization of the forgetful functor

\iota_{k,\ell}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}

through the category of
\ell
-adic
\textup{Gal}(\bar{k}/k)
-representations
(see
[
FJ
, Lem. 5.2.9]
for a detailed argument).

Although the choice to work with the opposite group
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}
in place of
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
seems quite unnatural here, we adopted this convention is order to get a cleaner result in Example
2.10
below.
∎

The main structural result about
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
is the existence of a canonical tensor product making it a neutral Tannakian category over
\mathbb{Q}
with fibre functor the forgetful functor
\iota_{k}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
;
the unit for this monoidal structure is precisely the unit motive
\mathbb{Q}_{k}
of Example
2.7
.
The monoidal structure on
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
, constructed by Nori, represented one of the most interesting features of his theory (see
[
HM17
, Thm. 9.3.4, Thm. 9.3.10]
).
From the perspective of perverse Nori motives, this result looks like a particular case of Theorem
2.3
,
but this is quite deceitful:
indeed, the geometric insights behind Nori’s original proof play a crucial role also in the identification of Nori’s category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
with Ivorra–Morel’s category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(\Spec(k))
.

For future reference, let us briefly comment on the tensor structure of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
in terms of Ivorra–Morel’s definition.
The homological functor
H^{0}\circ\Bti_{k}^{*}
is not monoidal, since
\Bti_{k}^{*}
is monoidal but
H^{0}
is not.
This prevents one from defining the tensor product on
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
by the general lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations.
As a workaround, one is led to consider the full additive subcategory

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)^{0}:=\left\{A\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)\;|\;\Bti_{k}^{*}(A)\in\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}\right\}\subset\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k),

which is nothing but the candidate for the heart of the conjectural motivic
t
-structure.
By design, the restriction of
H^{0}\circ\Bti_{k}^{*}
to
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)^{0}
reduces to
\Bti_{k}^{*}
and so is monoidal.
Hence, the associated universal abelian factorization
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)^{0}
inherits a well-defined tensor product.
Now the problem becomes to show that the natural faithful exact functor

\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)^{0}\to\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)

is in fact an equivalence.
Nori’s geometric insights play a major role in the proof of this fact.

Notation 2.9
.

We let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
denote the Tannaka dual of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
with respect to the fibre functor
\iota_{k}
, and we call it
Nori’s motivic Galois group
of
k
.

In concrete situations, one is typically interested in the Tannakian subcategory
\langle M\rangle^{\otimes}
of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
generated by a single motive
M
:
as usual, by this we mean the smallest full abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
containing
M
and stable under subquotients, tensor products, and duals.
By
[
DM82
, Prop. 2.21(a)]
, the inclusion of
\langle M\rangle^{\otimes}
into
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
induces a surjection of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
onto the Tannaka dual of
\langle M\rangle^{\otimes}
.
The situation is particularly simple in the case of Artin motives:

Example 2.10
.

Let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite Galois extension, and consider the Artin motive
\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}
from Example
2.8
.
By
[
HM17
, §§ 9.4-9.5]
, the Tannakian subcategory
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\rangle^{\otimes}\subset\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
is equivalent to
\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}}(\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k))
, with
the generator
\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}
corresponding to the regular representation;
this equivalence depends on the choice of an element
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
.
This determines a surjection between Tannaka dual groups

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)\twoheadrightarrow\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k),

(15)

where
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
is regarded as a finite algebraic group over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Following
[
HM17
, Cor. 8.1.17]
, the starting point is to compute the centralizer
E
of
\End_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})
inside
\End_{\mathbb{Q}}(\iota_{k}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}))
.
To this end, consider the canonical
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra isomorphism

\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}]\xrightarrow{\sim}\End_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})

obtained in Example
2.8
.
The vector space
\iota_{k}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})=H^{0}(\Spec(k^{\prime})^{\sigma};\mathbb{Q})
is a free
\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}]
-module of rank
1
(because the
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
-action on
\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
is simply transitive).
This implies that
E
is isomorphic to the opposite algebra
\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}]^{op}=\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)]
:
more precisely, the choice of an element
\sigma^{\prime}_{0}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
induces a
\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}]
-module isomorphism

\iota_{k}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}],

and the centralizer for the left-multiplication action of
\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)^{op}]
on itself is canonically isomorphic to the opposite algebra.
From now on, fix such an element
\sigma^{\prime}_{0}
.

Consider the linear dual
E^{\lor}
with its natural bialgebra structure.
The isomorphism
E\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)]
defined by
\sigma^{\prime}_{0}
induces an isomorphism

\Spec(E^{\lor})\simeq\Spec(\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)]^{\lor})=\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k).

Note that the forgetful functor
\iota_{k}\colon\langle\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\rangle^{\otimes}\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
factors through a monoidal functor

\langle\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\rangle^{\otimes}\to\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Spec(E^{\lor}))

sending the generator
\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}
to the regular representation
\mathbb{Q}^{\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}
.
We claim that the latter functor is an equivalence.
Since the abelian category
\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}}(\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k))
is a semisimple, this is the case as soon as the map

\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}^{\otimes n_{1}},\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}^{\otimes n_{2}})\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)}((\mathbb{Q}^{\Hom_{\sigma}(k,\mathbb{C})})^{\otimes n_{1}},(\mathbb{Q}^{\Hom_{\sigma}(k,\mathbb{C})})^{\otimes n_{2}})

is bijective for all
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In the case when
n_{1}=1=n_{2}
, the computation in Example
2.8
shows that this condition is indeed satisfied.
In order to conclude that the same holds in general, it suffices to prove the formula

\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}^{\otimes 2}\simeq\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}^{\oplus[k^{\prime}\colon k]}.

To this end, start from the canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}

\displaystyle:=e_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}

\displaystyle=e_{*}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}\otimes e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})

(by the projection formula)

\displaystyle=e_{*}e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}.

Now form the Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(k^{\prime}\otimes_{k}k^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(k^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(k^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(k),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

and regard
\Spec(k^{\prime}\otimes_{k}k^{\prime})
as a
k^{\prime}
-variety via the second projection
e^{\prime}_{2}
.
Since
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois, the scheme
\Spec(k^{\prime}\otimes_{k}k^{\prime})
decomposes as the disjoint union of
[k^{\prime}\colon k]
copies of
\Spec(k^{\prime})
, each of which maps isomorphically to
\Spec(k^{\prime})
via the first projection
e^{\prime}_{1}
.
Hence, we have an isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(\Spec(k^{\prime}))

e_{1,*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}\otimes_{k}k^{\prime}}\simeq\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}^{\oplus[k^{\prime}\colon k]}.

(16)

We deduce the isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(\Spec(k^{\prime}))

\displaystyle e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}

\displaystyle=e^{*}e_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=e^{\prime}_{1,*}{e^{\prime}_{2}}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}

(by proper base-change)

\displaystyle=e^{\prime}_{1,*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}\otimes_{k}k^{\prime}}

(as
{e^{\prime}_{2}}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}=\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}\otimes_{k}k^{\prime}}
)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}^{\oplus[k^{\prime}\colon k]},

(by (
16
))

which implies the sought-after formula.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

We have defined two distinct
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
-actions on the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
\iota_{k}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})
:
one determined by the
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
-action on the motive
\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}
(in Example
2.8
), and one defined by Tannakian formalism (in Example
2.10
).
Recall that the first action is canonical, whereas the second one depends on the choice of an element
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
.
Under the isomorphism between
\iota_{k}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})=\mathbb{Q}^{\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)}
determined by such a choice, they are induced by the left-multiplication and the right-multiplication actions of
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
on
\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)]
, respectively.
In particular, they commute with one another.

The above discussion leads one to study the difference between the motivic Galois groups of
k
and
k^{\prime}
in terms of classical Galois theory.
This requires some care, since the natural way to define the motivic Galois group of
k^{\prime}
is by regarding
k^{\prime}
rather than
k
as the base field of the theory.

Notation 2.12
.

For sake of clarity, we write
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X)
in place of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
when we want to stress its dependence on the base field
k
(and on the chosen complex embedding
\sigma
).

Until the end of the present subsection, let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite extension, and fix a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
X
be a
k
-variety;
set
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
, and write
e_{X}\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
for the corresponding finite étale morphism.
Then:

1.

The abelian categories
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
(defined by regarding
X^{\prime}
as a
k^{\prime}
-variety or as a
k
-variety, respectively) are canonically equivalent.

2.

If
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois, the inverse image functor
e_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
induces a canonical equivalence

\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)},

where the action of
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
on
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
is induced by its action on
X^{\prime}
via
k
-automorphisms.

Proof.

The first statement is a particular case of
[
IM24
, Prop. 6.11]

(see also
[
Ter24b
, Prop. 1.15]
for a related result on the level of generic points).

The second statement follows formally from the fact that perverse Nori motives form a stack for the étale topology, as noted in
[
IM24
, Prop. 2.7]
:
it suffices to repeat the argument for Lemma
1.27
(2) word-by-word.
∎

For the moment, we are interested in using this result only in the case when
X=\Spec(k)
.
As part of the structure of a six functor formalism, the inverse image functor

e^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)\to\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k^{\prime})

is canonically monoidal.
As a consequence of Remark
1.1
, the direct image functor

e_{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k^{\prime})\to\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)

is both left adjoint and right adjoint to
e^{*}
, and the unit natural transformations

\eta\colon M\to e_{*}e^{*}M,\qquad\eta\colon M^{\prime}\to e^{*}e_{*}M^{\prime}

are both split monomorphisms (see also
[
IM24
, §§ 2.4-2.5]
).
By Lemma
1.6
, the diagram of monoidal functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{\sigma,k}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{*}}

commutes up to monoidal natural isomorphism.
Composing with
H^{0}\colon\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and passing to the universal abelian factorizations, we deduce that the diagram of monoidal functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k^{\prime}}}

commutes up to monoidal natural isomorphism as well.
Thus, we get a homomorphism of Tannaka dual groups

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k).

(17)

The difference between the two sides is indeed measured by classical Galois theory:

Proposition 2.14
.

If
k^{\prime}/k
is a Galois extension, the sequence of pro-algebraic groups

1\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k^{\prime})\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)\to\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)\to 1

(18)

defined by the homomorphisms (
15
) and (
17
) is exact.

Proof.

The analogous statement for the algebraic closure
\bar{k}
in place of
k^{\prime}
is
[
HM17
, Thm. 9.1.16]
;
the proof of that statement (see
[
HM17
, § 9.5]
) contains all the ingredients to prove the version that we have stated.
Since that argument there is written down in terms of Nori’s definition of Nori motives, let us spell it out here in terms of Ivorra–Morel’s definition:
we have to show that the homomorphism
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
is an observable closed immersion and that its image coincides with the kernel of the projection to
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
.

The first property amounts to saying that every object
M^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k^{\prime})
is a subobject of some object in the image of
e^{*}
(see
[
DE22
, Cor. A.10]
).
To check that this condition is satisfied, one can use the unit monomorphism
\eta\colon M^{\prime}\hookrightarrow e^{*}e_{*}M^{\prime}
.
The assumption that
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois does not play any role here.

The second property amounts to saying that the Tannakian subcategory
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\rangle^{\otimes}\subset\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
contains exactly those objects of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
whose underlying
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k^{\prime})
-representation is trivial (see
[
DE22
, Prop. A.13]
).
In one direction, it suffices to show that the object
e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k^{\prime})
is a trivial
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k^{\prime})
-representation, which follows from the isomorphism

e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\simeq\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}^{\oplus[k^{\prime}\colon k]}

established at the end of Example
2.10
.
In the other direction, if an object
M\in\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
satisfies
e^{*}M\simeq\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}^{\oplus m}
for some
m\geq 0
, then the object
e_{*}e^{*}M
satisfies

e_{*}e^{*}M\simeq e_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}}^{\oplus m}=\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}^{\oplus m}

and thus belongs to
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\rangle^{\otimes}
.
Using the unit monomorphism
\eta\colon M\hookrightarrow e_{*}e^{*}M
, we deduce that
M
belongs to
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\rangle^{\otimes}
as well.
∎

2.3
Motivic local systems

In this subsection, we discuss the Tannakian aspects of the theory of Nori motives over general smooth
k
-varieties.
Just like perverse Nori motives are modelled on perverse sheaves, motivic local systems are modelled on shifted local systems.
Throughout, we let
X
be a smooth, quasi-projective
k
-variety.

Notation 2.15
.

•

We say that an object
M\in\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
is a
motivic local system
if the perverse sheaf
\iota_{X}(M)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
belongs to
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
.
We let
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
denote the full subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
spanned by the motivic local systems.

•

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
denote the smallest abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
containing the essential image of the forgetful functor
\iota_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
and stable under subquotients.

Recall that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
is stable under subquotients and extensions inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
(by Lemma
1.29
).
This implies that
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
is stable under subquotients and extensions inside
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
.
Moreover, it implies that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
is stable under subquotients inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
(see Notation
2.1
).
In fact, there is more to be said:

Proposition 2.16
.

The abelian category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
is stable under extensions inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
.

For the proof, we need to understand the behaviour of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
with respect to finite extensions of the base field
k
, along the lines of Lemma
1.33
.
Note that the inclusion
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
restricts to an inclusion
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite field extension;
set
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
, and write
e_{X}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
for the corresponding finite étale morphism.
Choose a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma
.
Then there is a canonical equivalence

\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X^{\prime}).

Proof.

By construction, the homologous equivalences of Lemma
1.27
(1) and Lemma
2.13
(1) fit into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Restricting to local systems, we get the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X^{\prime}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the horizontal arrows are still equivalences (as noted in Lemma
1.33
(1) for the lower one, and in Lemma
2.22
(1) below for the upper one).
Since the forgetful functors
\iota_{X}
and
\iota_{X^{\prime}}
are exact, this formally implies the thesis.
∎

Unfortunately, at this stage we are not able to prove the analogue of Lemma
1.33
(2), since it is not clear from the definition that the étale prestack
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
is in fact a stack.

Proof of Proposition
2.16
.

Of course, we may assume
X
non-empty.
Up to considering each connected component of
X
separately, we may assume
X
connected.

First, we reduce to the case when
X
is geometrically connected over
k
.
To this end, let
k(X)
denote the function field of
X
, and let
k^{\prime}
be the algebraic closure of
k
inside
k(X)
.
Note that, since
X
is normal (being smooth over
k
), all elements of
k^{\prime}
are regular functions on
X
.
By construction, the connected components of the smooth
k^{\prime}
-variety
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
are geometrically connected over
k^{\prime}
.
Hence, up to replacing the
k^{\prime}
-variety
X
by the
k
-variety
X^{\prime}
(using Lemma
2.17
) and the latter by its single connected components, we may assume
X
geometrically connected over
k
.

Next, we further reduce to the case when the geometrically connected
k
-variety
X
admits a
k
-rational point.
To this end, fix a closed point
x\in X(\bar{k})
, and let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite extension such that
x\in X(k^{\prime})
.
Note that the
k^{\prime}
-variety
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
is geometrically connected over
k^{\prime}
and has a canonical
k^{\prime}
-rational point
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(k^{\prime})
lying over
x
.
Up to replacing
X
by
X^{\prime}
(using again Lemma
2.17
), we may assume that
X
has already a
k
-rational point.

Finally, in this situation, the result is proved in
[
Jac25
, Thm. 7.7(1)]
.
∎

Recall that the extension-stability of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
is still unclear at this point, and represents in fact the only possible obstruction for the inclusion
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
to be an equivalence.
Unfortunately, knowing the extension-stability of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
does not imply formally that they coincide:
the issue is that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
is not defined as the intersection
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
.

Until the end of the present subsection, we assume the smooth
k
-variety
X
to be geometrically connected over
k
, say of dimension
d
, and
we let
a_{X}\colon X\to\Spec(k)
denote the structural morphism.
By
[
Ter24b
, Thm. 6.2]
, the shifted tensor product

M_{1}\otimes^{\dagger}M_{2}:=(M_{1}[-d]\otimes M_{2}[-d])[d]

turns
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
into a neutral Tannakian category over
\mathbb{Q}
with unit object given by the shifted unit motive
{{}^{p}\mathbb{Q}_{X}}:=\mathbb{Q}_{X}[d]
.
By construction, the forgetful functor

\iota_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)

is canonically monoidal.
Therefore, for every closed point
x\in X(\bar{k})
, the composite

\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\xrightarrow{\iota_{X}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)\xrightarrow{x^{*}[-d]}\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}

defines a fibre functor for
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
.

Notation 2.18
.

Let
x\in X(\bar{k})
be a closed point.

•

We let
\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(X,x)
denote the Tannaka dual of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
with respect to the fibre functor at
x
.

•

We let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)
denote the Tannaka dual of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
with respect to the fibre functor at
x
, and we call it
Nori’s motivic Galois group
of
X
with base-point
x
.

Remark 2.19
.

The shifted inverse image functor

a_{X}^{*}[d]\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)\to\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)

takes values in
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
and admits a right adjoint

{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ a_{X,*}[-d]\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k).

As for usual local systems, the functor
a_{X}^{*}[d]
is fully faithful, with essential image stable under subquotients.
By
[
DM82
, Prop. 2.21(a)]
, this determines a canonical surjection between Tannaka dual groups

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k).

(19)

To check the above properties of
a_{X}^{*}[d]
, it suffices to translate them in terms of unit and co-unit morphisms, as in Remark
1.30
:
since the forgetful functors
\iota_{k}
and
\iota_{X}
are conservative, the invertibility of the relevant natural transformations in the motivic setting follows from the invertibility of the corresponding natural transformations in the topological setting.

Recall that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
is defined as the full abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
generated by the image of the forgetful functor
\iota_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
under subquotients.
By the general theory of Tannakian categories,
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}
is stable under tensor products and duals inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
(see the proof of
[
Jac25
, Lem. 2.13]
), hence a Tannakian subcategory.
By construction, the homomorphism of Tannaka duals

\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(X,x)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)

(20)

induced by the forgetful functor
\iota_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
is a closed immersion (see
[
DM82
, Prop. 2.21(b)]
).
This suggests that part of the complexity of the Tannakian category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
comes from the complexity of
X
as an algebraic variety;
both the topological complexity of
X^{\sigma}
and the algebraic complexity of
k
contribute to the latter.
This can be seen clearly in the following generalization of Example
2.10
:

Example 2.20
.

Let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite Galois extension;
set
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
, and write
e_{X}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
for the corresponding finite étale morphism.
Since
X
is assumed to be geometrically connected,
e_{X}
is a Galois covering and the canonical homomorphism
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)\to\textup{Gal}(X^{\prime}/X)
is bijective.
Consider the motivic local system

{{}^{p}\mathbb{Q}}_{X^{\prime}/X}:=e_{X,*}{{}^{p}\mathbb{Q}}_{X^{\prime}}=e_{X,*}e_{X}^{*}{{}^{p}\mathbb{Q}}_{X}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X),

which we may call an
Artin motivic local system
.
We claim that the Tannakian subcategory
\langle{{}^{p}\mathbb{Q}}_{X^{\prime}/X}\rangle^{\otimes}\subset\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
is canonically equivalent to
\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}}(\textup{Gal}(X^{\prime}/X))
, with the generator
{{}^{p}\mathbb{Q}}_{X^{\prime}/X}
corresponding to the regular representation.
For any closed point
x\in X(\bar{k})
, this determines a surjection between Tannaka dual groups

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)\twoheadrightarrow\textup{Gal}(X^{\prime}/X).

(21)

To prove the claim, consider the canonical
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra homomorphism

\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(X^{\prime}/X)]\to\End_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)}({{}^{p}\mathbb{Q}}_{X^{\prime}/X})

obtained as in Example
2.10
.
The key point is to show that this is bijective;
the claim then follows by repeating the argument of Example
2.10
in the relative setting.
Note that the above
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra homomorphism fits into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\End_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(X^{\prime}/X)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\End_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)}({{}^{p}\mathbb{Q}}_{X^{\prime}/X}),}

where the right-most vertical arrow is induced by the shifted inverse image functor
a_{X}^{*}[d]
.
Since the upper horizontal arrow and the right-most vertical arrow are already known to be bijective (by Example
2.8
and Remark
2.19
, respectively), the same must hold for the lower horizontal arrow.
This proves the claim.
∎

As in Subsection
2.2
, this discussion leads us to study the difference between the motivic Galois groups of
X
and of
X^{\prime}
via classical Galois theory.
Note that
X^{\prime}
is geometrically connected over
k^{\prime}
but not over
k
.
Hence, in order to define the motivic Galois group of
X^{\prime}
, we need to regard
X^{\prime}
as a
k^{\prime}
-variety rather than as a
k
-variety.

Notation 2.21
.

Following Notation
2.12
, we write
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma}(X)
in place of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
when we want to stress its dependence on the base field
k
(and on the chosen complex embedding
\sigma
).

Fix a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma
.
Lemma
2.13
admits the following variant for motivic local systems:

Lemma 2.22
.

Keep the notation and assumptions of Lemma
2.13
, and assume
X
smooth over
k
.
Then:

1.

The abelian categories
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
(defined by regarding
X^{\prime}
as a
k^{\prime}
-variety or as a
k
-variety, respectively) are canonically equivalent.

2.

If
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois, the inverse image functor
e_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
induces a canonical equivalence

\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)},

where the action of
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
on
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma}(X^{\prime})
is induced by its action on
X^{\prime}
via
k
-automorphisms.

Proof.

The two statements follow from the corresponding statements of Lemma
2.13
.
∎

By Lemma
1.6
, the diagram of monoidal functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{X}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{\sigma,X}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},X^{\prime}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes up to monoidal natural isomorphism.
Composing with the
0
-th perverse truncation, passing to the universal abelian factorizations, and restricting to motivic local systems, we deduce that the diagram of monoidal functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{X}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes up to monoidal natural isomorphism.
This yields a canonical homomorphism between Tannaka dual groups

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x).

(22)

We have the following generalization of Proposition
2.14
:

Proposition 2.23
.

Keep the notation and assumptions of Example
2.20
.
The sequence of pro-algebraic groups

1\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)\to\textup{Gal}(X^{\prime}/X)\to 1

defined by the homomorphisms (
21
) and (
22
) is exact.

Proof.

It suffices to rewrite the proof of Proposition
2.14
with
X
in place of
\Spec(k)
, with Example
2.20
playing here the same role as Example
2.10
there;
we leave the details to the interested reader.
∎

We can finally state the main result of this subsection:

Theorem 2.24
.

Let
X
be a smooth, geometrically connected, quasi-projective
k
-variety.
Then, for every closed point
x\in X(\bar{k})
, the sequence of pro-algebraic groups

1\rightarrow\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(X,x)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)\rightarrow 1

defined by the homomorphisms (
20
) and (
19
) is exact.

Proof.

We divide the argument into two main steps.

Assume first that the base-point
x\in X(\bar{k})
is in fact a
k
-rational point
x\in X(k)
.
In this case, by
[
Jac25
, Thm. 7.7(1)]
, the sequence in the statement is even split-exact;
the section
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)
is induced by the section
x\colon\Spec(k)\rightarrow X
to the structural morphism.

In the general case, fix a finite Galois extension
k^{\prime}/k
such that
x\in X(k^{\prime})
;
as usual, set
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
, and write
e_{X}\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
for the corresponding finite Galois covering.
The
k^{\prime}
-variety
X^{\prime}
is still smooth and geometrically connected, and it has a canonical point
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(k^{\prime})
over
x
.
Choose a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma
.
The monoidal equivalence
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\sigma^{\prime}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X^{\prime})
(given by Lemma
2.17
) translates into an isomorphism of Tannaka duals

\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(X,x).

All in all, we get a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\textup{geo}}(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X^{\prime},x^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\textup{geo}}(X,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Gal}(X^{\prime}/X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where both columns are already known to be exact (the right-most one by Proposition
2.14
, the middle one by Proposition
2.23
).
Since the upper row is also known to be exact (by the previous step of the proof), the exactness of the middle row follows formally by diagram-chasing.
This concludes the proof.
∎

3
The fundamental sequence in Voevodsky’s setting

In this section, we describe an alternative construction of Tannakian categories of motivic local systems, based on Ayoub’s theory of sheaves of geometric origin.
Our guiding principle is that, for any reasonable notion of motivic local system over a smooth
k
-variety, the associated Tannaka dual should fit into a fundamental sequence like the one of Theorem
2.24
.
The fundamental sequence constructed in the present section uses Ayoub’s motivic Galois group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
in place of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
.

After reviewing the construction of Ayoub’s group and its main properties, we discuss Ayoub’s presentation of the motivic Galois group as a group of symmetries of constructible complexes of geometric origin.
We show that the resulting categories of equivariant local systems behave like Ivorra–Morel’s motivic local systems, and in particular, satisfy the analogous fundamental sequence:
this is Theorem
3.18
below.
The main constructions and results of this section rely heavily on the general formalism of equivariant Tannakian categories, summarized in Appendix
A
.

As in the previous section, we fix a complex embedding
\sigma\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

3.1
Ayoub’s motivic Galois group

Ayoub’s construction of the motivic Galois group,
worked out in
[
Ayo14
,
Ayo14a
,
Ayo17
]
,
exploits the Betti realization

\Bti_{k}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(k)\to D(\mathbb{Q})

and, specifically, the associated Betti algebra

\mathcal{B}_{k}:=\Bti_{k,*}\mathbb{Q}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(k)
.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
Ayo14
, Thm. 1.45, Defn. 2.9, Cor. 2.105]
)
.

The complex

\mathcal{H}_{k}:=\Bti_{k}^{*}\mathcal{B}_{k}=\Bti_{k}^{*}\Bti_{k,*}\mathbb{Q}\in D(\mathbb{Q})

is naturally a commutative Hopf algebra object, and its cohomology is concentrated in non-positive degrees.

The object
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is called the
motivic Hopf algebra
:
it inherits the algebra structure from the Betti algebra, whereas, to define the co-algebra structure, one also needs to use the canonical monoidal section

s_{k}\colon D(\mathbb{Q})\to\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(k)

sending a complex in
D(\mathbb{Q})
to the associated constant motive.
While
[
Ayo14
]
defines
\mathcal{H}_{k}
as a Hopf algebra object in the derived category
D(\mathbb{Q})
, the method of
[
Ayo17
]
promotes it canonically to a homotopy Hopf algebra, thereby solving the potential technical issues related to the lack of functoriality of triangulated categories.
The latter description of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
can be promoted to the
\infty
-categorical level, as done in
[
Ayo21
, § 1.3]
.

The property that the cohomology of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
vanishes in positive degrees is a highly non-formal result.
It implies the following:

Corollary 3.2
.

The
0
-th cohomology object

\mathcal{H}_{k}^{0}:=H^{0}(\mathcal{H}_{k})

is naturally a classical commutative Hopf algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
.

By
[
Ayo17
, Thm. 8.3]
, the Betti realization over
k
factors canonically as a composite of triangulated functors of the form

\Bti_{k}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(k)\to\mathsf{coMod}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}(D(\mathbb{Q}))\xrightarrow{\textup{for}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}}D(\mathbb{Q}),

where the triangulated structure of
\mathsf{coMod}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}(D(\mathbb{Q}))
is induced by the higher structure of the homotopy Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
By
[
Ayo14
, Prop. 1.55]
, the following universal property holds:
given a commutative bialgebra object
\mathcal{K}\in D(\mathbb{Q})
and a monoidal functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(k)\rightarrow\mathsf{coMod}_{\mathcal{K}}(D(\mathbb{Q}))

making both halves of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{coMod}_{\mathcal{K}}(D(\mathbb{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\mathbb{Q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{k}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{for}_{\mathcal{K}}}

commute up to monoidal natural isomorphism, there exists a unique bialgebra morphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{H}_{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
in
D(\mathbb{Q})
making both halves of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{coMod}_{\mathcal{K}}(D(\mathbb{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{coMod}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}(D(\mathbb{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\mathbb{Q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{for}_{\mathcal{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{for}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}

commute up to the induced monoidal natural isomorphism.

Notation 3.3
.

•

We let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
denote the spectrum of the derived Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}_{k}
in the sense of spectral algebraic geometry.

•

We let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
denote the spectrum of the classical Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{0}
, and we call it
Ayoub’s motivic Galois group
of
k
;
we write it as
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma)
if we want to stress its dependence on
\sigma
.

The morphism of derived Hopf algebras
\mathcal{H}_{k}\to\mathcal{H}_{k}^{0}
induces a canonical homomorphism of spectral groups

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)

with the property that the homomorphism on
\Lambda
-valued points

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)(\Lambda)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)(\Lambda)

is bijective whenever
\Lambda
is a classical
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra.

Remark 3.4
.

In fact, the spectral group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
is expected to be already classical,
hence equal to
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
:
in other words,
the complex
\mathcal{H}_{k}\in D(\mathbb{Q})

is expected to be already concentrated in degree
0
.
This would follow from
the existence of the motivic
t
-structure on
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)

(see
[
Ayo17a
, Prop. 3.2.9]
).

Even if the spectral group is the most natural object
to work with in the setting of Voevodsky motives,
in this paper we only use its classical counterpart.
We need to discuss some basic properties of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
,
some of which are only stated for
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
in the literature:
in particular, we need to study the behaviour of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
under finite extensions of the base field
k
, as done for Nori’s motivic Galois group in Section
2.2
.

So let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite extension, and write
e\colon\Spec(k^{\prime})\to\Spec(k)
for the corresponding finite étale morphism.
Choose a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma
.
Using Lemma
1.6
, we obtain a canonical morphism of motivic Hopf algebras

\mathcal{H}_{\sigma,k}:=\Bti_{\sigma,k}^{*}\Bti_{\sigma,k,*}\mathbb{Q}=\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{*}e^{*}e_{*}\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},k^{\prime},*}\mathbb{Q}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{*}\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},k^{\prime},*}\mathbb{Q}=:\mathcal{H}_{\sigma^{\prime},k^{\prime}}.

Taking the
0
-th cohomology objects and passing to the associated spectra, this defines a homomorphism

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k^{\prime})\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k).

(23)

Moreover, we have a canonical commutative algebra morphism in
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(k)

\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}:=e_{*}e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k}\xrightarrow{\eta}e_{*}\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},k^{\prime},*}\Bti_{\sigma^{\prime},k^{\prime}}^{*}e^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k}=\Bti_{\sigma,k,*}\Bti_{\sigma,k}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k}=\mathcal{B}_{k}.

Note that the complex
\Bti_{\sigma,k}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k}\in D(\mathbb{Q})
is concentrated in degree
0
, where it coincides with the cohomology group
H^{0}(\Spec(k^{\prime})^{\sigma};\mathbb{Q})
.
Recall that the latter can be identified with
\mathbb{Q}^{\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}
, where
\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
denotes the set of complex embeddings of
k^{\prime}
extending
\sigma
.

Suppose now that the extension
k^{\prime}/k
is Galois.
The choice of an element in
\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
defines a bijection between
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
and
\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
, hence induces an isomorphism between
\mathbb{Q}^{\Hom_{\sigma}(k^{\prime},\mathbb{C})}
and the dual group algebra
\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)]^{\lor}=\mathbb{Q}^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)}
.
If one chooses the same
\sigma^{\prime}
as above, the induced morphism of commutative algebras in
D(\mathbb{Q})

\mathbb{Q}[\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)]^{\lor}=\Bti_{\sigma,k}^{*}(\mathbb{Q}_{k^{\prime}/k})\to\Bti_{\sigma,k}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{k})=:\mathcal{H}_{k}

is in fact a morphism of commutative Hopf algebras.
Taking the
0
-th cohomology objects and passing to the associated spectra, we get a homomorphism

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)\to\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k).

(24)

We have the following analogue of Proposition
2.14
:

Proposition 3.5

(
[
Ayo21
, Lem. 1.4.9]
)
.

If
k^{\prime}/k
is a Galois extension, the sequence of pro-algebraic groups

1\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k^{\prime})\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)\to\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)\to 1

defined by the homomorphisms (
23
) and (
24
) is exact.

Proof.

The argument of
[
Ayo21
, Lem. 1.4.9]
establishes the exactness of the analogous sequence of spectral motivic Galois groups.
This immediately implies the version stated here, because the spectral group
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime}/k)
is already classical.
∎

The similarity with the behaviour of Nori’s motivic Galois group is by no means coincidental.
Indeed, an important result by Choudhury–Gallauer asserts that the pro-algebraic groups
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
are canonically isomorphic (see Theorem
5.8
below).
This result, which plays a crucial role in Subsection
5.2
, is implicitly used in Example
3.14
and in Example
3.15
below in order to apply results from Section
2
in Ayoub’s setting.
This operation is legitimate in view of Lemma
5.9
below, which implies that the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
-action on the underlying
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space of a Nori motive matches with the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)
-action defined by Tannakian formalism.
As the reader can check, the results of Subsection
5.2
(in particular, Lemma
5.9
) are logically independent of the new results collected in the present section, so there are no circular arguments.

3.2
The motivic Galois action on local systems

The stable
\infty
-categories of sheaves of geometric origin, introduced in Sections
1.2
and
1.3
, are closely related to the theory of Voevodsky motives.
The following result describes Ayoub’s motivic Galois group as the group of symmetries of these categories:

Theorem 3.6

(
[
Ayo21
, Thm. 2.2.3, Cor. 2.2.7]
)
.

The following hold:

1.

The spectral motivic Galois group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
can be naturally identified with the group of autoequivalences of the six functor formalism
X\mapsto D_{\textup{geo}}(X)
.

2.

Under the previous identification, the classical motivic Galois group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
can be identified with the subgroup of exact autoequivalences:
for every connective commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\Lambda
, an element
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)(\Lambda)
belongs to
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)(\Lambda)
if and only if, for every
k
-variety
X
, the triangulated functor

g\cdot-\colon D_{\textup{geo}}(X)_{\Lambda}\rightarrow D_{\textup{geo}}(X)_{\Lambda}

is
t
-exact with respect to the ordinary
t
-structure.

Here,
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
denotes the stable
\infty
-category of unbounded complexes of geometric origin (see
1.20
).
In the present paper, we are only interested in the motivic Galois action on
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
and subcategories thereof.

The construction of the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
-action on sheaves of geometric origin is based on Proposition
1.18
.
Indeed, by
[
Ayo21
, Thm. 1.3.21]
one can regard
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
as the spectral group parameterizing the self-equivalences of the Betti algebra
\mathcal{B}_{k}
, as defined in
[
Ayo21
, Defn. 1.3.13]
.
A self-equivalence of
\mathcal{B}_{k}
defines a self-equivalence of the Betti algebra
\mathcal{B}_{X}
(via Lemma
1.3
(2)), and the latter determines an autoequivalence of the stable
\infty
-category
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})
by transport of structure.
By construction, as
X
varies, these assemble into an autoequivalence of the six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
.
In order to deduce the first point of Theorem
3.6
, one has to show that this construction identifies autoequivalences of the six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})
with self-equivalences of
\mathcal{B}_{k}
:
this is essentially a consequence of Drew–Gallauer’s theorem (see Theorem
4.6
below).

In principle, Theorem
3.6
gives information about the
\Lambda
-valued points of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
whenever
\Lambda
is a connective commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra.
Since we are only interested in the action of the classical group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
, we may restrict our attention to classical
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra.
For notational convenience, we let
\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{Q}}
denote the category of classical commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras.

In this section we only need to consider the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
-action on local systems of geometric origin, regarded as perverse sheaves.
We start from the following observation:

Lemma 3.7
.

For every smooth
k
-variety
X
, the full subcategory
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathit{D}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
is
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
-stable.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
X
connected, say of dimension
d
.
In this situation, for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, the category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)_{\Lambda}
can be identified with the full subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)_{\Lambda}
spanned by the dualizable objects concentrated in degree
d
.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

In fact, Ayoub’s Theorem
3.6
admits a version tailored to local systems:
this is
[
Ayo21
, Thm. 4.4.2, Cor. 4.4.15]
, which identifies
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
with the autoequivalence group of the monoidal fibered category
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
.
For us, it is more convenient to use the full version from the beginning, since it allows us to use functoriality with respect to direct image functors without further comments.

We want to study the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
-action on local systems of geometric origin.
Let
X
be a smooth, geometrically connected
k
-variety of dimension
d
, with structural morphism
a_{X}\colon X\to\Spec(k)
.

Notation 3.9
.

We let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
denote the category of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic equivariant objects in the sense of Definition
A.22
,
and we write

\omega_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)

(25)

for the associated forgetful functor
\textup{for}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
(see Construction
A.4
).

Lemma 3.10
.

The category
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
is naturally neutral Tannakian over
\mathbb{Q}
, and the forgetful functor (
25
) is an exact tensor-functor.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
A.31
and Proposition
A.34
.
∎

Example 3.11
.

If
X=\Spec(k)
, the motivic Galois action is quite easy to understand.
As in Example
1.32
, let us identify
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(\Spec(k))
with
\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
as monoidal categories via the global sections functor.
Any monoidal action of a pro-algebraic group on
\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}

admits a trivialization (in the sense of Definition
A.12
), because it preserves the unit object and induces the identity map on its endomorphism ring.
Passing to the equivariant objects, we obtain a canonical equivalence of neutral Tannakian categories

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(\Spec(k))^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
