{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{coMod}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}(D(\mathbb{Q}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\mathbb{Q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nri_{k}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}}

commute up to monoidal natural isomorphism.
Since the complex
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k))\in D(\mathbb{Q})
is concentrated in degree
0
, the morphism (
36
) factors through
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{0}
.
This in turn determines a canonical homomorphism of pro-algebraic groups

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)=\Spec(\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)))\to\Spec(\mathcal{H}_{k}^{0})=:\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k).

(37)

We have the following comparison result by Choudhury–Gallauer:

Theorem 5.8

(
[
CG17
, Thm. 9.1]
)
.

The homomorphism (
37
) is an isomorphism.

Following
[
CG17
]
, one can construct the inverse isomorphism

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)

(38)

explicitly, using the universal property of Nori’s abelian category.
Let us explain this using Ivorra–Morel’s definition of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
.
Recall from Section
2.1
that, since the functor
H^{0}\circ\Bti_{k}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is not monoidal, the description of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
as the universal abelian factorization of this functor is not well-suited to constructing the monoidal structure.
Instead, following
[
Ter24b
, Thm. 1.12]
, one has to regard
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
as the universal abelian factorization of the monoidal functor
\Bti_{k}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)^{0}\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
The latter fits into the diagram of monoidal functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bti_{k}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}}

which commutes up to monoidal natural isomorphism.
Applying the universal property of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
, we obtain a canonical faithful exact monoidal functor

\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)\to\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k))

(39)

making the whole diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rep_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{for}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}}

commute up to monoidal natural isomorphism.
It is in this way that one gets the homomorphism (
38
).
The fact that this homomorphism is an isomorphism (by the proof of Theorem
5.8
) implies that the functor (
39
) is an equivalence.

We are now ready to address the comparison problem over fields:

Lemma 5.9
.

For every finite extension
k^{\prime}/k
, the triangulated functor

o_{\Spec(k^{\prime})}\colon\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(\Spec(k^{\prime})))\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(\Spec(k^{\prime})))=\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(\Spec(k^{\prime}))^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}

is an equivalence.

Proof.

We prove the equivalent statement that, for every finite extension
k^{\prime}/k
, the exact functor

o_{\Spec(k^{\prime})}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(\Spec(k^{\prime}))\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(\Spec(k^{\prime}))

is an equivalence.
Let us divide the argument into three steps.

First, assume that
k^{\prime}=k
.
In this case, in view of the above discussion, it suffices to make sure that the functor
o_{k}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)\to\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(k)
coincides with the functor (
39
) modulo the canonical equivalence of Example
3.11
.
To this end, since both functors are obtained by applying the universal property of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)
, it suffices to observe that the enriched Betti realization

\widetilde{\Bti}_{k}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)\to\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(\Spec(k))^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}

is nothing but the canonical monoidal functor
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\to\mathsf{comod}_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{0}}(\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{Q}))
defined by the motivic Hopf algebra.

Next, assume that
k^{\prime}/k
is a finite Galois extension.
Choose a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma
.
We have a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(\Spec(k^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}_{\sigma}(\Spec(k^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{\Spec(k^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{k^{\prime}}}

where the vertical arrows witness the equivalences of
Lemma
2.13
(1) and Lemma
4.13
(1).
Since the lower horizontal arrow is already known to be an equivalence (by the previous step, applied to
k^{\prime}
rather than to
k
), the upper horizontal arrow must be an equivalence as well.

Lastly, consider an arbitrary finite extension
k^{\prime}/k
.
Let
k^{\prime\prime}
denote the Galois closure of
k^{\prime}/k
inside
\bar{k}
.
The functor
o_{\Spec(k^{\prime\prime})}
is
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime\prime}/k)
-equivariant with respect to the
\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime\prime}/k)
-action on
\Spec(k^{\prime\prime})
by
k
-automorphisms (by Proposition
5.5
).
We have a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(\Spec(k^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(\Spec(k^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(\Spec(k^{\prime\prime}))^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime\prime}/k^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(\Spec(k^{\prime\prime}))^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime\prime}/k^{\prime})},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{\Spec(k^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{\Spec(k^{\prime\prime})}}

where the vertical equivalences are obtained by applying the obvious variants of Lemma
2.13
(2) and Lemma
3.16
(2) to the finite Galois extension
k^{\prime\prime}/k^{\prime}
(but still with respect to
\sigma
-analytification of
k
-varieties).
Since the lower horizontal arrow is already known to be an equivalence (by the previous step), the upper horizontal arrow must be an equivalence as well.
This concludes the proof.
∎

5.3
Comparison over function fields

In this technical subsection, as an intermediate step towards the full comparison theorem, we establish the following generic variant:

Proposition 5.10
.

For every (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
X
, the functor

\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(U))\to\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(U))

is an equivalence.

We stated this result is terms of triangulated categories in order to make its relation to Theorem
5.4
transparent.
However, its proof takes place at the level of abelian categories.
More precisely, we want to compare the categories of motivic local systems at the generic points of
k
-varieties, using the fundamental sequences of Theorem
2.24
and Theorem
3.18
.

We start by studying local systems of geometric origin at the generic points.
Recall that, for every
k
-variety
X
, we have an inclusion
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
(see Subsection
2.1
), which refines to an inclusion
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
if
X
is smooth (see Subsection
2.3
).
Passing to the colimit over the (smooth) dense open subsets of
X
, we get the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where all horizontal arrows are fully faithful exact functors (as filtered colimits of such).
Moreover, all vertical arrows are equivalences:
for the two right-most arrows, this follows from the fact that every perverse sheaf restricts to a local system over some smooth dense open subset;
for the left-most arrow, this follows from the analogous property of motivic local systems inside perverse Nori motives.

Lemma 5.11
.

For every (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
X
, the two functors

\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)\to\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U),\qquad\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)\to\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U)

are equivalences.

Proof.

As a consequence of the above discussion, the left-most functor is an equivalence if and only if so is the right-most functor;
since fully faithfulness is already known, it remains to show essential surjectivity.

For every
U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}
, the subcategory
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)
coincides with the smallest abelian subcategory containing
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)
and stable subquotients and extensions (by Corollary
1.26
).
This implies that
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U)
is generated by its abelian subcategory
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)
under subquotients and extensions inside
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)
.
Therefore, it suffices to check that
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)
is already stable under subquotients and extensions inside
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)
.
This is equivalent to checking that
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
is stable under subquotients and extensions inside
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(U)
.
And this follows formally from the fact that each abelian subcategory
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(U)
is already stable under subquotients (by definition) and under extensions (by Proposition
2.16
).
∎

Let us now focus on the behaviour of motivic local systems at the generic points.

Lemma 5.12
.

For every dense open immersion
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
between smooth (quasi-projective)
k
-varieties, the inverse image functors

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U),\quad j^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U),\quad j^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(U)

are fully faithful, with essential image stable under subquotients.

Proof.

For sake of simplicity, we write down the proof in the setting of local systems;
as the reader can easily check, the argument carries over to the motivic and equivariant settings.

Let us start by proving fully faithfulness.
For this, we are allowed to replace
U
by some smaller dense open subset
U^{\prime}
:
indeed, fully faithfulness with respect to the inclusions
U^{\prime}\subset X
and
U^{\prime}\subset U
implies fully faithfulness with respect to the original inclusion
U\subset X
.
Hence, up to replacing
U
by some dense affine open subset, we may assume
U
affine, in which case
j
is an affine open immersion.
This allows us to use the adjunctions

j_{!}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)\leftrightarrows\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)\colon j^{*},\qquad j^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)\leftrightarrows\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)\colon j_{*}

as well as the intermediate extension functor

j_{!*}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X),\quad K\mapsto\rm{im}\left\{j_{!}K\to j_{*}K\right\}.

For every object
L\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
, the co-unit morphism
\epsilon\colon j_{!}j^{*}L\to L
(resp. the unit morphism
\eta\colon L\to j_{*}j^{*}L
) is an epimorphism (resp. a monomorphism) in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
:
in particular,
L
is canonically isomorphic to
j_{!*}L
(see
[
BBD+18
, Lem. 4.3.2]
, and observe that the irreducibility assumption in the statement plays no role in the proof).
Therefore, the fully faithfulness of
j^{*}
on
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
follows formally from the fully faithfulness of
j_{!*}
.
To check the latter property, fix two objects
K_{1},K_{2}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)
, and consider the chain of maps

\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)}(K_{1},K_{2})\to\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)}(j_{!*}K_{1},j_{!*}K_{2})\to\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)}(j_{!*}K_{1},j_{*}K_{2})\to\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)}(j_{!}K_{1},j_{*}K_{2}).

Note that the second (resp. third) map is injective, since the morphism
j_{!*}K_{2}\to j_{*}K_{2}
(resp.
j_{!}K_{1}\to j_{!*}K_{1}
) is a monomorphism (resp. an epimorphism).
Since the composite of the chain is a bijection, with inverse given by the composite map

\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)}(j_{!}K_{1},j_{*}K_{2})=\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)}(K_{1},j^{*}j_{*}K_{2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(U)}(K_{1},K_{2}),

we conclude that all maps in the chain are in fact bijections.

Let us now prove stability under subquotients.
Since
j^{*}
is exact, it suffices to show that it is bijective on subobject lattices of local systems.
For this, we are allowed to replace
U
by some smaller dense open subset.
Thus, we may again assume
U
affine, in which case
j
is an affine open immersion.
In this situation, we claim that
j_{!*}
is a two-sided inverse of
j^{*}
on subobject lattices.
Since
j^{*}
is right-inverse to
j_{!*}
as a functor of perverse sheaves (being right-inverse to both
j_{!}
and
j_{*}
), it is also its right-inverse on subobject lattices.
To see that
j_{!*}
is right-inverse to
j^{*}
on subobject lattices of local systems, it suffices to observe that
j_{!*}
preserves monomorphisms (being a subfunctor of the exact functor
j_{*}
):
a given monomorphism
L^{\prime}\hookrightarrow L
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
gets identified with the monomorphism
j_{!*}j^{*}L^{\prime}\hookrightarrow j_{!*}j^{*}L
(using again
[
BBD+18
, Lem. 4.3.2]
).
∎

Construction 5.13
.

Let
X
be a smooth, geometrically connected (quasi-projective)
k
-variety with function field
k(X)
.
Following
[
BT25
, §§ 4.4-4.5]
, one can define a family of fibre functors for the Tannakian categories
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(U)
which are compatible with restriction along open immersions in
\mathsf{Op}_{X}
;
the usual fibre functor at a point
x\in X(\bar{k})
does not satisfy this property, since no such point belongs to
U^{\sigma}
for each
U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}
.

In the terminology of
[
BT25
, Defn. 4.5, Lem. 4.6]
, one can define the sought-after fibre functor as restriction of local systems to a
stable arc-point

\xi
of
X
:
an equivalence class of continuous paths on
X^{\sigma}
, defined on the open interval
(0,\delta)
for some
\delta>0
, satisfying suitable conditions ensuring that the path has finite intersection with any closed subset of the form
Z^{\sigma}
with
Z
a proper Zariski-closed subset of
X
.
Thus, a stable arc-point of
X
restricts to a stable arc-point of each
U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}
.

From now on, fix such a stable arc-point
\xi
;
let
\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(X,\xi)
(resp.
\pi_{1}^{\rm{A}}(X,\xi)
) denote the Tannaka dual of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
(resp. of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
) with respect to the fibre functor at
\xi
, and let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,\xi)
(resp.
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(X,\xi)
) denote the Tannaka dual of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
(resp. of
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
) with respect to the induced fibre functor.
Note that the sequences of group homomorphisms

1\to\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(X,\xi)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,\xi)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)\to 1

and

1\to\pi_{1}^{\rm{A}}(X,\xi)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(X,\xi)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)\to 1

are exact:
after fixing a path between
\xi
and the constant arc-point at some algebraic point
x\in X(\bar{k})
(which always exists, by
[
BT25
, Lem. 4.11]
), this reduces to Theorem
2.24
and Theorem
3.18
, respectively.
The same holds when
X
is replaced by some
U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}
.
In conclusion, we obtain a co-filtered poset of short exact sequences indexed by
\mathsf{Op}_{X}
.

As
U
varies in
\mathsf{Op}_{X}
, the categories
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(U)
form a filtered family where all transition functors are fully faithful with subobject stable image (by Lemma
5.12
), hence the same holds for the subcategories
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U)
.
Their Tannaka duals with respect to the fibre functors at
\xi
thus form a co-filtered inverse system of pro-algebraic groups with surjective transition maps (by
[
DM82
, Prop. 2.21(a)]
).
The Tannaka duals of
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)
and
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U)
are given by the inverse limits

\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(k(X),\xi):=\varprojlim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}}\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(U,\xi),\qquad\pi_{1}^{\rm{A}}(k(X),\xi):=\varprojlim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}}\pi_{1}^{\rm{A}}(U,\xi),

respectively.
The same discussion applies to the categories
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)
(again by Lemma
5.12
), so the Tannaka duals of
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)
and
\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)
are given by the inverse limits

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k(X),\xi):=\varprojlim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(U,\xi),\qquad\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k(X),\xi):=\varprojlim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(U,\xi),

respectively.
Finally, the two limit fundamental sequences

1\to\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(k(X),\xi)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k(X),\xi)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)\to 1

and

1\to\pi_{1}^{\rm{A}}(k(X),\xi)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k(X),\xi)\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)\to 1

are still exact:
using the surjectivity of the transition homomorphisms, it suffices to show the analogous property for inverse limits of abstract groups, which is easy to check.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.10
.

Since the formation of bounded derived categories is compatible with filtered
2
-colimits of abelian categories (see
[
Gal21
, Lem. 2.6]
), it suffices to show that the exact functor

\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(U)\to\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(U)

is an equivalence.
Up to replacing the
k
-variety
X
by some smooth dense open subset, we may assume
X
smooth.
In this case, we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(X),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the two vertical arrows are both equivalences (by cofinality).
Hence, it is equivalent to show that the exact functor

\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)\to\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)

(40)

is an equivalence.
To prove the latter claim, we proceed as follows.

Of course, we may assume the smooth
k
-variety
X
to be non-empty.
Up to considering each connected component of
X
separately, we may even assume
X
connected.

We further reduce to the case when
X
is geometrically connected over
k
.
To this end, let
k^{\prime}
denote the algebraic closure of
k
inside the function field
k(X)
.
By construction, the connected components of the smooth
k^{\prime}
-variety
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime}
are all geometrically connected over
k^{\prime}
;
similarly for each
U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}
.
Let
k^{\prime\prime}
denote the Galois closure of
k^{\prime}
inside
\bar{k}
, and consider the smooth
k^{\prime\prime}
-variety
X^{\prime\prime}:=X\times_{k}k^{\prime\prime}
.
We have a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X^{\prime\prime})^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime\prime}/k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X^{\prime\prime})^{\textup{Gal}(k^{\prime\prime}/k)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{X^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the horizontal arrows witness the equivalences of Lemma
2.22
(2) and Lemma
3.16
(2), respectively;
similarly for each
U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}
.
Thus, the claim holds for
X
as soon as it holds for the
k
-variety
X^{\prime\prime}
.
In fact, after choosing a complex embedding
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\colon k^{\prime\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
extending
\sigma
, we are even allowed to regard
X^{\prime\prime}
as a
k^{\prime\prime}
-variety (by Lemma
2.22
(1) and Lemma
3.16
(1)).
But each connected component of
X^{\prime\prime}
is geometrically connected over
k^{\prime\prime}
(since
X^{\prime}
enjoys the analogous property over
k^{\prime}
).
Therefore, up to replacing the
k
-variety
X
by the
k^{\prime\prime}
-variety
X^{\prime\prime}
and considering each connected component of the latter separately, we may assume
X
smooth and geometrically connected over
k
, say of dimension
d
.

In this situation, the functor (
40
) is a monoidal exact functor between neutral Tannakian categories.
Hence, in order to show that it is an equivalence, it suffices to show that the corresponding homomorphism between Tannaka dual groups is an isomorphism.
To this end, consider the diagram of monoidal functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(k),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{X}^{*}[d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{X}^{*}[d]}

where
a_{X}\colon X\to\Spec(k)
denotes the structural morphism.
Both squares are commutative up to monoidal natural isomorphism (the left-most one by construction, the right-most one by Proposition
5.5
), and the right-most vertical arrow is already known to be an equivalence (by Lemma
5.9
).
Taking the Tannaka dual groups with respect to the fibre functors at some chosen stable arc-point
\xi
of
X
(see Construction
5.13
), we obtain the commutative diagram of pro-algebraic groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(X,\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\rm{A}}(X,\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(X,\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

and similarly for each
U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}
.
Passing to the inverse limit, we obtain the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(k(X),\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k(X),\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\rm{A}}(k(X),\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k(X),\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where both rows are exact (as explained at the end of Construction
5.13
).
This corresponds to the colimit diagram of Tannakian categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\twocolim_{U\in\mathsf{Op}_{X}^{op}}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(k),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{k}}

where the left-most vertical arrow is already known to be an equivalence (by Lemma
5.11
).
Hence, the left-most vertical homomorphism in the previous diagram is an isomorphism.
But then the middle vertical homomorphism in the same diagram must be an isomorphism as well.
∎

5.4
Proof of the comparison theorem

The final step in the proof of Theorem
5.4
is to promote the generic equivalence of Proposition
5.10
to an integral result.
Before proving the full comparison theorem, let us settle the case of motivic local systems:

Theorem 5.14
.

For every smooth (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
X
, the exact functor

o_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)

(41)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
5.10
, we readily reduce to the case when
X
is geometrically connected over
k
.

In this situation, fix a closed point
x\in X(\bar{k})
, and consider the associated commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\rm{N}}(X,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(X,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{N}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\rm{A}}(X,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(X,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

obtained as in the proof of Proposition
5.10
.
Since
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
is a Tannakian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
(by construction), the left-most vertical arrow is an epimorphism.
By diagram-chasing, we deduce that the middle vertical arrow is an epimorphism as well:
this means that
o_{X}
identifies
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
with a Tannakian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
, in particular it is fully faithful (see
[
DM82
, Prop. 2.21(a)]
).
In order to conclude, it remains to show that it is essentially surjective.

To this end, fix an object
L\in\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(X)
.
As a consequence of Proposition
5.10
, there exists a dense open immersion
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
such that the object
j^{*}L\in\operatorname{\mathcal{P}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)
is isomorphic to
o_{U}(M)
for some
M\in\operatorname{\mathcal{M}\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}}(U)
;
without loss of generality we may assume
U
affine, in which case
j
is an affine morphism.
In this situation, we have the chain of isomorphisms in
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(X)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=j_{!*}j^{*}L

(by the proof of Lemma
5.12
)

\displaystyle\simeq j_{!*}o_{U}(M)

\displaystyle=o_{X}(j_{!*}M),

(by Proposition
5.5
)

which shows that
L
belongs to the essential image of
o_{X}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Thus, there is no longer any ambiguity about the notion of motivic local system over
X
.
Consequently, the same happens for the notion of local system of geometric origin:

Corollary 5.15
.

For every smooth (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
X
, the inclusion

\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)

is in fact an equivalence.

Proof.

Indeed, as a consequence of Theorem
5.14
, both categories in question coincide with the smallest abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
containing all local systems underlying motivic local systems and stable under subquotients.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

In particular, this implies that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
equals the intersection
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{Loc}}(X)
:
if a local system on
X
can be written as a subquotient of the realization of some motivic perverse sheaf, it can in fact be written as a subquotient of the realization of some motivic local system.

We are ready to complete the proof of our main result.
Throughout, we systematically use the compatibility of our comparison functors with the six functor formalism (as stated in Proposition
5.5
).
Our argument is formally the same as the one used by Ayoub in the proof of
[
Ayo21
, Thm. 1.93]
.

Proof of Theorem
5.4
.

By Lemma
5.9
, the triangulated comparison functor
o_{X}
is an equivalence in the case when
X=\Spec(k^{\prime})
for some finite extension
k^{\prime}/k
.
In order to prove the same over a general (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
X
, we establish fully faithfulness and then essential surjectivity.

Firstly, we show that
o_{X}
is fully faithful for every
X
.
To this end, fix two objects
M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
.
Writing
a_{X}\colon X\to\Spec(k)
for the structural morphism, we have the usual identification

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet})=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))}(\mathbb{Q}_{X},\Homint_{X}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet}))=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))}(a_{X}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k},\Homint_{X}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet}))=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k))}(\mathbb{Q}_{k},a_{X,*}\Homint_{X}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet})).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(X))}(o_{X}M_{1}^{\bullet},o_{X}M_{2}^{\bullet})=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(X))}(\mathbb{Q}_{X},\Homint_{S}(o_{X}M_{1}^{\bullet},o_{X}M_{2}^{\bullet}))=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(X))}(a_{X}^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{k},\Homint_{X}(o_{X}M_{1}^{\bullet},o_{X}M_{2}^{\bullet}))=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(k))}(\mathbb{Q}_{k},a_{X,*}\Homint_{X}(o_{X}M_{1}^{\bullet},o_{X}M_{2}^{\bullet}))=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(k))}(\mathbb{Q}_{k},a_{X,*}o_{X}\Homint_{X}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet}))=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(k))}(o_{k}\mathbb{Q}_{k},o_{k}a_{X,*}\Homint_{X}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet})),

where the last two passages witness the compatibility of the comparison functors with the six operations (given by Proposition
5.5
).
Under these identifications, the map

\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(X))}(o_{X}M_{1}^{\bullet},o_{X}M_{2}^{\bullet})

defined by
o_{X}
corresponds to the map

\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k))}(\mathbb{Q}_{k},a_{X,*}\Homint_{X}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet}))\rightarrow\Hom_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(k))}(o_{k}\mathbb{Q}_{k},o_{k}a_{X,*}\Homint_{X}(M_{1}^{\bullet},M_{2}^{\bullet}))

defined by
o_{k}
, which is already known to be a bijection (by Lemma
5.9
).
This shows that
o_{X}
is fully faithful, as wanted.

Secondly, we show that
o_{X}
is essentially surjective for every
X
.
We proceed by Noetherian induction on
X
.
The base step is when
\dim(X)=0
, in which case
X
is a disjoint union of spectra of finite extensions of
k
, so that the result is already known (essentially by Lemma
5.9
).
For the inductive step, assume that
\dim(X)>0
and that the result is known to hold over any proper closed subvariety
Z
of
X
.
Fix an object
N^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(X))
.
As a consequence of Proposition
5.10
, there exist a dense open immersion
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
and an object
M_{U}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(U))
with an isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(U))

o_{U}(M_{U}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}j^{*}N^{\bullet}.

Let
i\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
denote the complementary closed immersion.
By inductive hypothesis, there exists an object
M_{Z}^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(Z))
with an isomorphism in
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(Z))

o_{Z}(M_{Z}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\sim}i^{*}N^{\bullet}.

Now consider the localization triangle in
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))

j_{!}j^{*}N^{\bullet}\rightarrow N^{\bullet}\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}N^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\delta}j_{!}j^{*}N^{\bullet}[1].

Since we have already shown that
o_{X}
is fully faithful, there is a unique morphism in
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))

\hat{\delta}\colon i_{*}M_{Z}^{\bullet}\rightarrow j_{!}M_{U}^{\bullet}[1]

(42)

making the diagram in
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(X))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{X}(i_{*}M_{Z}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}o_{Z}(M_{Z}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}N^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{X}(j_{!}M_{U}^{\bullet})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}o_{U}(M_{U}^{\bullet})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}N^{\bullet}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(43)

commute.
Let us complete the morphism (
42
) to a distinguished triangle in
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))

j_{!}M_{U}^{\bullet}\rightarrow M_{X}^{\bullet}\rightarrow i_{*}M_{Z}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\hat{\delta}}j_{!}M_{U}^{\bullet}[1].

It is then possible to complete the commutative diagram (
43
) to a morphism of triangles in
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}(X))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{X}(j_{!}M_{U}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{X}(M_{X}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{X}(i_{*}M_{Z}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{X}(j_{!}M_{U}^{\bullet})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}o_{U}(M_{U}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}o_{Z}(M_{Z}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}o_{U}(M_{U}^{\bullet})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}N^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}N^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}N^{\bullet}[1].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

Since any arrow
o_{X}(M_{X}^{\bullet})\rightarrow N^{\bullet}
filling the latter diagram is automatically an isomorphism (because so are the other two vertical arrows), we see that
N^{\bullet}
lies in the essential image of
o_{X}
.
This shows that
o_{X}
is essentially surjective, thereby completing the proof.
∎

To conclude the present section, we solve the last doubt remained open about perverse sheaves of geometric origin.
In contrast with the case of Corollary
5.15
, this is not a formal consequence of our comparison results.

Proposition 5.17
.

For every (quasi-projective)
k
-variety
X
, the inclusion

\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)

is in fact an equivalence.

Proof.

We want to use the comparison criterion provided by
[
Ter24b
, Prop. 1.9]
.
Note that, while this criterion is stated for subcategories of perverse Nori motives, both the formulation and the proof rely solely on the functoriality of perverse sheaves.

We already know that, for every
k
-variety
X
, the inclusions
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(U)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(U)
as
U
varies in
\mathsf{Op}_{X}
induce an equivalence in the colimit (by Lemma
5.11
).
Therefore, in order to apply
[
Ter24b
, Prop. 1.9]
, it suffices to check that, as
X
varies, the subcategories
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)
are stable under
inverse images under open immersions, extensions by zero and direct images under affine open immersions, direct images under closed immersions, Tate twists, as well as Beilinson’s gluing functors.
This follows formally from the fact that all these functors are
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structures and lift to the categories of perverse Nori motives.
∎

Remark 5.18
.

This implies, in particular, that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{N}}(X)
is already stable under extensions inside
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
:
any extension between perverse sheaves which can be written as subquotients of the realization of some motivic perverse sheaves can be itself written as such a subquotient.

Remark 5.19
.

The comparison criterion of
[
Ter24b
, Prop. 1.9]
also offers an alternative way to deduce Theorem
5.4
from Proposition
5.10
:
instead of comparing the derived categories via localization triangles, one compares the perverse hearts via Beilinson’s gluing functors (which are compatible with the comparison functors, by Proposition
5.5
).

6
Applications and complements

In this final section, we establish some interesting consequences of our comparison theorem.
Most notably, we obtain an
\infty
-categorical enhancement for the six functor formalism of Nori motivic sheaves.
This yields a canonical system of realization functors of Voevodsky motivic sheaves into Nori motivic sheaves, which greatly extends Nori’s results over the base field.
We deduce a rigidity property of Nori motivic sheaves as a six functor formalism.
In the other direction, our comparison result implies strong independence properties of Ayoub’s categories of homotopy-fixed points from the chosen complex embedding
\sigma\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

6.1
A new construction of the Nori realization

The construction of the six operations on Ivorra–Morel’s categories of Nori motivic sheaves is quite involved, for example due to the lack of
t
-exactness of general inverse image functors and of the tensor product.
In particular, it is not clear at first sight whether the resulting monoidal fibered category over
k
-varieties admits an
\infty
-categorical enhancement in the sense of
[
DG22
]
.

This issue was solved by Tubach in
[
Tub25
, §§ 1,2]
:
using a motivic version of an argument due to Nori, he showed that the triangulated category
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
is equivalent to the bounded derived category of its so-called constructible heart.
This yields the sought-after enhancement, because inverse images and the tensor product respect the ordinary
t
-structure.
Our comparison theorem gives an alternative way to obtain the
\infty
-categorical enhancement:

Theorem 6.1
.

The six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
admits a canonical enhancement to a functor

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(-))\colon\mathsf{Var}_{k}^{op}\rightarrow\mathsf{CAlg}(\mathsf{Cat}_{\infty}^{st}),\quad X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))

with values in the
\infty
-category of stably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories.

Proof.

As recalled in Section
4.1
, the six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)}
comes equipped with a canonical
\infty
-categorical enhancement as in the statement.
Therefore, the thesis follows from Theorem
5.4
.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

The
\infty
-categorical enhancement described in Theorem
6.1
coincides with that of
[
Tub25
]
.
This follows from Ayoub’s study of sheaves of geometric origin in
[
Ayo21
, § 1.6]
:
in particular, he showed in
[
Ayo21
, Thm. 1.6.32]
that the stable
\infty
-category
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)
is equivalent to the bounded derived category of its constructible heart, again by adapting Nori’s argument.

The
\infty
-categorical enhancement of the six operations is a significant technical improvement, since it allows one to define coefficient categories coherently over diagrams of
k
-varieties.
This opens the way to a study of nearby and vanishing cycles within the axiomatic framework of
[
Ayo07
, § 3]
.
Furthermore, as explained in
[
Tub25
, Prop. A.4]
, this provides a canonical extension of the six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
to non-necessarily quasi-projective
k
-varieties.

In
[
Tub25
, Thm. 4.5]
, the
\infty
-categorical enhancement is used to extend Nori’s Theorem
5.7
to motivic sheaves, as an application of Drew–Gallauer’s Theorem
4.6
.
Our comparison theorem yields a different perspective on this important result:

Theorem 6.3
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, there exists a canonical triangulated realization functor

\Nri_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))

such that the Betti realization over
X
factors as

\Bti_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\Nri_{X}^{*}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\xrightarrow{\iota_{X}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X))=\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)

up to canonical natural isomorphism.
As
X
varies, the functors
\Nri_{X}^{*}
are compatible with the six operations, as well as with Beilinson’s gluing functors.

Proof.

Thanks to Theorem
5.4
, this follows from Ayoub’s Corollary
4.7
.
∎

This result shows the power of
\infty
-categorical methods in the study of Nori motives:
it provides all realization functors
\Nri_{X}^{*}
at once, without need to construct them individually.
In fact, at present, no direct construction of
\Nri_{X}^{*}
for a single
k
-variety
X
with
\dim(X)>0
is available:
to the authors’ knowledge, the only candidate construction (over smooth
X
) was proposed by Ivorra in
[
Ivo16
]
;
however, its compatibility with the Betti realization and with the six operations is only partially understood.

Remark 6.4
.

Recall that Ayoub’s Corollary
4.7
, and thus also our Theorem
6.3
, is essentially a consequence of Drew–Gallauer’s Theorem
4.6
.
By the uniqueness part in the latter result, the Nori realization is the unique possible morphism of six functor formalisms between Voevodsky and Nori motivic sheaves up to
2
-isomorphism (in the sense of Definition
6.9
(2) below).

The existence of the Nori realization over bases has one important consequence for the conjectural motivic picture.
Recall that, for every
k
-variety
X
, the triangulated category
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
is expected to carry a motivic perverse
t
-structure, characterized by its compatibility with the perverse
t
-structure on
\mathit{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
under the Betti realization.
As noted in Remark
2.2
, if this is the case, then the perverse heart of
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
is exactly
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
.
In fact, as proved by Tubach in
[
Tub25
, Thm. 4.22]
, the existence of the motivic perverse
t
-structures implies that the functors
\Nri_{X}^{*}
are all equivalences;
the proof uses the universal property of Ivorra–Morel’s categories, and again Drew–Gallauer’s Theorem
4.6
.
Combining Tubach’s result with our comparison theorem, we deduce an interesting formula for Voevodsky motivic sheaves:

Corollary 6.5
.

Suppose that, for every
k
-variety
X
, the conjectural motivic perverse
t
-structure on
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
exists.
Then there is a canonical system of equivalences

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)}

compatible with the six operations.

Proof.

If the conjectural motivic
t
-structure on
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)
exists, then by
[
Ayo17a
, Prop. 3.2.9]
the spectral motivic Galois group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)
is already classical, thus equal to
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)
.
Using Corollary
1.19
, we get canonical equivalences

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X;\mathcal{B}_{X})^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k)},

compatibly with the six operations.
If the motivic perverse
t
-structure exists on every
k
-variety
X
, then by
[
Tub25
, Thm. 4.22]
and Theorem
5.4
we get canonical equivalences

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(X)},

again compatibly with the six operations.
This implies the thesis.
∎

We conclude by discussing the compatibility of the Nori realization with weights.
The triangulated categories
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
are endowed with the
Chow weight structure
, constructed by Bondarko in
[
Bon14
]
.
As shown in
[
IM24
, Cor. 6.27]
, the derived categories
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
carry a canonical weight structure which is
transversal
to the perverse
t
-structure in the sense of
[
Bon12
, Defn. 1.2.2]
:
in particular, objects of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
possess a canonical weight filtration so that morphisms in
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
are strictly filtered (see also
[
IM24
, Cor. 6.16, Prop. 6.17]
).

Proposition 6.6
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, the Nori realization functor
\Nri_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
is weight-exact.

This result was proved in
[
Tub25
, Prop. 4.9]
using Hodge-theoretic methods.
Here, we offer a different proof of arithmetic nature, based on the formalism of mixed
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves studied by Morel in
[
Mor25
]
.

Notation 6.7
.

Let
X
be a
k
-variety.

•

Let
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\ell}(X)
denote the triangulated category of
constructible
\ell
-adic complexes
on
X
, as defined in
[
Eke90
]
or in
[
BS15
]
.

•

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\ell}(X)
denote the abelian category of
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves over
X
.

•

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}(X)
denote the full abelian subcategory of
mixed
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves admitting a weight filtration
, as defined in
[
Mor25
, Defn. 2.6.2]
.

In
[
Ayo14b
]
, Ayoub constructed a system of
\ell
-adic étale realization functors

\mathsf{R}_{\ell,X}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\ell}(X)

compatible with the six operations as well as with Beilinson’s gluing functors.
By
[
Bei87a
, Thm. 1.3]
, the realization functor

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\ell}(X)

is an equivalence.
By
[
Mor25
, Thm. 3.2.4, Thm. 6.2.2, Prop. 8.2, Prop. 8.3]
, as
X
varies, the derived categories
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X))
are endowed with the six operations, as well as with Beilinson’s gluing functors, and the forgetful functors

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}(X))

commute with them.
However, it is not clear a priori whether the six operations on mixed perverse sheaves lift to the
\infty
-categorical level:
this makes it difficult to show that Ayoub’s
\ell
-adic realization functor over
X
factors through
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X))
.
However,
[
IM24
, Prop. 6.11(2)]
allows us to identify
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
with the universal abelian factorization of the homological functor

\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{R}_{\ell,X}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\ell}(X)\xrightarrow{{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}}\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}(X).

Consider the associated
\ell
-adic forgetful functor

\iota_{\ell,X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}(X),

and extend the latter to a conservative triangulated functor

\iota_{\ell,X}\colon\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\to\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X))=\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\ell}(X).

The construction of the six operations of
[
IM24
]
and
[
Ter24b
]
can be rewritten using the
\ell
-adic realization in place of the Betti realization everywhere:
this means that the triangulated
\ell
-adic forgetful functors commute with the six operations and with the gluing functors.
Lastly,
[
IM24
, Prop. 6.17]
implies that the
\ell
-adic forgetful functor factors through a faithful exact functor

\iota_{\ell,X}^{mf}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X).

Combining this with Theorem
6.3
, we obtain the following result:

Proposition 6.8
.

For every
k
-variety
X
, there exists a canonical triangulated realization functor

\mathsf{R}^{mf}_{\ell,X}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X))

such that the
\ell
-adic realization over
X
factors as

\mathsf{R}_{\ell,X}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{R}^{mf}_{\ell,X}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}(X))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\ell}(X)

up to canonical natural isomorphism.
As
X
varies, the functors
\mathsf{R}^{mf}_{\ell,X}
are compatible with the six operations, as well as with Beilinson’s gluing functors.

Proof.

For every
k
-variety
X
, the functor
\mathsf{R}^{mf}_{\ell,X}
is defined as the composite

\mathsf{R}_{\ell,X}^{mf}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\xrightarrow{\Nri_{X}^{*}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\xrightarrow{\iota^{mf}_{\ell,X}}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X)).

The compatibility with the six operations and with the gluing functors follows from the analogous compatibilities for the Nori realization and for the
\ell
-adic forgetful functors individually.
Therefore, the fact that the construction recovers Ayoub’s
\ell
-adic realization follows from the uniqueness part of Drew–Gallauer’s Theorem
4.6
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.6
.

Since weights in
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
are uniquely determined by their compatibility with weights in
\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X)
(by definition), it suffices to show that the refined
\ell
-adic realization
\mathsf{R}^{mf}_{\ell,X}
is weight-exact.
In view of the very definition of the Chow weight structure on
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
, this amounts to checking that, for every proper morphism
p\colon W\to X
with
W
a smooth
k
-variety, the object
p_{!}\mathbb{Q}_{W}(n)[2n]\in\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\ell}^{mf}(X))
is pure of weight
0
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By definition of weights on mixed
\ell
-adic complexes, this follows from Deligne’s proof of the Weil Conjecture in
[
Del80
]
.
∎

6.2
Autoequivalences of Nori motivic sheaves

In this section, we use the Nori realization to study autoequivalences of Nori motivic sheaves as a six functor formalism.
We start by recalling the following general notions:

Definition 6.9
.

Let
X\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{1}(X)
and
X\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{2}(X)
be two six functor formalisms over (quasi-projective)
k
-varieties.

1.

A
morphism of six functor formalisms

R\colon\mathcal{D}_{1}(-)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{2}(-)
is the datum of

•

for every
k
-variety
X
, a triangulated functor

F_{X}\colon\mathcal{D}_{1}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{2}(X)

as well as a natural isomorphism between functors
\mathcal{D}_{1}(X)\times\mathcal{D}_{1}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{2}(X)

R_{X}(-)\otimes R_{X}(-)\xrightarrow{\sim}R_{X}(-\otimes-),

(44)

•

for every morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathcal{D}_{1}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{2}(X)

f^{*}\circ R_{Y}\xrightarrow{\sim}R_{X}\circ f^{*}

(45)

satisfying the natural compatibility conditions stated in
[
Ayo10
]
.
Such a morphism
R
is called
strong
if the natural transformation

\Homint_{X}(R_{X}(-),R_{X}(-))\rightarrow R_{X}\Homint_{X}(-,-)

obtained from (
44
) by adjunction in the second variable and the natural transformation

R_{Y}\circ f_{*}\rightarrow f_{*}\circ R_{X}

obtained from (
45
) by adjunction are invertible for every choice of
X
and
f\colon X\to Y
.

2.

Given two morphisms
R_{1},R_{2}\colon\mathcal{D}_{1}(-)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{2}(-)
, a
2
-morphism

\alpha\colon R_{1}\rightarrow R_{2}
is the datum of

•

for every
k
-variety
X
, a natural transformation of functors
\mathcal{D}_{1}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{2}(X)

\alpha_{X}\colon R_{1,X}\rightarrow R_{2,X}

compatible with the structure of monoidal fibered category in the following sense:

(i)

For every
k
-variety
X
, the diagram of functors
\mathcal{D}_{1}(X)\times\mathcal{D}_{1}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{2}(X)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{1,X}(-)\otimes R_{1,X}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{1,X}(-\otimes-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2,X}(-)\otimes R_{2,X}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2,X}(-\otimes-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}\otimes\alpha_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}(-\otimes-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes.

(ii)

For every morphism of
k
-varieties
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
, the diagram of functors
\mathcal{D}_{1}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{2}(X)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\circ R_{1,Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{1,X}\circ f^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\circ R_{2,Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2,X}\circ f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\alpha_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes.

Such a
2
-morphism
\alpha
is called a
2
-isomorphism
if the natural transformation
\alpha_{X}
is invertible for every
k
-variety
X
;
in this case, we say that
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are
isomorphic
.

Strong morphisms are automatically compatible with the entire six functor formalism.
We are particularly interested in the case where
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathcal{D}_{2}
.

Definition 6.10
.

Let
X\mapsto\mathcal{D}(X)
be a six functor formalism over (quasi-projective)
k
-varieties, and let
R\colon\mathcal{D}(-)\to\mathcal{D}(-)
be a self-morphism.

1.

We say that
R
is an
autoequivalence
if the functor
R_{X}\colon\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}(X)
is an equivalence for every
k
-variety
X
.

2.

We say that
R
is a
trivial autoequivalence
if it is
2
-isomorphic to the identity autoequivalence
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
in the sense of Definition
6.9
(2).
In this case, we call
trivialization
of
R
every
2
-isomorphism
\gamma\colon R\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
.

Note that any self-morphism
R\colon\mathcal{D}(-)\to\mathcal{D}(-)
which is
2
-isomorphic to an autoequivalence is itself an autoequivalence, and that every autoequivalence is automatically a strong self-morphism.
Autoequivalences of
X\mapsto\mathcal{D}(X)
form a
2
-group under composition, and trivial autoequivalences form a normal sub-
2
-group of the latter.

Notation 6.11
.

We let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Auteq}}(X\mapsto\mathcal{D}(X))
denote the quotient of the
2
-group of autoequivalences of
X\mapsto\mathcal{D}(X)
by the sub-
2
-group of trivial autoequivalences, and we call it the
autoequivalence
2
-group
of the six functor formalism
X\mapsto\mathcal{D}(X)
.

The six functor formalism of motivic sheaves should possess no interesting autoequivalences.
For Voevodsky motivic sheaves, this can be proved without difficulty:

Proposition 6.12
.

The
2
-group
\operatorname{\mathsf{Auteq}}(X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X))
is trivial.

Proof.

Note that every autoequivalence of
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)
restricts to an autoequivalence of
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)
and that, conversely, every autoequivalence of the latter extends uniquely to an autoequivalence of the former;
since this correspondence is compatible with
2
-isomorphisms, it preserves trivial autoequivalences.
Hence, we have a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{\mathsf{Auteq}}(X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X))=\operatorname{\mathsf{Auteq}}(X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X)),

and the latter is trivial as a consequence of Drew–Gallauer’s Theorem
4.6
.
∎

If the Nori realization functors
\Nri_{X}^{*}
were known to be equivalences, this would imply the triviality of the
2
-group
\operatorname{\mathsf{Auteq}}(X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)))
.
Unfortunately, we are not able to confirm this expectation in full.
Nevertheless, as a consequence of Theorem
5.4
, we can at least establish a weaker variant of the expected result.
To this end, let us say that an autoequivalence
R
of the six functor formalism
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
is
exact
if, for every
k
-variety
X
, the triangulated functor
R_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\to\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
is
t
-exact for the perverse
t
-structure.
Since all trivial autoequivalences are obviously exact, we can define the sub-
2
-group

\operatorname{\mathsf{Auteq}}^{ex}(X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}))\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Auteq}}(X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}))

collecting the exact autoequivalences of
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
modulo the trivial ones.
Our result can then be stated as follows:

Theorem 6.13
.

The
2
-group
\operatorname{\mathsf{Auteq}}^{ex}(X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)))
is trivial.

Proof.

Fix an exact autoequivalence
R
of the coefficient system
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
, and let us construct a trivialization
\gamma\colon R\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}})}
.
For every
k
-variety
X
, the
t
-exact functor

R_{X}:\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))

induces an equivalence of abelian categories

R_{X}^{0}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X).

In fact, since the triangulated functor
R_{X}
admits a dg-enhancement,
[
Vol10
, Thm. 1]
implies that it is naturally isomorphic to the trivial derived functor of
R_{X}^{0}
.
We claim that, as
X
varies, these identifications can be chosen compatibly with the structure of monoidal fibered category of
X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
.
To see this, it suffices to consider the functoriality on quasi-projective
k
-varieties.
In the quasi-projective setting, using the method of
[
Ter24b
]
, we are reduced to showing that the identifications in question respect (shifted) inverse images under smooth morphisms, direct images under closed immersions, and the external tensor product functors.
Since these functors are all
t
-exact and dg-enhanced, by
[
Vol10
, Thm. 1]
it suffices to check the required compatibilities on the level of abelian categories, where they are obvious.

Suppose for a moment that we had defined the sought-after trivialization
\gamma
of
R
.
For each
k
-variety
X
, the natural isomorphism between functors
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))

\gamma_{X}\colon R_{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))}

would restrict to a natural isomorphism between exact functors
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)

\gamma_{X}^{0}\colon R_{X}^{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)},

and, in fact,
[
Vol10
, Thm. 2]
would imply that
\gamma_{X}
equals the natural isomorphism induced by
\gamma_{X}^{0}
.
We are left to construct the natural isomorphisms
\gamma_{X}^{0}
.
The key point is that, by Remark
6.4
, there is a
2
-isomorphism of six functor formalisms

\beta\colon R\circ\Nri^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Nri^{*}.

For every fixed
k
-variety
X
, we obtain a natural isomorphism between functors
\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)

\beta_{X}^{0}\colon R_{X}^{0}\circ{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ\Nri_{X}^{*}={}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ R_{X}\circ\Nri_{X}^{*}\xrightarrow{\beta_{X}}{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ\Nri_{X}^{*}.

(46)

By the very construction of the Nori realization, the composite functor
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ\Nri_{X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
coincides with the homological functor
h_{X}^{0}
.
The trick now is to identify
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
with the universal abelian factorization of the latter:
this allows us to obtain
\gamma_{X}^{0}
directly from the lifting principles of universal abelian factorizations.
In detail, since the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{X}^{0}}

is commutative up to the natural isomorphism
\beta_{X}^{0}
, we get an exact functor

F_{X}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)\to\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)

making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}}

commute strictly, together with a natural isomorphism

\tilde{\beta}_{X}^{0}\colon R_{X}^{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{X}.

Since
F_{X}
is uniquely determined by the strict commutativity requirement (see Remark
2.5
), we must have
F_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)}
.
Thus, we can set
\gamma_{X}^{0}:=\tilde{\beta}_{X}^{0}
.

In order to make sure that, as
X
varies, the resulting natural isomorphisms
\gamma_{X}
define a trivialization as wanted, one needs to prove that they are compatible with inverse image functors as well as with the tensor product in the quasi-projective setting.
Using the method of
[
Ter24c
,
Ter24
]
, it suffices to show that they are compatible with (shifted) inverse images under smooth morphisms, direct images under closed immersions, and the external tensor product functors.
Since these are all
t
-exact and dg-enhanced, one can use
[
Vol10
]
to check the required compatibilities on the abelian level, where they are obvious.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

In view of Theorem
6.13
, showing the triviality of the whole group
\operatorname{\mathsf{Auteq}}(X\mapsto\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)))
amounts to showing that any autoequivalence of Nori motivic sheaves must be exact.
It is not clear to us how hard this would be (unconditionally on the existence of the motivic perverse
t
-structures).
Using Drew–Gallauer’s Theorem
4.6
as in the proof of Theorem
6.13
, one sees that all objects of
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X))
lying in the image of
\Nri_{X}^{*}
must stay fixed under any autoequivalence, and
[
Ter24b
, Thm. 1.12]
implies that the abelian category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
is generated by objects of this kind under (co)kernels.
However, this does not allow us to conclude formally that any autoequivalence must keep the whole of
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(X)
fixed, precisely because one does not know in advance that it commutes with (co)kernels but only with cones.

6.3
Independence properties of Ayoub’s construction

Recall that the very construction of Nori motivic sheaves over
k
-varieties, both in Ivorra–Morel’s and in Ayoub’s sense, depends on the choice of a complex embedding
\sigma\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
In this subsection, we discuss this aspect more closely, leading to some interesting independence-of-
\sigma
results.
Throughout, we restore the more precise Notation
2.12
for Ivorra–Morel’s categories.

The starting point is the following strong independence result in Ivorra–Morel’s setting:

Proposition 6.15

(
[
IM24
, Prop. 6.11]
)
.

Let
X
be a (quasi-projective)
k
-variety.
For every choice of two complex embeddings
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, the abelian categories
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma_{1}}(X)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma_{2}}(X)
are canonically equivalent.

Proof.

Indeed, the abelian category
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}(X)
is defined in
[
IM24
, § 2.1]
as the Serre quotient of the abelian hull
\mathbf{A}(\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(X))
by the kernel of the homological functor
{}^{\mathrm{p}}\!\operatorname{\mathit{H}}^{0}\circ\Bti_{\sigma,X}^{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{DA}}^{\textup{\'{e}t}}_{\mathrm{ct}}(k)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}(X)
.
Therefore, it suffices to show that the kernels associated to
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
coincide.
This ultimately follows from the existence of comparison isomorphisms between the corresponding Betti cohomology theories after scalar extension:
for example, one can use the comparison isomorphism between Betti and de Rham cohomology.
∎

We deduce the following result in Ayoub’s setting:

Theorem 6.16
.

Let
X
be a (quasi-projective)
k
-variety.
For every choice of two complex embeddings
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\colon k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, there exists a canonical equivalence of stable
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{1}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma_{1})}=\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{2}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma_{2})}

(47)

satisfying the natural cocycle condition for triples of embeddings.
As
X
varies, the equivalences (
47
) are compatible with the six operations, as well as with Beilinson’s gluing functors.

Proof.

We define the equivalence (
47
) as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{1}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{2}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma_{1}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma_{1})})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma_{1}}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma_{2}}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Perv}}_{\sigma_{2}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}^{\rm{A}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma_{2})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the central equivalence is given by Proposition
6.15
while the other equivalences are provided by Theorem
4.14
and Theorem
5.4
;
by construction, these are all equivalences of stable
\infty
-categories.
It is easy to see that this definition satisfies the cocycle condition for triples of complex embeddings.
The compatibility with the six operations and with Beilinson’s gluing functors follows from Proposition
5.5
.
∎

One could wonder whether the equivalence (
47
) comes from an equivalence

\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{1}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)=\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{2}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)

(48)

making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{1}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{2}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{1}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{2}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\sigma_{1},X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\sigma_{2},X}}

(49)

commute up to natural isomorphism.
What would certainly be too much to ask for is a system of equivalences of the form (
48
) for all (quasi-projective)
k
-varieties
X
compatible with the six operations.
Indeed, using compatibility with direct images and with the monoidal structure, this would yield functorial isomorphisms of Betti cohomology algebras

H_{\sigma_{1}}^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})\simeq H_{\sigma_{2}}^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q}).

(50)

But it is known that such an isomorphism cannot exist in general.
For instance,
when
k
is imaginary quadratic,
Charles’ work
[
Cha09
]
provides examples of
smooth projective
k
-varieties
X

whose Betti cohomology algebras under
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}

are not isomorphic (not even with
\mathbb{R}
-coefficients).
The closest one can have in general is a canonical isomorphism

H_{\sigma_{1}}^{*}(X,\mathbb{C})=H_{\sigma_{2}}^{*}(X,\mathbb{C}),

induced by the comparison isomorphism between Betti and de Rham cohomology.

In the same vein, one can ask how Nori’s motivic Galois groups
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k,\sigma_{1})
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k,\sigma_{2})
compare to one another.
As a particular case of Proposition
6.15
, the abelian categories
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma_{1}}(k)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma_{2}}(k)
are canonically equivalent, compatibly with the monoidal structures.
Therefore, the Tannaka duals
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k,\sigma_{1})
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}(k,\sigma_{2})
get identified as pro-algebraic groups over
\mathbb{Q}
.
However, this identification is not induced by a natural isomorphism between the corresponding fibre functors

\iota_{k,\sigma_{1}},\;\iota_{k,\sigma_{2}}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{M}}(k)\to\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Indeed, such a natural isomorphism would yield isomorphisms of Betti cohomology algebras as in (
50
).
In other words, even if the derived categories
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{1}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(\Spec(k))
and
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma_{2}\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(\Spec(k))
are canonically equivalent (being both canonically equivalent to
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathsf{vect}_{\mathbb{Q}})
), this equivalence does not make the diagram (
49
) commute.

In conclusion, the significance of Theorem
6.16
is that, even though the category
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)
and the
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma)
-action do depend on the choice of
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, such ambiguities cancel out when passing to the homotopy-fixed points
\operatorname{\mathit{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\sigma\text{-}\mathrm{geo}}(X)^{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{mot}}^{\mathrm{A}}(k,\sigma)}
.

Remark 6.17
.

It would be interesting to see if one can construct the equivalences (
47
) without passing through Ivorra–Morel’s categories.

Appendix A
Equivariant objects under pro-algebraic groups

In this appendix, we collect general results about actions of pro-algebraic groups on ordinary categories and their associated categories of equivariant objects.
We work systematically with explicit definitions, which allows us to check many facts by direct computations.

For sake of simplicity, we start by discussing the basic constructions in the setting of abstract group actions, then we explain how they can be adapted to the pro-algebraic setting.
In the final part, we specialize to neutral Tannakian categories:
the most important result is Proposition
A.38
, in which we compute the Tannaka dual of equivariant Tannakian categories under suitable assumptions.

A.1
Generalities on categories of equivariant objects

In this subsection, we consider actions of abstract groups on ordinary categories by self-equivalences.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category, and let
Q
be an abstract group.
By an
action
of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
we mean the datum of

•

for every
q\in Q
, a functor

q\cdot-\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C},\quad C\mapsto q\cdot C,

•

a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

1_{Q}\cdot-\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}},

(51)

•

for every
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

q_{2}\cdot(q_{1}\cdot-)\xrightarrow{\sim}(q_{2}q_{1})\cdot-

(52)

satisfying the following coherence conditions:
for every
q\in Q
, the two diagrams of functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Q}\cdot(q\cdot-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{Q}q)\cdot-}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q1_{Q})\cdot-}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot(1_{Q}\cdot-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commute and, for every
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\in Q
