, the diagram of functors
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{3}\cdot(q_{2}\cdot(q_{1}\cdot-))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{3}\cdot(q_{2}q_{1}\cdot-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{3}q_{2}\cdot(q_{1}\cdot-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{3}q_{2}q_{1}\cdot-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes.

The coherence conditions imply that, for every
q\in Q
, the functor
q\cdot-\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence with canonical quasi-inverse
q^{-1}\cdot-
.
In practice, we are allowed to pretend that
Q
acts strictly on
\mathcal{C}
by self-automorphisms, with
1_{Q}
acting as the identity.
In order not to overload the notation, from now on we systematically write the additional natural isomorphisms (
51
) and (
52
) as equalities.

Definition A.2
.

Let
Q
be an abstract group acting on a category
\mathcal{C}
as in Definition
A.1
.
The associated category of
equivariant objects

\mathcal{C}^{Q}
is defined as follows:

•

Objects are pairs
(C,\alpha)
consisting of an object
C\in\mathcal{C}
and a collection
\alpha=(\alpha_{q})_{q\in Q}
of isomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}

\alpha_{q}\colon q\cdot C\xrightarrow{\sim}C

such that the isomorphism
\alpha_{1_{Q}}
is induced by the natural isomorphism (
51
), and the following cocycle condition is satisfied:
for every
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}(q_{1}\cdot C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}\cdot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}q_{1}\cdot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}\cdot\alpha_{q_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q_{2}q_{1}}}

commutes.

•

A morphism
\phi\colon(C_{1},\alpha_{1})\rightarrow(C_{2},\alpha_{2})
is the datum of a morphism
\phi\colon C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
such that, for every
q\in Q
, the diagram in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

commutes.

•

Composition in
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
is induced by composition in
\mathcal{C}
.

An elementary yet useful example is the following:

Example A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category, and let
Q
be an abstract group.
Suppose that, for every
q\in Q
, we are given a copy
q\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{C}
and an equivalence
\epsilon_{q}\colon\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\sim}q\mathcal{C}
, and assume for simplicity that
1_{Q}\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}
and
\epsilon_{1_{Q}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
For every
q_{0}\in Q
, consider the functor

q_{0}\colon-\prod_{q\in Q}q\mathcal{C}\to\prod_{q\in Q}q_{0}q\mathcal{C}

defined by the equivalences
q\mathcal{C}\xleftarrow{\epsilon_{q}}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{q_{0}q}}q_{0}q\mathcal{C}
as
q
varies in
Q
.
As the reader can check, this canonically defines a
Q
-action on
\prod_{q\in Q}q\mathcal{C}
in the sense of Definition
A.1
;
it is determined by the left-translation action of
Q
on itself.
The associated category of equivariant objects

(\prod_{q\in Q}q\mathcal{C})^{Q}

is canonically equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
via the projection onto the identity factor.
∎

Equivariant objects behave functorially with respect to group homomorphisms:

Construction A.4
.

Let
f\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{2}
be a morphism of abstract groups.
Suppose that we are given a
Q_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{C}
, and consider the
Q_{1}
-action on
\mathcal{C}
deduced via
f
.
There is a natural
restriction functor

\textup{res}_{f}=\textup{res}_{Q_{2}}^{Q_{1}}\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{Q_{1}},\quad(C,\alpha)\mapsto(C,\alpha^{f}),

where, for every
q\in Q_{1}
, the isomorphism
\alpha^{f}_{q}\colon q\cdot C\xrightarrow{\sim}C
is defined as

q\cdot C:=f(q)\cdot C\xrightarrow{\alpha_{f(q)}}C.

In the case of the trivial homomorphism
1\to Q
, we obtain in this way the
forgetful functor

\textup{for}_{Q}\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q}\to\mathcal{C},\quad(C,\alpha)\mapsto C,

where we identify
\mathcal{C}^{1}
with
\mathcal{C}
in the obvious way.
∎

Restriction functors are clearly compatible with composition of group homomorphisms.
As a consequence, they enjoy the basic properties of forgetful functors:

Lemma A.5
.

Keep the notation of
Construction
A.4
.
Then:

1.

The functor
\textup{res}_{Q_{2}}^{Q_{1}}
is
faithful and conservative.

2.

If
f\colon Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{2}
is surjective,
the functor
\textup{res}_{Q_{2}}^{Q_{1}}
is also full.

Proof.

For the first statement, the result is clear if
Q_{1}=1
.
In the general case, consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{Q_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{Q_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{res}_{Q_{2}}^{Q_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{Q_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{Q_{1}}}

Since the functor
\textup{for}_{Q_{2}}
, which is already known to be faithful (resp. conservative), factors through
\textup{res}_{Q_{2}}^{Q_{1}}
, the latter must be faithful (resp. conservative) as well.

For the second statement, fix two objects
(C_{1},\alpha_{1}),(C_{2},\alpha_{2})\in\mathcal{C}^{Q_{2}}
.
Since
f
is surjective, saying that a morphism
C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
is compatible with
\alpha^{f}_{1,q}
and
\alpha^{f}_{2,q}
for all
q\in Q_{1}
is the same as saying that it is compatible with
\alpha_{1,q}
and
\alpha_{2,q}
for all
q\in Q_{2}
.
∎

Notation A.6
.

When an abstract group
Q
acts on a category
\mathcal{C}
and
Q^{\prime}\leq Q
is a subgroup, we write the effect of the restriction functor along the inclusion homomorphism as

\textup{res}_{Q}^{Q^{\prime}}\colon(C,\alpha)\mapsto(C,\alpha|_{Q^{\prime}}).

Inclusions of normal subgroups have an additional feature:

Construction A.7
.

Let
Q
be an abstract group acting on a category
\mathcal{C}
(as in Definition
A.1
), and let
Q^{\prime}\leq Q
be a normal subgroup.
Then
Q
acts naturally on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
by the formula

q\cdot(C,\alpha)=(q\cdot C,{{}^{q}\alpha}),

where, for every
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}
, the isomorphism
{{}^{q}\alpha_{q^{\prime}}}\colon q^{\prime}\cdot(q\cdot C)\xrightarrow{\sim}q\cdot C
is defined as

q^{\prime}\cdot(q\cdot C)=q\cdot(q^{-1}q^{\prime}q\cdot C)\xrightarrow{q\cdot\alpha_{q^{-1}q^{\prime}q}}q\cdot C.

Note that
{{}^{q}\alpha_{q^{\prime}}}
is well-defined, since the element
q^{-1}q^{\prime}q\in Q
belongs to
Q^{\prime}
(as
Q^{\prime}
is normal in
Q
by hypothesis).
In order to make sure that the pair
(q\cdot C,{{}^{q}\alpha})
really defines an object of
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
, we have to check that the cocycle condition is satisfied:
this amounts to showing that, for every
q^{\prime}_{1},q^{\prime}_{2}\in Q^{\prime}
, the diagram in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{2}\cdot(q^{\prime}_{1}\cdot(q\cdot C))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{2}\cdot(q\cdot C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{2}q^{\prime}_{1}\cdot(q\cdot C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{2}\cdot{{}^{q}\alpha_{q^{\prime}_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{q}\alpha_{q^{\prime}_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{q}\alpha_{q_{2}q_{1}}}}

commutes.
This follows from the cocycle condition for the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
;
we leave the details to the interested reader.
By construction, the forgetful functor
\textup{for}_{Q^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is
Q
-equivariant.
∎

Notation A.8
.

For sake of simplicity, we write
((C,\alpha^{\prime}),\alpha)
for a typical object of the category of equivariant objects
(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}
for the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
discussed in Construction
A.7
:
here
(C,\alpha^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
,
(C,\alpha)\in\mathcal{C}^{Q}
and, for every
q\in Q
, the isomorphism
\alpha_{q}\colon q\cdot C\xrightarrow{\sim}C
defines a morphism
q\cdot(C,\alpha^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}(C,\alpha^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
in the sense of Definition
A.2
.

Lemma A.9
.

Keep the notation and assumptions of Construction
A.7
.
Then the functor

(\textup{for}_{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}\colon(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{Q}

induced by the
Q
-equivariant functor
\textup{for}_{Q^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{C}
admits a canonical fully faithful section

\textup{sec}^{Q}_{Q^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q},\quad(C,\alpha)\mapsto((C,\alpha|_{Q^{\prime}}),\alpha).

Proof.

In the first place, let us check that the formula in the statement really defines a functor
\mathcal{C}^{Q}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}
.
We have to show that, for every object
(C,\alpha)\in\mathcal{C}^{Q}
and every element
q\in Q
, the isomorphism
\alpha_{q}\colon q\cdot C\xrightarrow{\sim}C
in
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the compatibility condition of Definition
A.2
with respect to the
Q^{\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}
:
this amounts to saying that, for every
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}
, the diagram in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}\cdot(q\cdot C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}\cdot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}\cdot\alpha_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{q}\alpha_{q^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q}}

commutes.
This follows from the cocycle condition for the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
;
we leave the details to the interested reader.
It is clear that the composite

\mathcal{C}^{Q}\xrightarrow{\textup{sec}_{Q^{\prime}}^{Q}}(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}\xrightarrow{(\textup{for}_{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}}\mathcal{C}^{Q}

is the identity functor, so
\textup{sec}_{Q^{\prime}}^{Q}
is a section to
(\textup{for}_{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}
as claimed.
This implies, in particular, that
\textup{sec}_{Q^{\prime}}^{Q}
is faithful.
It is also full, because giving a morphism
\phi\colon((C_{1},\alpha^{\prime}_{1}),\alpha_{1})\rightarrow((C_{2},\alpha^{\prime}_{2}),\alpha_{2})
in
(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}
amounts to giving a morphism
\phi\colon C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
which defines both a morphism
(C_{1},\alpha^{\prime}_{1})\rightarrow(C_{2},\alpha^{\prime}_{2})
in
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
and a morphism
(C_{1},\alpha_{1})\rightarrow(C_{2},\alpha_{2})
in
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
.
∎

Remark A.10
.

In general, the functor
\textup{sec}_{Q^{\prime}}^{Q}
is not induced by a functor
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
:
in fact, there is no natural way to define such a functor.

A.2
Compatibility with short exact sequences

Throughout this subsection, we fix a category
\mathcal{C}
endowed with an action by an abstract group
Q
fitting into a short exact sequence of the form

1\rightarrow Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 1.

For notational simplicity, we identify
Q^{\prime}
with its image in
Q
, which is a normal subgroup.
We want to relate the equivariant objects under
Q
with those under
Q^{\prime}
by means of
Q^{\prime\prime}
.
Recall the natural
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
described in Construction
A.7
.
Intuitively, the
Q^{\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
should be trivial, so the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
should factor through the quotient
Q^{\prime\prime}
and induce an equivalence

\mathcal{C}^{Q}=(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}}.

This is not literary true in general, but it becomes so under additional assumptions.
A first example is given by the following result:

Proposition A.11
.

Suppose that the projection
Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime\prime}
admits a splitting;
use it to view
Q^{\prime\prime}
as a subgroup of
Q
, and identify
Q
with the semi-direct product
Q^{\prime}\rtimes Q^{\prime\prime}
.
Then the composite functor

\mathcal{C}^{Q}\xrightarrow{\textup{sec}^{Q}_{Q^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}\xrightarrow{\textup{res}_{Q}^{Q^{\prime\prime}}}(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}}

is an equivalence.

Proof.

By construction, the composite in the statement sends

(C,(\alpha_{q})_{q\in Q})\mapsto((C,(\alpha_{q^{\prime}})_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}),(\alpha_{q^{\prime\prime}})_{q^{\prime\prime}\in Q^{\prime\prime}}).

Every given element
q\in Q
can be written in a unique way as a product
q^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}
with
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime\prime}\in Q^{\prime\prime}
.
Hence, for every object
(C,(\alpha_{q})_{q\in Q})\in\mathcal{C}^{Q}
, the cocycle condition for the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
asserts that the diagram in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}\cdot(q^{\prime\prime}\cdot C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}\cdot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}\cdot\alpha_{q^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q}}

commutes, which implies that
\alpha_{q}
is uniquely determined by
\alpha_{q^{\prime}}
and
\alpha_{q^{\prime\prime}}
.
This observation allows one to construct a quasi-inverse functor explicitly;
we leave the details to the interested reader.
∎

If one drops the assumption that the projection
Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime\prime}
admits a section, the expression
(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}}
becomes problematic, since there is no natural way to define a
Q^{\prime\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
.
In order to make sense of this residual action, one has to make it precise in which sense
Q^{\prime}
acts trivially on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
.
This is based on the following notion:

Definition A.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category, and let
Q
be an abstract group.

1.

Suppose that we are given two actions of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
;
for every
q\in Q
, we write
q\cdot_{A}-
and
q\cdot_{B}-
for the associated functors
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
in these two actions.
By an
isomorphism

\gamma
between the two actions of
Q
of
\mathcal{C}
we mean the datum of

•

for every
q\in Q
, a natural isomorphism between functors
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

\gamma_{q}\colon q\cdot_{A}-\xrightarrow{\sim}q\cdot_{B}-

satisfying the following coherence conditions:
the diagram of functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Q}\cdot_{A}-}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Q}\cdot_{B}-}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{1_{Q}}}

commutes and, for every
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q
, the diagram of functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{2}q_{1})\cdot_{A}-}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}\cdot_{A}(q_{1}\cdot_{A}-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}\cdot_{A}(q_{1}\cdot_{B}-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{2}q_{1})\cdot_{B}-}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}\cdot_{B}(q_{1}\cdot_{B}-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{q_{2}q_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}\cdot_{A}\gamma_{q_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma{q_{2}}}

commutes.

2.

By a
trivialization
of a given action of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
we mean an isomorphism between that action and the trivial action where
q\cdot-=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
for every
q\in Q
.

Example A.13
.

For every action of an abstract group
Q
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, the induced
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
(defined as in Construction
A.7
) admits a canonical trivialization.
Indeed, fix an element
q_{0}\in Q
.
Given
(C,\alpha)\in\mathcal{C}^{Q}
, the isomorphism
\alpha_{q_{0}}\colon q_{0}\cdot C\xrightarrow{\sim}C
in
\mathcal{C}
induces an isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}^{Q}

\alpha_{q_{0}}\colon q_{0}\cdot(C,\alpha)\xrightarrow{\sim}(C,\alpha).

(53)

To make sure that this is the case, we only need to check that, for every
q\in Q
, the diagram in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces qq_{0}\cdot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}\cdot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot\alpha_{q_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}^{q_{0}}\alpha_{q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q_{0}}}

commutes, which follows from the cocycle condition for the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
.
As
(C,\alpha)
varies in
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
, the isomorphisms (
53
) define a natural isomorphism between functors
\mathcal{C}^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{Q}

\alpha_{q_{0}}\colon q_{0}\cdot-\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}^{Q}}.

(54)

As
q_{0}
varies in
Q
, the natural isomorphisms (
54
) satisfy the coherence conditions of Definition
A.12
, again by the cocycle condition.
Hence, they define a trivialization as claimed.
∎

Isomorphisms of actions have the expected effect on equivariant objects:

Construction A.14
.

Suppose that we are given two actions of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
as well as an isomorphism between them (in the sense of Definition
A.12
(1)).
This canonically induces an isomorphism between the associated categories of equivariant objects.
To see this, following the notation of Definition
A.12
(1), write

\gamma_{q}\colon q\cdot_{A}-\xrightarrow{\sim}q\cdot_{B}-

for the natural isomorphisms between the two endofunctors of
\mathcal{C}
defined by a given element
q\in Q
;
write
\mathcal{C}^{Q}_{(A)}
and
\mathcal{C}^{Q}_{(B)}
for the categories of equivariant objects associated to the two actions.
We have a functor

\mathcal{C}^{Q}_{(A)}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{Q}_{(B)},\quad(C,(\alpha_{q})_{q\in Q})\mapsto(C,(\alpha_{q}\circ\gamma_{q}^{-1})_{q\in Q}).

To make sure that this is well-defined, we only need to check that, for every object
(C,\alpha)\in\mathcal{C}^{Q}_{(A)}
, the isomorphisms
\alpha_{q}\circ\gamma_{q}^{-1}\colon q\cdot_{B}C\xrightarrow{\sim}C
satisfy the cocycle condition for the second
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
.
Using the compatibility of the isomorphisms
\gamma_{q}
with respect to composition, this follows from the cocycle condition for the first
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
.
It is clear that the specular construction

\mathcal{C}^{Q}_{(B)}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{Q}_{(A)},\quad(C,(\beta_{q})_{q\in Q})\mapsto(C,(\beta_{q}\circ\gamma_{q})_{q\in Q})

defines an inverse to the previous functor.
Hence, we obtain an isomorphism of categories as stated.
∎

The extra flexibility provided by isomorphic actions allows us to make sense of the formula
\mathcal{C}^{Q}=(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}}
even without assuming the existence of a splitting:

Proposition A.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category endowed with the action of an abstract group
Q
which fits into the short exact sequence

1\rightarrow Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 1.

Assume that we are given, in addition, a
Q^{\prime\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
, and consider the two resulting
Q
-actions on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
:
the one induced by the original
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
(as in Construction
A.7
), and the one deduced from the additional
Q^{\prime\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
.
Suppose that we are given an isomorphism
\gamma
between these two
Q
-actions on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
which, when restricted to
Q^{\prime}
, coincides with the canonical trivialization for the canonical
Q^{\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
(as described in Example
A.13
).
Then we get a canonical equivalence

\mathcal{C}^{Q}=(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}}.

Proof.

For sake of clarity, given an element
q\in Q
and object
(C,\alpha)\in\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
, we write
q\cdot_{A}(C,\alpha)
for the action induced by the original
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
, and
q\cdot_{B}(C,\alpha)
for the action induced by the additional
Q^{\prime\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
;
similarly for the categories of equivariant objects (as in Construction
A.14
above).
Consider the two functors

\textup{sec}_{Q}^{Q^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}_{(A)}

(55)

and

\textup{res}_{Q^{\prime\prime}}^{Q}\colon(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}_{(B)},

(56)

which are both known to be fully faithful (by Lemma
A.9
and Lemma
A.5
(2), respectively).
By construction, the image of (
55
) consists of the objects
((C,\alpha^{\prime}),\alpha)\in(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}_{(A)}
with
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha|_{Q^{\prime}}
, while the image of (
56
) consists of the objects
((C,\alpha^{\prime}),\beta)\in(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}_{(B)}
satisfying
\beta_{q^{\prime}}=\mathrm{id}_{C}
for every
q^{\prime}\in Q
.

Now consider the isomorphism of actions
\gamma
in the hypothesis:
its compatibility with the canonical trivialization over
Q^{\prime}
means precisely that
\gamma_{q^{\prime}}=\alpha_{q^{\prime}}
for every
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}
.
Clearly, this implies that the images of the functors (
55
) and (
56
) correspond to one another under the canonical equivalence

(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}_{(A)}=(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}_{(B)}

obtained as in Construction
A.14
.
This proves the thesis.
∎

Remark A.16
.

The existence of a genuine
Q^{\prime\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
compatible with the action of
Q
is the key hypothesis in Proposition
A.15
.
If the projection
Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime\prime}
admits a splitting, then this hypothesis is automatically satisfied, and we recover the result of Proposition
A.11
.

To conclude this subsection, we discuss a specific situation where Proposition
A.15
can be applied.
This is based on a categorical analogue of the notion of induced representation:

Example A.17
.

Suppose that we are given a
Q^{\prime}
-action on a category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
Choose a set-theoretic splitting

Q^{\prime\prime}\to Q,\quad r\mapsto q_{r}

to the projection
Q\to Q^{\prime\prime}
, and assume for simplicity that
q_{1_{Q^{\prime\prime}}}=1_{Q}
.
Suppose that, for each
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, we are given a category
q_{r}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
together with an equivalence

\epsilon_{r}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sim}q_{r}\mathcal{C}^{\prime},

and assume for simplicity that
1_{Q^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\epsilon_{1_{Q^{\prime\prime}}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
.
Form the direct product category

\mathcal{C}:=\prod_{s\in Q^{\prime\prime}}q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

Given an element
q\in Q
mapping to
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, we define a functor
q\cdot-\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
as follows:
for every
s\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, we let
q\cdot-\colon q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to q_{rs}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be the composite

q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\epsilon_{s}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{q_{rs}^{-1}qq_{s}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{rs}}q_{rs}\mathcal{C}^{\prime},

and we define
q\cdot-\colon\prod_{s\in Q^{\prime\prime}}q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\prod_{s\in Q^{\prime\prime}}q_{rs}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
consequently.
This defines a
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
with the property that, for every
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}
, the functor
q^{\prime}\cdot-\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
is the direct product of the functors

q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\epsilon_{s}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{q_{s}^{-1}q^{\prime}q_{s}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{s}}q_{s}\mathcal{C}.

In particular, the
Q^{\prime}
-action on the identity component
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is nothing but the original
Q^{\prime}
-action.
Moreover, for every
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, the functor
q_{r}\cdot-\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
acts on the identity component as
\epsilon_{r}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\rightarrow q_{r}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

We claim that this
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
A.15
.
To this end, note that we have

\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}=\prod_{s\in Q^{\prime\prime}}(q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}},

where the
Q^{\prime}
-action on each
q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is twisted by
q_{s}
as above.
Consider the canonical
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
(as described in Construction
A.7
):
for every
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, the equivalence
q_{r}\cdot-\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
induces an equivalence

q_{r}\colon-\colon(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(q_{r}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}

making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{r}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{r}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{Q^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{Q^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{r}}

commute.
This allows us to define a
Q^{\prime\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
as follows:
given
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, for every
s\in Q^{\prime\prime}
we let
r\cdot-\colon(q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}\to(q_{rs}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}
be the composite

(q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}\xleftarrow{q_{s}\cdot-}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{q_{rs}\cdot-}(q_{rs}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}},

(57)

and we define
r\cdot-\colon\prod_{s\in Q^{\prime\prime}}(q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}\to\prod_{s\in Q^{\prime\prime}}(q_{rs}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}
consequently.

To prove the claim, we have to construct an isomorphism between the two
Q
-actions on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
extending the canonical trivialization over
Q^{\prime}
.
The crucial observation is the following:
given an element
q\in Q
mapping to
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, the action of
q
on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
induced by the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
is defined by the functors

(q_{s}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}\xleftarrow{q_{s}\cdot-}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{q_{rs}^{-1}qq_{s}\cdot-}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{q_{rs}\cdot-}(q_{rs}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}.

(58)

Now consider the canonical trivialization for the
Q^{\prime}
-action on
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}
(as discussed in Example
A.13
):
the natural isomorphism of functors
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}\to(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}

q_{rs}^{-1}qq_{s}\cdot-\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{id}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}}}

induces a natural isomorphism between the functors (
58
) and (
57
);
taking the product over all
s\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, this defines a natural isomorphism between the two actions of
q
on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
.
By construction, these natural isomorphisms assemble into an isomorphism of
Q
-actions with the required properties;
we leave the details to the interested reader.
Finally, applying Proposition
A.15
, we get a canonical equivalence

\mathcal{C}^{Q}=(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{Q^{\prime}},

where the second passage is induced by the projection onto the identity factor (as in Example
A.3
).
∎

Remark A.18
.

There is a simple way to recognize categorical induced representations in practice.
As in Example
A.17
, suppose that we are given a
Q^{\prime}
-action on a category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
;
suppose that, for every
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, we are given a category
r\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, and assume that
1_{Q^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
Form the direct product category

\mathcal{C}:=\prod_{s\in Q^{\prime\prime}}s\mathcal{C}^{\prime},

and suppose that we are given a
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
with the property that, given an element
q\in Q
mapping to
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, the functor
q\cdot-\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
is the direct product of suitable functors

q\cdot-\colon s\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to(rs)\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\qquad(s\in Q^{\prime\prime}).

This means that, in particular, the
Q^{\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}
obtained by restriction is the direct product of suitable
Q^{\prime}
-actions on the single components
s\mathcal{C}
.
We claim that, up to isomorphism, the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}
is induced by the original
Q^{\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in the sense of Example
A.17
.
To see this, fix a set-theoretic splitting

Q^{\prime\prime}\to Q,\quad r\mapsto q_{r}

satisfying
q_{1_{Q^{\prime\prime}}}=1_{Q}
.
Given an element
q\in Q
mapping to
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(rs)\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{s}\cdot-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{rs}^{-1}qq_{s}\cdot-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{rs}\cdot-}

commutes up to natural isomorphism for every
s\in Q^{\prime\prime}
.
Hence, defining the equivalences

\epsilon_{r}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{q_{r}\cdot-}r\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

and renaming
r\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=:q_{r}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
for all
r\in Q^{\prime\prime}
, we are exactly in the situation of Example
A.17
, as wanted.

A.3
Equivariant objects under pro-algebraic groups

From now henceforth, we fix a field
F
of characteristic
0
.
All categories and functors considered are
F
-linear;
in particular, all actions of groups are defined by
F
-linear functors.
We let
\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
denote the category of classical commutative
F
-algebras.

Notation A.19
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
F
-linear category.
For every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, we let
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
denote the category with the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
and with homomorphisms defined as

\Hom_{\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}}(C_{1},C_{2})=\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(C_{1},C_{2})\otimes_{F}\Lambda.

We usually write
C\otimes\Lambda
for the object of
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
corresponding to a given object
C\in\mathcal{C}
.

Remark A.20
.

Given an
F
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
, the categories
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
as
\Lambda
varies in
\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
form a fibered category over
\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}^{op}
:
for every
F
-algebra homomorphism
\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}
, the transition functor
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
sends
C\otimes\Lambda\mapsto C\otimes\Lambda^{\prime}
for every
C\in\mathcal{C}
.
As a consequence of faithfully flat descent for modules, this fibered category is a stack for the fppf topology.

The natural notion of pro-algebraic group action is based on the associated functor of points, as follows:

Definition A.21
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
F
-linear category, and let
Q
be a pro-algebraic group over
F
.
By an
F
-algebraic action
of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
we mean the datum of

•

for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, a
\Lambda
-linear action of the abstract group
Q(\Lambda)
on
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
(in the sense of Definition
A.1
)

functorially with respect to
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
in the following sense:
for every
F
-algebra homomorphism
\phi\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, the diagram of
\Lambda
-linear functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(q)\cdot-}

commutes.

The natural notion of equivariant objects in the pro-algebraic setting follows the same lines:

Definition A.22
.

Let
Q
be a pro-algebraic group acting
F
-algebraically on
\mathcal{C}
as in Definition
A.21
.
The associated category of
F
-algebraic equivariant objects

\mathcal{C}^{Q}
is defined as follows:

•

Objects are pairs
(C,\underline{\alpha})
consisting of an object
C\in\mathcal{C}
and a family
\underline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}}
where, for each
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
,
\alpha_{\Lambda}=(\alpha_{\Lambda,q})_{q\in Q(\Lambda)}
is a collection of isomorphisms

\alpha_{\Lambda,q}\colon q\cdot(C\otimes\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\sim}C\otimes\Lambda

in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
such that the pair
(C\otimes\Lambda,\alpha_{\Lambda})
defines a homotopy-fixed point for the action of the abstract group
Q(\Lambda)
on
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
(in the sense of Definition
A.2
), functorially with respect to
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
in the following sense:
for every
F
-algebra homomorphism
\phi\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, the
\Lambda^{\prime}
-module isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}}(q\cdot(C\otimes\Lambda),C\otimes\Lambda)\otimes_{\Lambda,\phi}\Lambda^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Hom_{\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}}(q\cdot(C\otimes\Lambda^{\prime}),C\otimes\Lambda^{\prime})}

sends
\alpha_{\Lambda,q}\otimes 1\mapsto\alpha_{\Lambda^{\prime},\phi(q)}
.

•

A morphism
f\colon(C_{1},\underline{\alpha}_{1})\rightarrow(C_{2},\underline{\alpha}_{2})
is the datum of a morphism
f\colon C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
such that, for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, the diagram in
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot(C_{1}\otimes\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot(C_{2}\otimes\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}\otimes\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}\otimes\Lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1,\Lambda,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2,\Lambda,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes.

•

Composition in
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
is induced by composition in
\mathcal{C}
.

Note that, given an
F
-algebraic action of a pro-algebraic group
Q
on an
F
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
, there is always a canonical faithful functor from the
F
-algebraic equivariant objects to the equivariant objects associated to the abstract action of
Q(F)
on
\mathcal{C}
(as in Definition
A.2
):
namely, the functor

\mathcal{C}^{Q}\to\mathcal{C}^{Q(F)},\quad(C,\underline{\alpha})\mapsto(C,\alpha_{F}).

(59)

The two notions match in the case of finite groups:

Example A.23
.

Let
Q
be a finite group, and regard it as a finite constant algebraic group over
F
.
Then, for any
F
-algebraic action of
Q
on an
F
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
, the functor (
59
) is an equivalence.
Indeed, fix an object
(C,\underline{\alpha})\in\mathcal{C}^{Q}
.
For every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, we have a canonical bijection
Q(\Lambda)=Q=Q(F)
.
Hence, the isomorphism
\alpha_{\Lambda,q}
for a chosen
q\in Q(\Lambda)
is uniquely determined by the isomorphism
\alpha_{F,q}
for the corresponding
q\in Q(F)
.
∎

In general, equivariant objects in the
F
-algebraic setting enjoy stronger finiteness properties than those in the abstract setting.
The difference is apparent for categories in which all Hom groups are finite-dimensional over
F
.

Example A.24
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a neutral Tannakian category over
F
, and let
K
denote its Tannaka dual with respect to a fixed fibre functor
\omega\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathsf{vect}_{F}
.
With respect to the trivial
K
-action on
\mathsf{vect}_{F}
, the category of
F
-algebraic equivariant objects
\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{K}
is just the category
\Rep_{F}(K)
of
F
-algebraic representations of
K
.
Via Tannaka duality, this allows us to interpret
\mathcal{T}
as a category of
F
-algebraic equivariant objects.
∎

The constructions and results collected
in Subsection
A.1

for abstract group actions
admit natural analogues in the
F
-algebraic setting.
We content ourselves of giving an overview,
modifying the arguments of
Subsection
A.1
when needed.

To begin with, given a morphism of pro-algebraic groups
Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{2}
and an
F
-algebraic
Q_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{C}
, there is a restriction functor

\textup{res}_{Q_{2}}^{Q_{1}}\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{Q_{1}},

analogously to Construction
A.4
.
The conclusions of Lemma
A.5
hold in the
F
-algebraic setting as well:

Lemma A.25
.

For every morphism
f:Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{2}
of pro-algebraic groups over
F
, the following hold:

1.

The functor
\textup{res}_{Q_{2}}^{Q_{1}}
is faithful and conservative.

2.

If
f
is an epimorphism, the functor
\textup{res}_{Q_{2}}^{Q_{1}}
is also full.

Proof.

The first statement follows by applying Lemma
A.5
(1) to the group homomorphism
Q_{1}(\Lambda)\rightarrow Q_{2}(\Lambda)
for each
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
.

For the second statement, fix two objects
(C_{1},\underline{\alpha}_{1}),(C_{2},\underline{\alpha}_{2})\in\mathcal{C}^{Q_{2}}
, and choose a morphism
\phi\colon C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
which is compatible with
\alpha_{1,\Lambda,f(q_{1})}
and
\alpha_{2,\Lambda,f(q_{1})}
for every
q_{1}\in Q_{1}(\Lambda)
and every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
.
Since the categories
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
form an fppf stack over
\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and the morphism
f\colon Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{2}
is an fppf epimorphism, it follows that
\phi
is automatically compatible with
\alpha_{1,\Lambda,q_{2}}
and
\alpha_{2,\Lambda,q_{2}}
for every
q_{2}\in Q_{2}(\Lambda)
and every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
.
∎

If
Q
acts algebraically on
\mathcal{C}
and
Q^{\prime}\leq Q
is a closed normal subgroup, the functor
(\textup{for}_{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}\colon(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{Q}
admits a fully faithful section

\textup{sec}_{Q^{\prime}}^{Q}\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q},

analogously to Lemma
A.9
.
If moreover
Q
fits into a split short exact sequence of pro-algebraic groups

1\rightarrow Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 1,

we have a canonical equivalence

\mathcal{C}^{Q}=(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}},

analogously to Proposition
A.11
.

In order to state the
F
-algebraic analogue of Proposition
A.15
, we need to spell out the precise notion of isomorphism between
F
-algebraic actions:

Definition A.26
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
F
-linear category, and let
Q
be a pro-algebraic group over
F
.

1.

Suppose that we are given two
F
-algebraic actions of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
;
for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, we write
q\cdot_{A}-
and
q\cdot_{B}-
for the associated functors
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
in these two actions.
By an
isomorphism
between the two actions of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
we mean the datum of

•

for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, a
\Lambda
-linear isomorphism
\gamma_{\Lambda}
between the two actions of the abstract group
Q(\Lambda)
on
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
(in the sense of Definition
A.12
)

functorially with respect to
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
in the following sense:
for every
F
-algebra homomorphism
\phi\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, the diagram of functors
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q\cdot_{A}-)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q\cdot_{B}-)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(q)\cdot_{A}(-\otimes_{\Lambda,\phi}\Lambda^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(q)\cdot_{B}(-\otimes_{\Lambda,\phi}\Lambda^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Lambda,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Lambda^{\prime},\phi(q)}}

commutes.

2.

By a
trivialization
of a given action of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
we mean an isomorphism between that action and the trivial action where
q\cdot-=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}}
for every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
and every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
.

For every
F
-algebraic action of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
, the induced
Q
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
admits a canonical trivialization, analogously to Example
A.13
.
Moreover, an isomorphism between two actions of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
canonically induces an equivalence between the associated categories of
F
-algebraic homotopy-fixed points, analogously to Construction
A.14
.

Here is the
F
-algebraic analogue of Proposition
A.15
:

Proposition A.27
.

Let
Q
be a pro-algebraic group acting on an
F
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
and fitting into the short exact sequence

1\rightarrow Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 1.

Assume that we are given, in addition, a
Q^{\prime\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
, and consider the two resulting
Q
-actions on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
.
Suppose that we are given an isomorphism between these two
Q
-actions which, when restricted to
Q^{\prime}
, coincides with the canonical trivialization for the
Q^{\prime}
-action on
\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}}
.
Then we get a canonical equivalence

\mathcal{C}^{Q}=(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}}.

Proof.

This follows by adapting the proof of
Proposition
A.15

to the
F
-algebraic setting.
The only delicate point concerns the fully faithfulness of

\textup{res}_{Q^{\prime\prime}}^{Q}\colon(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q^{\prime\prime}}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{Q^{\prime}})^{Q}_{(B)},

which follows from Lemma
A.25
(2).
∎

Remark A.28
.

As in the case of abstract group actions, Proposition
A.27
can be applied to the categorical induced representations.
Note that, in order to make the discussion of Example
A.17
work in the pro-algebraic setting, one needs to require the existence of a scheme-theoretic splitting to the projection
Q\to Q^{\prime\prime}
respecting the unit sections.
This needs not exist in general, but it always exists when
Q^{\prime\prime}
is a finite group and the ground field
F
is algebraically closed.

A.4
The abstract fundamental sequence

We further specialize the discussion to the setting of monoidal
F
-linear categories.

Notation A.29
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (unitary, symmetric) monoidal
F
-linear category;
given two objects
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
, we let
C_{1}\otimes C_{2}
denote their tensor product.
For every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, we regard the category
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
introduced in Notation
A.19
as a (unitary, symmetric) monoidal
\Lambda
-linear category in the natural way:
for every
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
, we set

(C_{1}\otimes\Lambda)\otimes(C_{2}\otimes\Lambda):=(C_{1}\otimes C_{2})\otimes\Lambda.

Definition A.30
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (unitary, symmetric) monoidal
F
-linear category, and let
Q
be a pro-algebraic group over
F
.
By a
(unitary, symmetric) monoidal
F
-algebraic action
of
Q
on
\mathcal{C}
we mean the datum of

•

for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, a (unitary, symmetric) monoidal
\Lambda
-linear action of the abstract group
Q(\Lambda)
on
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
(in the sense of Definition
A.1
)

functorially with respect to
F
-algebra homomorphisms as in Definition
A.21
.

There is no need to introduce another notion of equivariant objects in the monoidal setting.
Indeed:

Lemma A.31
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (unitary, symmetric) monoidal
F
-linear category, and let
Q
be a pro-algebraic group over
F
acting on
\mathcal{C}
as in Definition
A.30
.
Then the category of
F
-algebraic equivariant objects
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
from Definition
A.22
carries a canonical (unitary, symmetric) monoidal structure making the forgetful functor
\textup{for}_{Q}\colon\mathcal{C}^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
(unitary, symmetric) monoidal.

Proof.

One defines the tensor product on
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
be the formula

(C_{1},\underline{\alpha}_{1})\otimes(C_{2},\underline{\alpha}_{2}):=(C_{1}\otimes C_{2},\underline{\alpha}_{1}\otimes\underline{\alpha}_{2})

where, for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, the isomorphism

(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2})_{\Lambda,q}\colon q\cdot((C_{1}\otimes C_{2})\otimes\Lambda)=q\cdot((C_{1}\otimes\Lambda)\otimes(C_{2}\otimes\Lambda))=q\cdot(C_{1}\otimes\Lambda)\otimes q\cdot(C_{2}\otimes\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\sim}(C_{1}\otimes\Lambda)\otimes(C_{2}\otimes\Lambda)

is defined as any of the two composites in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot(C_{1}\otimes\Lambda)\otimes q\cdot(C_{2}\otimes\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{1}\otimes\Lambda)\otimes q\cdot(C_{2}\otimes\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\cdot(C_{1}\otimes\Lambda)\otimes(C_{2}\otimes\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{1}\otimes\Lambda)\otimes(C_{2}\otimes\Lambda).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{q\cdot C_{1}}\otimes\alpha_{2,\Lambda,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1,\Lambda,q}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{q\cdot C_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{C_{1}}\otimes\alpha_{2,\Lambda,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1,\Lambda,q}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C_{2}}}

It is straightforward to check that this indeed gives a well-defined (unitary, symmetric) monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}^{Q}
;
we leave the details to the interested reader.
It is also clear that the forgetful functor canonically becomes (unitary, symmetric) monoidal in this way.
∎

We are particularly interested in monoidal actions on neutral Tannakian categories over
F
.

Notation A.32
.

Let
K
be a pro-algebraic group over
F
, and let
\Rep_{F}(K)
denote the (unitary, symmetric) monoidal
F
-linear category of finite-dimensional
F
-algebraic representations of
K
.
We write a typical object
V\in\Rep_{F}(K)
as a pair
(W,\rho)
consisting of an object
W\in\mathsf{vect}_{F}
and a morphism of pro-algebraic groups

\rho\colon K\rightarrow\underline{\textup{GL}}_{F}(W).

For every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, we write the corresponding object
V\otimes\Lambda\in\Rep_{F}(K)_{\Lambda}
as the pair
(W\otimes\Lambda,\rho_{\Lambda})
, where

\rho_{\Lambda}\colon K_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\underline{\textup{GL}}_{\Lambda}(W\otimes\Lambda)

denotes the morphism of group schemes over
\Lambda
obtained by base-change from
\rho
.

Example A.33
.

Let
K
be a pro-algebraic group over
F
.
By an
F
-algebraic (right) action
of
Q
on
K
we mean a morphism of
F
-schemes
a\colon K\times Q\rightarrow K
such that, for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, the map on
\Lambda
-valued points

a\colon K(\Lambda)\times Q(\Lambda)\rightarrow K(\Lambda)

defines a right action of
Q(\Lambda)
on
K(\Lambda)
by group automorphisms.
Note that, for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, the action of
q
canonically extends to a morphism of
\Lambda
-group schemes

a_{q}\colon K_{\Lambda}\rightarrow K_{\Lambda}.

An
F
-algebraic action of
Q
on
K
canonically induces a (unitary, symmetric) monoidal
F
-algebraic action of
Q
on
\Rep_{F}(K)
:
for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, the action of an element
q\in Q(\Lambda)
on the category
\Rep_{F}(K)_{\Lambda}
is defined by the formula

q\cdot(W\otimes\Lambda,\rho_{\Lambda})=(W\otimes\Lambda,\rho_{\Lambda}^{q}),

where
\rho_{\Lambda}^{q}\colon K_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\underline{\textup{GL}}_{\Lambda}(W\otimes\Lambda)
denotes the composite morphism
K_{\Lambda}\xrightarrow{a_{q}}K_{\Lambda}\xrightarrow{\rho_{\Lambda}}\underline{\textup{GL}}_{\Lambda}(W\otimes\Lambda)
.
∎

Proposition A.34
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a neutral Tannakian category over
F
, and let
Q
be a pro-algebraic group over
F
acting monoidally on
\mathcal{T}
as in Definition
A.30
.
Then the (unitary, symmetric) monoidal category of
F
-linear equivariant objects
\mathcal{T}^{Q}
is neutral Tannakian over
F
.

Proof.

Every morphism
f\colon(V_{1},\underline{\alpha}_{1})\rightarrow(V_{2},\underline{\alpha}_{2})
in
\mathcal{T}^{Q}
admits a (co)kernel, obtained by equipping the (co)kernel of
f\colon V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2}
in
\mathcal{T}
with the unique structure of equivariant object compatible with
\underline{\alpha}_{1}
and
\underline{\alpha}_{2}
.
This implies at once that the additive category
\mathcal{T}^{Q}
is abelian and that the forgetful functor
\mathcal{T}^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
is exact.
It is also clear that the monoidal structure obtained in Lemma
A.31
makes
\mathcal{T}^{Q}
an abelian tensor category.
Moreover, any object
(V,\underline{\alpha})\in\mathcal{T}^{Q}
admits a strong dual
(V^{\lor},\underline{\alpha}^{\lor})
, where
V^{\lor}
denotes the strong dual of
V
in
\mathcal{T}
and, for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, the isomorphism

\alpha^{\lor}_{\Lambda,q}\colon q\cdot(V^{\lor}\otimes\Lambda)=q\cdot(V\otimes\Lambda)^{\lor}=(q\cdot(V\otimes\Lambda))^{\lor}\xrightarrow{\sim}(V\otimes\Lambda)^{\lor}=V^{\lor}\otimes\Lambda

is defined as the inverse-transpose of
\alpha_{\Lambda,q}
.
Finally, one obtains a fibre functor for
\mathcal{T}^{Q}
by composing the forgetful functor
\textup{for}_{Q}\colon\mathcal{T}^{Q}\to\mathcal{T}
with any given fibre functor for
\mathcal{T}
.
∎

Example A.35
.

Let
Q
be any pro-algebraic group over
F
, and let it act trivially on the neutral Tannakian category
\mathsf{vect}_{F}
;
this is induced by the trivial action of
Q
on its Tannaka dual
\Spec(F)
via Example
A.33
.
Then the associated category of
F
-algebraic equivariant objects
\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{Q}
is canonically equivalent to
\Rep_{F}(Q)
as an abelian tensor category.
∎

The construction of
F
-algebraic equivariant objects under a given pro-algebraic group
Q
is clearly compatible with
Q
-equivariant
F
-linear functors.
In the Tannakian setting, this observation translates as follows:

Construction A.36
.

Any
Q
-equivariant
F
-linear tensor functor between neutral Tannakian categories
t\colon\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
induces an
F
-linear tensor functor

t^{Q}\colon{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{Q}

making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}^{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

commute.
Applying this to the case where
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathsf{vect}_{F}
(with trivial action by
Q
, as in Example
A.35
) and
t
is the unique
F
-linear tensor functor
\mathsf{vect}_{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
, we obtain a canonical functor

\Rep_{F}(Q)=\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{Q}.

Note that its essential image is stable under subobjects, since the same holds for the underlying functor
\mathsf{vect}_{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
.
As noted in
[
Jac25
, Prop. 2.11]
, this automatically implies that it is fully faithful.
∎

We move on to the last main result of the appendix.

Construction A.37
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a neutral Tannakian category over
F
;
fix a fibre functor
\omega\colon\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathsf{vect}_{F}
, and let
K
denote the associated Tannaka dual group.
Let
Q
be a pro-algebraic group over
F
acting on
\mathcal{T}
(as in Definition
A.30
), and regard
\mathcal{T}^{Q}
as a neutral Tannakian category as in Proposition
A.34
;
let
G
denote its Tannaka dual group with respect to the fibre functor
\omega\circ\textup{for}_{Q}\colon\mathcal{T}^{Q}\rightarrow\mathsf{vect}_{F}
.
Under Tannaka duality, the forgetful functor
\textup{for}_{Q}\colon\mathcal{T}^{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
corresponds to a morphism of pro-algebraic groups

K\rightarrow G.

Similarly, the tensor functor
t^{Q}\colon\Rep_{F}(Q)\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{Q}
described in Construction
A.36
corresponds to a morphism of pro-algebraic groups

G\rightarrow Q,

which is faithfully flat by
[
DM82
, Prop. 2.21(b)]
.
In fact,
t^{Q}
identifies
\Rep_{F}(Q)
with the full Tannakian subcategory of
\mathcal{T}^{Q}
consisting of those objects
V=(W,\underline{\alpha})
whose underlying object
W\in\mathcal{T}
is a trivial
K
-representation.
Under Tannaka duality, this means that the composite homomorphism

K\to G\to Q

is trivial.
∎

The above discussion leads one to wonder about the exactness properties of the sequence

1\rightarrow K\rightarrow G\rightarrow Q\rightarrow 1.

(60)

This seems a delicate question in general, especially for what concerns the injectivity of
K\rightarrow G
.
The following particular case suffices for our main applications:

Proposition A.38
.

Keep the notation and assumptions of Construction
A.37
.
Suppose that, under the equivalence
\mathcal{T}=\Rep_{F}(K)
defined by
\omega
, the
F
-algebraic
Q
-action on
\mathcal{T}
is induced by an
F
-algebraic right action of
Q
on
K
(in the sense of Example
A.33
);
form the associated semi-direct product
K\rtimes Q
.
Then there exists a canonical isomorphism of pro-algebraic groups

G\xrightarrow{\sim}K\rtimes Q

under which the sequence (
60
) gets identified with the split short exact sequence

1\rightarrow K\rightarrow K\rtimes Q\rightarrow Q\rightarrow 1.

Proof.

For notational clarity, throughout the proof we identify
\mathcal{T}
with
\Rep_{F}(K)
, and we write
\Rep_{F}(G)
as
\Rep_{F}(K)^{Q}
.
By Tannaka duality, in order to prove the thesis it suffices to construct an equivalence of neutral Tannakian categories

\Rep_{F}(K\rtimes Q)=\Rep_{F}(K)^{Q}

making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rep_{F}(K)^{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rep_{F}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rep_{F}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rep_{F}(K\rtimes Q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{for}_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{res}_{Q}^{K\rtimes Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{res}_{K\rtimes Q}^{K}}

(61)

commute.
Using the canonical equivalences provided by Example
A.35
, we can write the sought-after equivalence in the form

\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{K\rtimes Q}=(\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{K})^{Q},

which makes it easier to construct it explicitly.
Indeed, consider the canonical action of
K\rtimes Q
on
\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{K}
(defined as in Construction
A.7
) and the associated functor

\textup{sec}_{K}^{K\rtimes Q}\colon\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{K}\rightarrow(\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{K})^{K\rtimes Q}.

By the
F
-algebraic version of Proposition
A.11
, we know that the composite functor

\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{K\rtimes Q}\xrightarrow{\textup{sec}_{K}^{K\rtimes Q}}(\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{K})^{K\rtimes Q}\xrightarrow{\textup{res}_{K\rtimes Q}^{Q}}(\mathsf{vect}_{F}^{K})^{Q}

is an equivalence.
It is easy to check that this functor indeed makes the diagram (
61
) commute and, in fact, it is characterized by this property;
we leave the details to the interested reader.
∎

Remark A.39
.

In the setting of Proposition
A.38
, the original
F
-algebraic right action of
Q
on
K
can be recovered as the conjugation action induced by the short exact sequence (
60
).

Remark A.40
.

If one knows that every object of
\mathcal{T}

is a subobject of some object in the image of
\mathcal{T}^{Q}
,
then one can deduce the exactness of
the entire sequence (
60
),
even without the technical assumption
of Proposition
A.38
,
as an application of
[
DE22
, Prop. A.13]
.

We conclude by providing a useful criterion in the direction of Proposition
A.38
.
In a nutshell, a given monoidal
F
-algebraic action of
Q
on
\Rep_{F}(K)
is induced by an
F
-algebraic action of
Q
on
K
precisely when it is an action within the
2
-category of neutralized (as opposed to neutral) Tannakian categories - that is, when it is compatible with the natural fibre functor of
\Rep_{F}(K)
.
In the language of
F
-algebraic actions employed here, this feature comes down to the following notion:

Definition A.41
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a neutral Tannakian category over
F
;
fix a fibre functor
\omega\colon\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathsf{vect}_{F}
, and let
K
denote the associated Tannaka dual group.
Let
Q
be a pro-algebraic group over
F
acting monoidally on
\mathcal{T}
(as in Definition
A.30
).
By a
concretization

\gamma
for the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{T}
with respect to
\omega
we mean the datum of

•

for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\Rep_{F}(K)_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathsf{mod}_{\Lambda}

\gamma_{q}\colon\omega_{\Lambda}\circ(q\cdot-)\xrightarrow{\sim}\omega_{\Lambda}

compatibly with composition in
Q
and functorially with respect to
F
-algebra homomorphism, in the following sense:
for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q(\Lambda)
, the diagram of functors
\Rep_{F}(K)_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathsf{mod}_{\Lambda}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\Lambda}\circ(q_{1}\cdot(q_{2}\cdot-))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\Lambda}\circ(q_{2}\cdot-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\Lambda}\circ(q_{1}q_{2}\cdot-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{q_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{q_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{q_{1}q_{2}}}

commutes and, for every
F
-algebra homomorphism
\phi\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, the diagram of functors
\Rep_{F}(K)_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathsf{mod}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\circ((q\cdot-)\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\omega_{\Lambda}\circ(q\cdot-))\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\circ(\phi(q)\cdot(-\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\circ(-\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{q}\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\phi(q)}}

commutes.

Remark A.42
.

Suppose that we are given two
F
-algebraic actions of
Q
on
\mathcal{T}
as well as an isomorphism
\gamma
between them (as in Definition
A.26
(1));
in addition, suppose that we are given a concretization
\gamma_{A}
for the first action.
Then we obtain a concretization
\gamma_{B}
for the second action by defining
\gamma_{B,\Lambda,q}
as the composite

\omega_{\Lambda}\circ(q\cdot_{B}-)\xrightarrow{\omega_{\Lambda}(\gamma_{q}^{-1})}\omega_{\Lambda}\circ(q\cdot_{A}-)\xrightarrow{\gamma_{A,\Lambda,q}}\omega_{\Lambda}

for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
:
indeed, the compatibility of
\gamma_{B}
with composition in
Q
and
F
-algebra homomorphisms follows from the corresponding compatibility conditions for
\gamma_{A}
and for
\gamma
.

Here is the promised criterion:

Lemma A.43
.

Keep the notation and assumptions of Construction
A.37
.
The
Q
-action on
\mathcal{T}
admits a concretization if and only if, up to isomorphism of actions (in the sense of Definition
A.26
(1)), it is induced by an
F
-algebraic right action of
Q
on
K
(in the sense of Example
A.33
).

Proof.

Suppose that the
Q
-action on
\mathcal{T}
is induced by an
F
-algebraic right action of
Q
on
K
.
Then one can trivially define a concretization
\gamma
by taking
\gamma_{q}
to be the identity natural transformation for every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
and every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
:
this is legitimate in view of the explicit formula for the
Q
-action on
\Rep_{F}(K)
given in Example
A.33
.

Conversely, suppose that the action of
Q
on
\mathcal{T}
admits a concretization
\gamma
.
By
[
DM82
, Prop. 2.8]
, for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, the group
K(\Lambda)
is naturally identified with the group of tensor-automorphisms of the fibre functor
\omega_{\Lambda}\colon\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathsf{mod}_{\Lambda}
, functorially with respect to
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
.
For every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
, we define a map

a_{\Lambda}\colon K(\Lambda)\times Q(\Lambda)\rightarrow K(\Lambda),\quad(k,q)\mapsto a_{\Lambda,q}(k)

(62)

by the formula

a_{\Lambda,q}(k):=\gamma_{q}\circ(k(q\cdot-))\circ\gamma_{q}^{-1},

where
k(q\cdot-)
denotes the tensor-automorphism of the composite functor
\omega\circ(q\cdot-)
induced by
k
.
We claim that the maps (
62
) define an
F
-algebraic right action
a\colon K\times Q\rightarrow K
as in Example
A.33
.
To this end, we need to check that each map (
62
) defines an action of the abstract group
Q(\Lambda)
on
K(\Lambda)
and that they are functorial with respect to
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
.
But these two facts follow immediately from the two coherence conditions in Definition
A.41
.

Now we have two distinct
Q
-actions on
\mathcal{T}
:
the original one, and the one induced by the
Q
-action of
K
via Example
A.33
;
for every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, let us write
q\cdot_{A}-
and
q\cdot_{B}-
for the corresponding functors
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
in these two actions, respectively.
For every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{CAlg}_{F}
and every
q\in Q(\Lambda)
, consider the isomorphism of functors
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\to\mathsf{mod}_{\Lambda}

\gamma_{\Lambda,q}\colon\omega_{\Lambda}\circ(q\cdot_{A}-)\xrightarrow{\sim}\omega_{\Lambda}=\omega_{\Lambda}\circ(q\cdot_{B}-),

where the last equality comes from the explicit description of the second
Q
-action on
\mathcal{T}
provided in Example
A.33
.
We claim that, for every
V\in\mathcal{T}
, the isomorphism in
\mod_{\Lambda}

\gamma_{\Lambda,q,V}\colon\omega_{\Lambda}(q\cdot_{A}(V\otimes\Lambda))\xrightarrow{\sim}\omega_{\Lambda}(q\cdot_{B}(V\otimes\Lambda))

comes from an isomorphism in
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}

q\cdot_{A}(V\otimes\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\sim}q\cdot_{B}(V\otimes\Lambda).

(63)

To see this, it is convenient to identify
\mathcal{T}
with
\Rep_{F}(K)
via the fibre functor
\omega\colon\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathsf{vect}_{F}
, which allows us to write
V=(W,\rho)
as in Notation
A.32
;
for sake of simplicity, let us also write
q\cdot_{A}(V\otimes\Lambda)=(W^{\prime}\otimes\Lambda,\rho^{\prime}_{\Lambda})
for some
W^{\prime}\in\mathsf{vect}_{F}
.
Then our claim amounts to the fact that, for every
F
-algebra homomorphism
\phi\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}
and every
k\in K(\Lambda^{\prime})
, the diagram in
\mathsf{mod}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime}\otimes\Lambda^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\otimes\Lambda^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime}\otimes\Lambda^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\otimes\Lambda^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{\prime}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Lambda^{\prime},\phi(q),V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{\phi(q)}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Lambda^{\prime},\phi(q),V}}

commutes, which follows from the very definition of the homomorphism
\rho_{\Lambda}^{q}\colon K_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\underline{\textup{GL}}_{\Lambda}(W\otimes\Lambda)
.
By construction, the isomorphisms (
63
) are coherent with composition in
Q
and with
F
-algebra homomorphisms, hence they define the sought-after isomorphism of actions.
∎

Remark A.44
.

A general
F
-algebraic action of
Q
on
\mathcal{T}
needs not admit a concretization, not even when
F
is algebraically closed.
In fact, if
F
is algebraically closed, then for every
q\in Q(F)
the two fibre functors
\omega\circ(q\cdot-)
and
\omega
are isomorphic, but not canonically so in general.
In particular, it is not clear that one can choose the various isomorphisms defining a concretization compatibly with composition in
Q(F)
, as required in Definition
A.41
.
